Group completions and the homotopical monadicity theorem
Hana Jia Kong, J. Peter May, Foling Zou

Abstract
. We abstract and generalize homotopical monadicity statements, placing in a single conceptual framework a range of old and recent recognition and characterization principles in iterated loop space theory in classical, equivariant, and multiplicative frameworks. Some of the examples are new and some are old, but all are illuminated by the coherent framework, which we feel certain will encompass examples not yet thought of. The work is currently divided into three independently readable papers. This first paper is itself divided into three parts. In the first, we give the general abstract theory and treat the classical examples with structured spaces or G G -spaces as input. In the second, we develop a general context of composite adjunctions that feeds into the first. It specializes, quite differently, to give infinite loop space machines that take either orbital presheaves or categories of operators as input. In the brief third part, we show how the multiplicative theory fits directly into the frameworks of the first and second parts. The second paper will focus on new constructions and applications when the starting category is that of orbital presheaves. The third will feed in new multiplicative constructions of the second author. Both papers fit into the general context established here, but the new constructions are of considerable independent interest.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 55P42, 55P43, 55P91;

Secondary 18A25, 18E30, 55P48, 55U35

Introduction

We give a conceptual reinterpretation of work that started with the introduction of operads in what the senior author called “The geometry of iterated loop spaces”
[
May72
]
. The word “geometry” was meant to convey that the structures involved really were geometric at heart. They implicitly reduced the homotopical study of iterated loop spaces to the study of configuration spaces. The same idea works equivariantly
[
GM17
,
MMO
]
and multiplicatively
[
May82
,
May09b
]
, but the equivariant multiplicative theory here is not in the earlier literature.
1
1

1

Equivariant multiplicative theory based on the Segal machine is given in
[
GMMO19
,
GMMO23
]
, but it is not general enough for the applications we have in mind and is designed to shoot through the space level, going as directly as possible from categorical input to spectrum level output.

0.1.
Introduction to Part 1, the classical theory

We start with a general conceptual reinterpretation of material in
[
May09b
]
. That focused on direct comparison with the proof of the Beck monadicity theorem. Here we axiomatize a general homotopical variant of that classical categorical context. The axiomatization simplifies things by focusing attention on exactly what is needed. It is hoped and expected that it will serve as a guide to future applications. We emphasize that the theory here primarily focuses throughout on monads, not necessarily those coming from operads.

In brief, adjoint pairs of functors
(\Sigma,\Omega)
are ubiquitous in mathematics, and so are monads. The right adjoint

\Omega\colon\scr{S}\longrightarrow\scr{T}
always carries extra structure that is encoded by its taking values in the category
\Gamma[\scr{T}]
of algebras over the adjunction monad

\Gamma=\Omega\Sigma
. The Beck monadicity theorem explains categorically when
the resulting functor
\Omega_{\Gamma}\colon\scr{S}\longrightarrow\Gamma[\scr{T}]
is an equivalence of categories. We are concerned with the deeper situation when
\Omega_{\Gamma}
is
not
an equivalence. In homotopical contexts,
\Omega
very often takes values in the category
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras for some other monad
\mathbb{C}
. Moreover, this structure often encodes calculational information that is invisible in the original adjunction and its monad.

Writing
\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\scr{S}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
for the resulting more structured version of
\Omega
, we find that
\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}
has a left adjoint
\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]\longrightarrow\scr{S}
which we think of as a
monadically coequalized
variant of
\Sigma
. When applied to a free
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
\mathbb{C}X
, the coequalizer simplifies to give an isomorphism
\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}X\cong\Sigma X
, and the unit of the adjunction restricts on free
\mathbb{C}
-algebras to give a map of monads from
\mathbb{C}
to the adjunction monad
\Gamma
; see
Theorem 1.15
and
Remark 1.28
. Approximating general
\mathbb{C}
-algebras
Y
by well-behaved approximations
\overline{Y}
, one finds a recognition principle of the following general form.

Theorem 0.1
.

There is a functor
\mathrm{Bar}\colon\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
, written
Y\mapsto\overline{Y}
, and a natural equivalence
\zeta\colon\overline{Y}\longrightarrow Y
such that the unit
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\overline{Y}\longrightarrow\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{Y}
is a group completion and is therefore an equivalence if
Y
is grouplike.

In turn, this leads to a characterization principle of the following general form. It is a homotopical analog of the categorical Beck monadicity theorem.

Theorem 0.2
.

(\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}},\Omega_{\mathbb{C}})
induces an adjoint equivalence from the homotopy category of grouplike
\mathbb{C}
-algebras in
\scr{T}
to the category of
\Omega
-connective objects of
\scr{S}
.

The terms
group completion
,
grouplike
, and
\Omega
-connective
have axiomatized meanings and can specialize to have different meanings in different contexts. In particular, we shall have two very different ideas of grouplike objects and group completions. The first is characterized axiomatically (Definitions
2.5
and
2.7
) but the examples are concrete and homological or homotopical (for example Definitions
4.1
and
4.7
); that idea is the one that leads both to our proofs and to myriads of explicit calculations. The other is formal and conceptual (
Definition 3.22
) and is characterized by a universal property on passage to homotopy categories (
Theorem 3.29
), but it is not at all geared towards calculations. We shall find an equivalence between them under appropriate assumptions (
Theorem 3.24
).

We emphasize that despite its obvious specialization in topology,
(\Sigma,\Omega)
can be any suitable adjoint pair in our general axiomatic context. Here
\overline{Y}
might come as a cofibrant approximation of
Y
, but we focus on the highly structured choice given by the monadic bar construction
\overline{Y}=B(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C},Y)
; it is usually itself an example of a cofibrant approximation (see
Section 3.5
), but that is not our emphasis.

For convenience in discussing applications and with potential new applications in mind, we begin with standing “Assumptions” that together will lead to results of the form given in Theorems
0.1
and
0.2
. In
Section 1
we establish our categorical context, explaining a general adjunction
(\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}},\Omega_{\mathbb{C}})
that will have many specializations. While
\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}

was previously defined in passing, its centrality to our theory is a new perspective.

In
Section 1.4
, we review the categorical Beck monadicity theorem for the sake of comparison and motivation, emphasizing the role of the specialization of
(\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}},\Omega_{\mathbb{C}})
to
(\Sigma_{\Gamma},\Omega_{\Gamma})
. That perspective seems to be only implicit in the categorical literature about this 1960’s result.

In
Section 2
, we explain our homotopical assumptions, giving remarks as we go about how they are verified in special cases.
The most substantial is
Section 2.3
, the approximation theorem, which requires an always present natural map of monads
\mathbf{C}X\longrightarrow\Gamma X
to give a group completion for all
X
. In
Section 3
, we explain how these assumptions fit together to prove various versions of Theorems
0.1
and
0.2
.

The original topological examples are developed in
Section 4
. They use monads associated to operads of topological spaces, with
\scr{T}
being the category of based spaces and
\scr{S}
being either a category of based spaces or a category of spectra, and similarly equivariantly.

Nonequivariantly, we specialize to
n
-fold loop spaces in
Section 4.1
, summarizing our specializations of Theorems
0.1
and
0.2
in
Theorem 4.12
. We find that every
(n-1)
-connected or, synonymously, “
n
-connective”, space is equivalent to the coequalized
n
-fold suspension of an
E_{n}
-space. The emphasis is on a new perspective on the group completion of
E_{n}
spaces to
n
-fold loop spaces. When
n\geq 2
, two different classical notions (Definitions
4.1
,
4.7
) of group completion are shown to be equivalent to each other and to the formal conceptual notion of
Definition 3.22
.

In the case
n=1
only one of the classical notions, that of
Definition 4.7
, applies. In this case, the approximation theorem is given a conceptual proof in
Section 4.2
, which is largely a new perspective on material in
[
Fie84
]
. Here the focus shifts to a comparison of two monads in
\scr{T}
with two analogous monads in the category
\scr{V}
that is the natural home of Moore loop spaces. A first example,
(4.27)
, of the composite adjunction context of Part 2 plays a central role.

Equivariantly, Sections
4.3
and
4.4
give the analogous examples of
V
-fold loop
G
-spaces for a representation
V
of
G
and of
1
-fold loop
G
-spaces. Here the two classical notions of group completion are Definitions
4.36
and
4.37
and our specializations of Theorems
0.1
and
0.2
are given in
Theorem 4.44
.

While
G
is finite in
Section 4.3
, it can be any topological group in
Section 4.4
. As far as we know, the material of
Section 4.4
is new, but we have written
Section 4.2
in such a way that its equivariant generalization is immediate. The main results here, Theorems
4.42
and
4.48
, are variants of the approximation theorem that follow by passage to fixed points from their nonequivariant versions, Theorems
4.11
and
4.34
.

Passing to
n=\infty
, we give foundations in
Section 4.5
and rework the equivariant operadic infinite loop space machine in
Section 4.6
. This shows how to construct
G
-spectra from
E_{\infty}

G
-spaces when
G
is finite. To avoid duplicative exposition, the nonequivariant case is viewed as the case
G=\{e\}
of the equivariant case. The homological definition of group completion of
E_{\infty}

G
-spaces is the same as for
E_{V}

G
-spaces when
\mathbb{R}^{2}\subset V
(
Definition 4.36
). It is again equivalent to the conceptual definition of
Definition 3.22
. Every
E_{\infty}
-
G
-space group completes to the
0
th
G
-space of a connective
G
-spectrum, and every connective
G
-spectrum is equivalent to the coequalized suspension
G
-spectrum of a grouplike
E_{\infty}
-
G
-space,
Theorem 4.44
.

Here
G
-spectra mean genuine
G
-spectra, incorporating representations. There is also a very partial generalization from finite to compact Lie groups (
Remark 4.59
), and there is a parallel theory for classical (or naive)
G
-spectra, which ignore representations, and that works for any topological group
G
(
Remark 4.60
).

We must say something about our spectral foundations of
Section 4.5
. There are many good concrete point-set level categories of spectra and of
G
-spectra. Only one is known to fit into the achingly elementary formal context of
Section 1.1
that is our starting point. No category of spectra can both fit into that context and be symmetric monoidal with the sphere spectrum as unit, but all good categories give equivalent homotopy categories, and in fact equivalent
\infty
-categories.

However, in this paper we are being old-fashioned and taking no interest in
\infty
-categories, although we are implicitly proving many equivalences of such, as we plan to show elsewhere. We care about when precise point-set level categories and concepts are equivalent. Many decades of concrete applications and calculations show the value of this perspective. One must be eclectic.

0.2.
Introduction to Part 2, composite adjunctions

In Part 2, we build a general context that will specialize to apply to infinite loop space machines that are defined in two quite different equivariant contexts. The first concerns algebras over monads in categories related to categories of operators, such algebras being suitable covariant functors. The second concerns algebras over monads in categories of orbital presheaves, such algebras being suitable contravariant functors. At first sight, this seems farfetched since, as we shall see, there are many quite significant differences between these two contexts. Nevertheless, both are special cases of a single general context, which itself is a special case of our original context. We explain the general context in
Section 5
and its specializations to categories of operators in
Section 6
and to orbital presheaves in
Section 7
.
Paradoxically, the agreement of context allows us both to highlight precisely how the differences play out and to discover surprising parallels and connections.

The categories of operators specialization is largely classical, starting from
[
MT78
]
. Aside from the conceptual context, the main new feature is the introduction of a new kind of operad, called a
G
-operad. Instead of having
G
-spaces
\scr{C}(n)
for non-negative integers
n
, it has
G
-spaces
\scr{C}(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})
for finite
G
-sets
\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}
determined by homomorphisms
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow\Sigma_{n}
. These operads and their associated monads fill a longstanding conceptual gap in the relationship between equivariant operads and monads on the one hand and the two kinds of equivariant categories of operators that appear prominently in
[
MMO
,
GMMO23
]
and elsewhere.

The operadic presheaf context can be viewed as a conceptual reinterpretation of an early paper of Costenoble and Waner
[
CW91
]
. The general theory is described here, but its application requires non-obvious examples of monads in the category of orbital presheaves. They are constructed from
G
-operads, but not in an obvious way. The construction is worked out in the sequel
[
KMZ24
]
, where examples may be found.

0.3.
Introduction to Part 3, the multiplicative theory

We apply the context of
Section 1.1
multiplicatively in
Section 8
. This entails working with monad pairs as introduced by Beck
[
Bec69
]
. We recall their basic theory in
Section 8.1
. We have two different ways to construct monad pairs
(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{J}_{0})
from suitable operadic pairs. We describe the classical way in
Section 8.2
. There is a new construction based on “multiplicative operads” that will be given in
[
May24
]
. These two constructions start from the same additive monad
\mathbb{C}
but give different constructions of the multiplicative monad
\mathbb{J}_{0}
. We emphasize that the monadic theory described here applies equally well to both. The theory described so far is general, and we briefly specialize to operad pairs in general and in
G
-spaces in Sections
8.2
and
8.3
.

We review the 1980’s
[
LMS86
]
definition of
E_{\infty}
ring
G
-spectra in
Section 8.4
. These are defined in the present context of spectra given in
Section 4.5
, but they are equivalent to any modern reincarnation of the notion. Their 1990’s equivalents
[
EKMM97
]
were studied in
[
EM97
,
May96
,
May98
]
. For example,
[
May98
]
shows how to construct Brown-Peterson
G
-spectra for any compact Lie group
G
. However, these older contexts are not very well known in the modern world, so review is necessary. A variant new view of
E_{\infty}
ring spectra will be given in
[
May24
]
.

Taking
\scr{T}
and
\scr{S}
in our general theory to be (based)
\mathbb{J}_{0}
-
G
-spaces and
\mathbb{J}_{0}
-
G
-spectra, we establish a multiplicative special case of

Section 1.1
in
Section 8.5
. We then check the remaining assumptions and show how to construct
E_{\infty}
-ring
G
-spectra from
E_{\infty}
ring
G
-spaces in
Section 8.6
. Although not in the present context, the theory in this section has been known since the 1990’s but it has never been written up before.
2
2

2

Due to the unfashionability of equivariant stable homotopy theory in the following decades.

We apply the composite adjunction context of
Section 5.1
multiplicatively in
Section 9
. We establish a general context involving maps of monad pairs in different categories in
Section 9.1
. While elementary, such a notion does not seem to appear in the literature. We indicate how the theory specializes to categories of operators in
Section 9.2
, where examples include equivariant generalizations of those summarized in
[
May09a
]
. We indicate how the theory specializes to orbital presheaves in
Section 9.3
, where interesting applications are work in progress.
In
Section 9.4
, we briefly indicate, without details, how the whole theory, additive and multiplicative, can be generalized by composing the orbital presheaf and categories of operators adjunctions.

0.4.
Remarks on recent related papers

The theory here starts from conceptual and equivariant elaboration of the exposition of
[
May09b
, Sections 8 and 9]
. The specialization to
G
-spaces is largely a contextualization and summary exposition of parts of
[
GM17
,
MMO
]
;
[
GM17
]
focuses on applications that start from categorical input (which we do not repeat here), and
[
MMO
]
focuses on development of the equivariant Segal machine and its comparison with the equivariant operadic machine. Neither of those papers deals at all with orbital presheaves or the multiplicative theory in our Parts 2 and 3 and in the sequels
[
KMZ24
,
May24
]
. The paper
[
GMMO23
]
develops the equivariant and multiplicative Segal machine, with no consideration of operadic or space level structure. It is geared towards application in
[
GM11
]
to the description of the category of genuine
G
-spectra as a category of presheaves of nonequivariant spectra.

Part I
The classical theory

1.
The categorical context

1.1.
Section 1.1
: an adjunction
(\Sigma,\Omega)
and a related monad
\mathbb{C}

Categorical monadicity starts with an adjunction
(\Sigma,\Omega)
and its associated monad
\Gamma=\Omega\Sigma
. We are interested in situations where
categorical monadicity fails, and we modify that starting point accordingly. Categorical monadicity does not assume cocompleteness of the relevant categories, but we shall find it convenient to do so.

Assumption A.
We assume given an adjoint pair of functors
(\Sigma,\Omega)
,
\Sigma\colon\scr{T}\longrightarrow\scr{S}
and
\Omega\colon\scr{S}\longrightarrow\scr{T}
, together with a monad
\mathbb{C}
in
\scr{T}
and a natural action of
\mathbb{C}
on
\Omega Y
for objects
Y\in\scr{S}
. We assume further that
\scr{T}
and
\scr{S}
are cocomplete.

Notations 1.1
.

We write
\vartheta\colon\mathbb{C}\Omega Y\longrightarrow\Omega Y
for the given natural action.
We let
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
denote the category of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras in
\scr{T}
, and we let
\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\scr{S}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
denote the functor
\Omega
, but viewed as taking values in
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
. Thus we write
\Omega Y
when forgetting that it is a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra and
\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Y
when remembering the action of
\mathbb{C}
.

Remark 1.2
.

Section 1.1
sometimes needs minor but essential technical modification, as for example in based spaces
\scr{U}_{\ast}
, which is cocomplete, versus nondegenerately based spaces

\scr{T}
, which is not. Formally, we then have a monad
\mathbb{C}
on
\scr{U}_{\ast}
which restricts to a monad on
\scr{T}
. We then view
\scr{T}
as a preferred subcategory of good objects of
\scr{U}_{\ast}
, to which we restrict whenever possible.

The idea is that
\scr{U}_{*}
is essential for formal generality, but
\scr{T}
is essential for homotopical precision. Such cofibration issues are handled with care in
[
MMO
]
and will only be mentioned in passing here. The difference is of no practical importance since “whiskering” and “bearding” constructions of
[
MT78
, Proposition 1.6]
and
[
MMO
, Proposition 2.12 and Section 11]
show that general objects of interest can always be replaced by equivalent good objects.

Except where otherwise specified, we always assume that we are in the context given in
Section 1.1
, even if we neglect to say so. We shall define a “monadically coequalized” variant
\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}
of
\Sigma
and prove the following basic result in
Section 1.3
.
3
3

3

\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}
was first defined in
[
May09b
, (8.4)]
, but its importance was not understood then.

Proposition 1.3
.

The functor
\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]\longrightarrow\scr{S}
is left adjoint to
\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}
.

We fix notations for the units and counits of our adjunctions.

Notations 1.4
.

Let
\eta\colon\operatorname{Id}\longrightarrow\Omega\Sigma
and
\varepsilon\colon\Sigma\Omega\longrightarrow\operatorname{Id}
be the unit and counit of the adjunction
(\Sigma,\Omega)
.
Let
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\operatorname{Id}\longrightarrow\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Id}
be the unit and counit of
(\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}},\Omega_{\mathbb{C}})
.

Recall that for any adjunction
(\Sigma,\Omega)
from any category
\scr{T}
to another category
\scr{S}
, with unit
\eta
and counit
\varepsilon
, the isomorphism

(1.5)

\scr{S}(\Sigma X,Y)\cong\scr{T}(X,\Omega Y)

is given by the composite

(1.6)

\textstyle{\scr{S}(\Sigma X,Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\scr{T}(\Omega\Sigma X,\Omega Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{*}}
\textstyle{\scr{T}(X,\Omega Y)}

with inverse the composite

(1.7)

\textstyle{\scr{T}(X,\Omega Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma}
\textstyle{\scr{S}(\Sigma X,\Sigma\Omega Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{*}}
\textstyle{\scr{S}(\Sigma X,Y).}

The composite
\Gamma=\Omega\Sigma
is a monad with product
\mu=\Omega\varepsilon\colon\Gamma\Gamma\longrightarrow\Gamma
and unit
\eta\colon\operatorname{Id}\longrightarrow\Gamma
. For
Z\in\scr{S}
,
\Omega Z
is a
\Gamma
-algebra with action
\Omega\varepsilon\colon\Gamma Z\longrightarrow\Omega Z
.

The following special case of
(1.6)
serves as a guide to be kept in mind.

(1.8)

\textstyle{\scr{S}(\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}X,Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\scr{T}(\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}X,\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}}
\textstyle{\scr{T}(X,\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Y)}

We can take
\mathbb{C}=\Gamma
in
Section 1.1
. In
Section 1.4
, we will interpret the Beck monadicity theorem as characterizing when
\eta_{\Gamma}
is an isomorphism and therefore when
\eta_{\Gamma}^{*}
and
\Omega_{\Gamma}
in
(1.8)
are isomorphisms. See
Corollary 1.45
and
Remark 1.46
.

Aside from the notation, the following definition is standard.

Definition 1.9
.

The (free, forgetful) adjunction
(\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{C}},\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{C}})
of a monad
\mathbb{C}
is given by
\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{C}}X=(\mathbb{C}X,\mu)
and
\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{C}}(Y,\theta)=Y
. Note that the adjunction monad
\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{C}}
coincides with the monad
\mathbb{C}
.

Clearly
\Omega=\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\circ\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}
. The following untwisting isomorphism is a formal consequence. It is a formal precursor of the Barratt–Priddy–Quillen theorem (compare
Theorem 3.14
). Comparison of
(1.19)
and
(1.21)
below gives a more concrete proof.

Proposition 1.10
.

For
X
in
\scr{T}
,
\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{C}X)
is naturally isomorphic to
\Sigma X
.

These isomorphisms of functors imply the following result.

Proposition 1.11
.

The adjunction
(\Sigma,\Omega)
can be identified with the composite
(\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\circ\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{C}},\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\circ\Omega_{\mathbb{C}})
of the adjunctions

(\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}},\Omega_{\mathbb{C}})
and
(\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{C}},\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{C}})
.

It is illuminating to display this composite adjunction in a diagram. We will see many specializations.

(1.12)

\textstyle{\scr{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\scr{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}

1.2.
The map of monads
\alpha\colon\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\Gamma=\Omega\Sigma
and its adjoint
\beta

Assuming
Section 1.1
, we here relate the monads
\mathbb{C}
and
\Gamma=\Omega\Sigma
.

Definition 1.13
.

For
X\in\scr{T}
, define
\alpha\colon\mathbb{C}X\longrightarrow\Omega\Sigma X
to be the composite

\textstyle{\alpha\colon\mathbb{C}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{C}\eta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\Omega\Sigma X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta}
\textstyle{\Omega\Sigma X,}

where
\vartheta
is the action assumed in
Section 1.1
.
Define
\beta\colon\Sigma\mathbb{C}X\longrightarrow\Sigma X
to be the adjoint of
\alpha
, namely the composite

\textstyle{\beta\colon\Sigma\mathbb{C}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\alpha}
\textstyle{\Sigma\Omega\Sigma X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{\Sigma X.}

The following categorical observations about these maps were first noticed in special cases

[
May72
, Theorem 5.2 and Examples 9.5]
, but the same formal diagram chasing proofs work in general, as in
[
May09b
]
.
The starting point is that
\alpha\colon\mathbb{C}X\longrightarrow\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma X
is a map of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras, which is immediate from the
definitions.

Lemma 1.14
.

The map
\alpha\colon\mathbb{C}X\longrightarrow\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma X
is the map of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras given by the universal property
of the free functor
\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{C}}
, applied to the map
\eta\colon X\longrightarrow\Omega\Sigma X
.

Theorem 1.15
.

The following results hold in the context of
Section 1.1
.

(i)

\alpha\colon\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\Gamma=\Omega\Sigma
is a map of monads in
\scr{T}
.

(ii)

\alpha\colon\mathbb{C}X\longrightarrow\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma X
is the unique map of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras whose composite
with the unit
\eta\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
of
\mathbb{C}
is the unit
\eta\colon X\longrightarrow\Omega\Sigma X
of the adjunction.

(iii)

The natural action
\vartheta\colon\mathbb{C}\Omega\longrightarrow\Omega
is the composite

\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\Omega\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\Gamma\Omega\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega\varepsilon}
\textstyle{\Omega,}

that is, the pullback along
\alpha
of the action
\Omega\varepsilon\colon\Gamma\Omega Y\longrightarrow\Omega Y
of
\Gamma
.

(iv)

(\Sigma,\beta)
is a
\mathbb{C}
-functor with action the composite

\textstyle{\Sigma\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\Sigma\Gamma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{\Sigma,}

that is, the pullback along
\Sigma\alpha
of the action
\varepsilon\colon\Sigma\Gamma\longrightarrow\Sigma
of
\Gamma
.

Proof.

We will recall the definitions of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras and
\mathbb{C}
-functors in
Section 1.3
.
To prove (
i
), we see that
\alpha
is compatible
with the units,
\alpha\circ\eta=\eta
, by the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

We see that
\alpha
is compatible with the product,
\alpha\circ\mu=\mu\circ\alpha\circ\mathbb{C}\alpha
, by the
following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{C}\eta}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{C}\Gamma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{C}\vartheta}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\Gamma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{C}\eta}
\scriptstyle{=}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\Gamma\Gamma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{C}\Omega\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta}
\textstyle{\Gamma\Gamma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega\epsilon=\mu}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\Gamma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{C}\eta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\Gamma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta}
\textstyle{\Gamma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{\Gamma}

Here the left rectangle and right pentagon are naturality diagrams, the small triangle commutes by a triangle identity, the large triangle commutes tautologically, and the remaining rectangle commutes since
\vartheta
is an action of
\mathbb{C}
. For (ii), the unit diagram and the left half of the product diagram above show that
\alpha
is a map of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras, and uniqueness holds since
\mathbb{C}
is the free
\mathbb{C}
-algebra functor.

For (iii), a triangle identity and naturality give that

\vartheta=\vartheta\circ\mathbb{C}\Omega\epsilon\circ\mathbb{C}\eta=\Omega\varepsilon\circ\vartheta\circ\mathbb{C}\eta=\Omega\varepsilon\circ\alpha.

Similarly, for (iv), it is clear that
\Sigma
is a
\Gamma
-functor via
\varepsilon\colon\Sigma\Gamma\longrightarrow\Sigma
, and then it is immediate from the definition of
\beta
that it is a
\mathbb{C}
-functor by pullback along
\mathbb{C}\alpha
.
∎

Taken together, parts (i) and (ii) have the following converse, which encodes a reformulation of
Section 1.1
.

Theorem 1.16
.

Let
(\Sigma,\Omega)
be an adjunction,
\Sigma\colon\scr{T}\longrightarrow\scr{S}
and
\Omega\colon\scr{S}\longrightarrow\scr{T}
, with associated monad

\Gamma=\Omega\Sigma
in
\scr{T}
. Let

\alpha\colon\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\Gamma
be a map of monads in
\scr{T}
.

(i)

\mathbb{C}
acts naturally on objects
\Omega Y
via the pullback action
\vartheta
:

\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\Omega\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\Gamma\Omega\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega\varepsilon}
\textstyle{\Omega.}

(ii)

The given map of monads
\alpha
is the composite

\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{C}\eta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\Gamma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta}
\textstyle{\Gamma.}

Proof.

Part (i) is clear. Part (ii) holds by the following commutative diagram.

\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\Gamma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta}
\textstyle{\Gamma\Gamma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega\varepsilon}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{C}\eta}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{C}\eta}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{\mathbf{C}\mathbf{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{C}\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\Gamma}

The upper left triangle and the rectangle commute since
\alpha
is a map of monads;
\vartheta
is defined by the upper right triangle and it follows that the lower right triangle commutes. The lower left triangle commutes since
\mathbb{C}
is a monad.
∎

1.3.
Monadic coequalizers and the adjunction
(\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}},\Omega_{\mathbb{C}})

Recall that a coequalizer of maps
f,g\colon A\longrightarrow B
in
\scr{T}
is a map
q\colon B\longrightarrow C
in
\scr{T}
such that
qf=qg
and
q
is universal with this property. A split coequalizer is given by a diagram

(1.17)

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{i}

such that

qf=qg,\,\,qi=\operatorname{id},\,\,fj=iq,\,\,\text{and}\,\,gj=\operatorname{id}.

It is a coequalizer since if
h\colon B\longrightarrow D
is such that
hf=hg
, then
(hi)q=h
and
hi
is the unique map
C\longrightarrow D
with this property.
If we apply any functor
\scr{T}\longrightarrow\scr{S}
to a split coequalizer we get another split coequalizer.

Let
(\mathbb{C},\mu,\eta)
, abbreviated
\mathbb{C}
, be our monad in
\scr{T}
. This means that
\mathbb{C}\colon\scr{T}\longrightarrow\scr{T}
is a monoid in the functor category of
\scr{T}
with product
\mu
and unit
\eta
.

Recall that a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
(Y,\theta)
, abbreviated
Y
, is an object of
\scr{T}
with an action
\theta\colon\mathbb{C}Y\longrightarrow Y
such that
\theta\circ\mathbb{C}\theta=\theta\circ\mu
and
\theta\circ\eta=\operatorname{id}
. The following diagram then exhibits
Y
as a split coequalizer.

(1.18)

\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{C}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{C}\theta}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{\eta}

Here
\mathbb{C}\theta\circ\eta=\eta\circ\theta
by the naturality of
\eta
applied to the morphism
\theta
.
4
4

4

The first
\eta
is
\eta
on
\mathbb{C}Y
; category theorists might usually write it as
\eta\circ\mathbb{C}
.

Recall too that a
\mathbb{C}
-functor
(\Sigma,\beta)
, abbreviated
\Sigma
, is a functor
\Sigma\colon\scr{T}\longrightarrow\scr{Z}
for some category
\scr{Z}
with an action
\beta\colon\Sigma\circ\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\Sigma
such that
\beta\circ\Sigma\mu=\beta\circ\beta
and
\beta\circ\Sigma\eta=\operatorname{id}

[
May72
, Definition 9.4]
. We will be focusing on the case
\scr{Z}=\scr{S}
. In analogy with
(1.18)
, the following diagram exhibits the functor
\Sigma
as naturally given by a split coequalizer for each object
X\in\scr{T}
.

(1.19)

\textstyle{\Sigma\mathbb{C}\mathbb{C}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\mu}
\textstyle{\Sigma\mathbb{C}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\mathbb{C}\eta}
\textstyle{\Sigma X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\eta}

Here
\beta\circ\Sigma\mathbb{C}\eta=\Sigma\eta\circ\beta
by the naturality of
\beta
applied to the morphism
\eta
.

As seen in
Section 1.2
, the adjunction
(\Sigma,\Omega)
in
Section 1.1

provides an action
\beta
of
\mathbb{C}
on
\Sigma
. The following categorical starting point
makes sense whenever
\Sigma
is a
\mathbb{C}
-functor.

Definition 1.20
.

Let
\Sigma\colon\scr{T}\longrightarrow\scr{S}
be a
\mathbb{C}
-functor. Define the
\mathbb{C}
-coequalized functor

\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]\longrightarrow\scr{S}
to be the tensor product
\Sigma\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}(-)
. Explicitly, on a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
Y
, it is given by the coequalizer in
\scr{S}
displayed in the diagram

(1.21)

\textstyle{\Sigma{\mathbb{C}}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\theta}
\textstyle{\Sigma Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}Y.}

Remark 1.22
.

Specializing
Y
in
(1.21)
to
Y=\mathbb{C}X
, we see that the same pairs of arrows are being coequalized as in
(1.19)
. Therefore the two coequalizers can be identified, reproving
Proposition 1.10
, and this identifies
q\colon\Sigma\mathbb{C}X\longrightarrow\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}X
with
\beta\colon\Sigma\mathbb{C}X\longrightarrow\Sigma X
. Moreover, since
\beta\circ\Sigma\mu=\beta\circ\beta
,

\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\mu
agrees with
\beta
under the isomorphism
\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{C}X)\cong\Sigma\mathbb{C}X
.

Returning to
Section 1.1
, we specialize
\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}
to that context.

Proof of
Proposition 1.3
.

Consider the following diagram, in which
Y
is in
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
and
Z
is in
\scr{S}
.

(1.23)

\textstyle{\scr{S}(\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}Y,Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}](Y,\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\subset}
\textstyle{\scr{S}(\Sigma Y,Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\scr{T}(Y,\Omega Z).}

We claim that a map
f\colon Y\longrightarrow\Omega Z
in
\scr{T}
is a map of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras if and only if its adjoint
\tilde{f}\colon\Sigma Y\longrightarrow Z
factors uniquely through
q\colon\Sigma Y\longrightarrow\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}Y
. That will give the top isomorphism. Thus consider the diagram

(1.24)

\textstyle{\mathbb{C}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{C}f}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z.}

It commutes, so that
f
is a map of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras, if and only if its adjoint
commutes. The top row of the following diagram in
\scr{T}
is the adjoint

\widetilde{\vartheta\circ\mathbb{C}f}
of
\vartheta\circ\mathbb{C}f
; its left column is
\beta
, the adjoint of
\alpha
.

(1.25)

\textstyle{\Sigma\mathbb{C}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\mathbb{C}f}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\mathbb{C}\eta}
\textstyle{\Sigma\mathbb{C}\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\vartheta}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\mathbb{C}\eta}
\textstyle{\Sigma\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\eta}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{Z}
\textstyle{\Sigma\mathbb{C}\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\mathbb{C}\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma f}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\vartheta}
\textstyle{\Sigma\mathbb{C}\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\vartheta}
\textstyle{\Sigma\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma f}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{\Sigma\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma\Omega Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{\Sigma Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma f}
\textstyle{\Sigma\Omega Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}

The diagram commutes by naturality and a triangle identity. This identifies
\widetilde{\vartheta\circ\mathbb{C}f}
with the composite

(1.26)

\textstyle{\Sigma\mathbb{C}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\Sigma Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma f}
\textstyle{\Sigma\Omega Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{Z.}

The adjoint
\widetilde{f\circ\theta}
of
f\circ\theta
is the composite

(1.27)

\textstyle{\Sigma\mathbb{C}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\theta}
\textstyle{\Sigma Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma f}
\textstyle{\Sigma\Omega Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{Z.}

These two composites are equal if and only if the adjoint of
(1.24)
commutes, which holds if and only if
(1.24)
itself commutes. By the universal property of the coequalizer
\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}Y
, this holds if and only if the composite
\varepsilon\circ\Sigma f
factors uniquely through
\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}Y
. This proves our claim.
∎

Remark 1.28
.

The unit
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}
and counit
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{C}}
of the adjunction are described by the following diagrams. Here a little chase shows that
\varepsilon\circ\beta=\varepsilon\circ\Sigma\vartheta
.

\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\Omega\Sigma Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega q}
and
\textstyle{\Sigma{\mathbb{C}}\Omega Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\vartheta}
\textstyle{\Sigma\Omega Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}Y}
\textstyle{Z}

The following reinterpretation of
\alpha
will play a central role in our theory.

Lemma 1.29
.

The map
\alpha
can be reinterpreted as a special case of the unit
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

This is immediate from the following commutative diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}\Omega\Sigma X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\Sigma X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\Sigma\mathbb{C}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\Sigma\mathbb{C}\Omega\Sigma X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\Sigma\Omega\Sigma X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\Sigma X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\Sigma\mathbb{C}\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\Sigma\vartheta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

∎

We can abbreviate the previous diagram to

(1.30)

\textstyle{\mathbb{C}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\Omega\Sigma X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\Omega\Sigma X}

We think of
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}
as formal and
\alpha
as relatively concrete. It is
\alpha
rather than
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}
that leads to computational applications.

Warning 1.31
.

The functor
(\Omega\circ\Sigma)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}(-)
is also defined, but it appears to be of little or no interest. It must not be confused with
\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\circ\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}
.

1.4.
The Beck monadicity theorem

This section is a digressive motivating contextualization. We again let
\Gamma=\Omega\Sigma

be the monad associated to an adjunction
(\Sigma,\Omega)
between
\scr{T}
and
\scr{S}
, with no assumptions on
\scr{T}
and
\scr{S}
in this subsection. We have the
unit and counit
\eta
and
\varepsilon
of the adjunction. The action
\vartheta
of

\Gamma
on objects
\Omega Z
is given by
\Omega\varepsilon
and that gives the functor
\Omega_{\Gamma}\colon\scr{S}\longrightarrow\Gamma[\scr{T}]
.
With our previous notations,
\alpha
specializes to the identity functor
\Gamma\longrightarrow\Omega\Sigma
and
\beta
specializes to
\varepsilon\colon\Sigma\Gamma\longrightarrow\Sigma
. The following definition is standard.

Definition 1.32
.

The adjunction
(\Sigma,\Omega)
is said to be
monadic
if
\Omega_{\Gamma}
is an equivalence of categories.

We need a preliminary definition to state the Beck monadicity theorem. It does not require an adjunction, only a functor
\Omega\colon\scr{S}\longrightarrow\scr{T}
. Some of it, but not the key isomorphism part, substitutes for our requirement in
Section 1.1
that
\scr{T}
and
\scr{S}
are cocomplete.

Definition 1.33
.

An
\Omega
-split pair
is a pair of maps

(1.34)

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{B}

in
\scr{S}
together with a split coequalizer diagram

(1.35)

\textstyle{\Omega A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega f}
\scriptstyle{\Omega g}
\textstyle{\Omega B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{i}

in
\scr{T}
. A functor
\Omega\colon\scr{S}\longrightarrow\scr{T}

creates coequalizers of
\Omega
-split pairs
if

(i) For every
\Omega
-split pair
(f,g)
, there is a fork

(1.36)

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{C}

in
\scr{S}
such that the composite

\eta=\Omega q\circ i\colon X\longrightarrow\Omega B\longrightarrow\Omega C\ \ \text{with}\ \ \eta\circ r=\Omega q

given via the split coequalizer
(1.35)
is an isomorphism and

(ii)
C
is a coequalizer of
f
and
g
for any such fork
(1.36)
.

The functor
\Omega

strictly creates coequalizers of
\Omega
-split pairs
if every split coequalizer

(1.35)
lifts uniquely to a coequalizer
(1.36)
.

Theorem 1.37
.

[Beck monadicity theorem] The adjunction
(\Sigma,\Omega)
is monadic if and only if the functor
\Omega
creates coequalizers of
\Omega
-split pairs.

It is not emphasized, or even mentioned, in most standard categorical references that the proof actually gives an adjoint equivalence

(\Sigma_{\Gamma},\Omega_{\Gamma})
, and that fact is the real starting point of our homotopical analog.

The first step in the proof is the following easy categorical observation
[
Rie16
, Proposition 5.4.9]
. It
has nothing to do with the given adjunction
(\Sigma,\Omega)
.

Proposition 1.38
.

For any monad
\mathbb{C}
on any category
\scr{T}
, the forgetful functor
\mathbb{U}\colon\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]\longrightarrow\scr{T}
strictly creates coequalizers of
\mathbb{U}
-split pairs.

As in
[
Rie16
, Corollary 5.4.10(i)]
, the definition of monadic implies the following consequence, which gives the “only if” implication of
Theorem 1.37
.

Corollary 1.39
.

If
(\Sigma,\Omega)
is monadic, then
\Omega
creates coequalizers of
\Omega
-split pairs.

We complete the proof of
Theorem 1.37
with an elaboration of the “if” part.

Theorem 1.40
.

[Monadicity] If the functor
\Omega
creates coequalizers of
\Omega
-split pairs, then

\Omega_{\Gamma}
has a left adjoint
\Sigma_{\Gamma}
, and
(\Sigma_{\Gamma},\Omega_{\Gamma})
is an adjoint equivalence of categories.

(1.24)

\textstyle{\Gamma Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

This is the special case
(Y,\theta)=(\Omega Z,\Omega\varepsilon)
of

, and
the content of the theorem is that, up to isomorphism, this special case is the
general case. Our hypothesis gives that the fork
(1.43)
displays a
