(1.42)
gives an
isomorphism
\varepsilon_{\Gamma}\colon\Sigma_{\Gamma}\Omega_{\Gamma}Z\longrightarrow Z
such that
\varepsilon_{\Gamma}\circ q=\varepsilon
. As in
Remark 1.28
, the isomorphisms
\eta_{\Gamma}
and
\varepsilon_{\Gamma}
are the unit and counit of the adjunction
(\Sigma_{\Gamma},\Omega_{\Gamma})
.
∎

We emphasize three points that come out of our proof of
Theorem 1.40
.

Corollary 1.45
.

Consider the case
\mathbb{C}=\Gamma
of the
(\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}},\Omega_{\mathbb{C}})
adjunction
(1.8)
.

(i)

The unit
\eta_{\Gamma}
is an isomorphism, hence
\eta_{\Gamma}^{*}
and
\Omega_{\Gamma}
in
(1.8)
are isomorphisms.

(ii)

\eta_{\Gamma}^{*}
expresses a universal property of
\eta_{\Gamma}
: a map of
\Gamma
-algebras
Y\longrightarrow\Omega_{\Gamma}Z
factors uniquely through
\eta_{\Gamma}\colon Y\longrightarrow\Omega_{\Gamma}\Sigma_{\Gamma}Y
.

(iii)

For a
\Gamma
-algebra
(Y,\theta)
,
\theta
factors as
\theta_{\Gamma}\circ\Omega q
, where
\theta_{\Gamma}\colon\Omega_{\Gamma}\Sigma_{\Gamma}Y\longrightarrow Y
is an action of
\Omega_{\Gamma}\Sigma_{\Gamma}
on
Y
;
\theta_{\Gamma}
is the inverse of the isomorphism
\eta_{\Gamma}
. In particular, when
(Y,\theta)=(\Omega_{\Gamma}Z,\Omega\varepsilon)
, the inverse is
\Omega_{\Gamma}\varepsilon_{\Gamma}
, as the triangle identity dictates.

Analogously, in our general context,
Theorem 0.2
says that
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}
is a weak equivalence on grouplike
\mathbb{C}
-algebras
(Y,\theta)
, and we shall see in
Theorem 3.29
that it satisfies a universal property up to homotopy analogous to (ii) of
Corollary 1.45
.

Remark 1.46
.

As far as we know, the categorical literature says nothing about what happens when an adjunction is
not
monadic. In contrast, the group completion property intrinsic to the recognition principal is telling us precisely how different the categories
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
and
\scr{S}
are homotopically. We have introduced
\Omega
-connective objects to deal with the fact that these are the only objects of
\scr{S}
seen by
\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}
, but the homotopical adjunction is general and factors as in
(1.8)
.

2.
The homotopical context

2.1.
Assumptions
2.1
and
2.1
: weak equivalences and
\Omega
-connectivity

Section 1.1
specifies a minimal formal framework, and we flesh it out with minimal homotopical assumptions.

Assumption B.
We assume that
\scr{T}
and
\scr{S}
have classes of weak equivalences satisfying the two out of three property, and we say that a map
f
in
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
is a weak equivalence if
\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{C}}f
is a weak equivalence in
\scr{T}
. We assume that the functors
\Omega
,
\Sigma
, and

\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{C}}
, hence also
\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\mathbb{C}
, preserve weak equivalences, at least under restriction to good objects (as in
Remark 1.2
for example).

Remark 2.1
.

As discussed in
Section 3.5
below,
\scr{T}
and
\scr{S}
are generally model categories such that
(\Sigma,\Omega)
and
(\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{C}},\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{C}})
are Quillen adjunctions. The reader may prefer to assume that holds and restrict the preservation properties accordingly. This can depend on the choice of relevant model structures. Our applications will focus mainly on topological model categories in which all objects are fibrant, hence the right adjoints preserve all weak equivalences, as we assume. The left adjoints do not, but rectifying this generally requires much less than cofibrant approximation. Restricting attention to good objects as in
Remark 1.2
is sufficient and much less of a nuisance in practice. As said before, we tacitly make this restriction throughout. We prefer our objects as they come in nature, with their natural algebraic structure.

Remark 2.2
.

The assumption that
\Sigma
preserves weak equivalences should be regarded as negotiable. It fails in a very interesting way in
Section 7
, but, as there, it can be made true by cofibrant approximation. Even when it holds, the functor
\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}
is problematic. We do not believe that
\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}
preserves weak equivalences, even when restricted to good objects. That will account for some subtleties later. However, as we shall see in
Lemma 3.5
, the assumption on
\Sigma
ensures that the composite
\mathbb{E}=\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\circ\mathrm{Bar}
defined in
Definition 3.1
does preserve weak equivalences (implicitly, between good objects) regardless of whether or not

\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}
does so.

The following additional homotopical assumption is essential to make sense of
Theorem 0.2
, but is peripheral to
Theorem 0.1
.

Assumption C.
We assume that we have a notion of an
\Omega
-connective object in
\scr{S}
such that a map
f
between
\Omega
-connective objects is a weak equivalence if and only if
\Omega f
is a weak equivalence in
\scr{T}
. We assume further that the functor
\Sigma\colon\scr{T}\longrightarrow\scr{S}
takes values in
\Omega
-connective objects.

Remark 2.3
.

In practice, the subcategory
\scr{S}_{C}
of
\Omega
-connective objects in
\scr{S}
is the largest full subcategory in which a map
f
is a weak equivalence if and only if

\Omega f
is a weak equivalence.

Remark 2.4
.

This assumption makes no reference to the monad
\mathbb{C}
. However we shall see in
Proposition 3.18
that, together with our other assumptions,
Section 2.1
implies that the composite
\mathbb{E}=\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\circ\mathrm{Bar}
defined in
Definition 3.1
takes values in
\Omega
-connective objects.

2.2.
Section 2.2
: group completions

A priori, the following curious definition has nothing at all to do with groups or with
Section 1.1
. It formalizes the properties of the notions used throughout iterated loop space theory, and groups do appear prominently in examples.
5
5

5

We thank Dustin Clausen for noticing serious sloppiness in our first version of the definition.

Definition 2.5
.

We abstract what we mean by notions of grouplike objects and group completions in a category
\scr{T}
with a subcategory of weak equivalences. We mean that we have a category
\scr{H}
with a forgetful functor
\mathbb{U}\colon\scr{H}\longrightarrow\scr{T}
together with a full subcategory
\scr{H}_{gp}
of
\scr{H}
, whose objects are called grouplike. We say that a map
f
in
\scr{H}
is a weak equivalence if

\mathbb{U}f
is a weak equivalence in
\scr{T}
and we require that objects of
\scr{H}
weakly equivalent to grouplike objects be grouplike.

We mean further that we have a concomitant notion of a group completion
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
in
\scr{H}
, where
Y
is grouplike. The class of group completions must satisfy the following properties.

(i)

A map
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
in
\scr{H}
, where both
X
and
Y
are grouplike, is a group
completion if and only if it is a weak equivalence.

(ii)

Consider a commutative diagram of maps in
\scr{H}

(2.6)

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{W.}

(a)

If
Z
and
W
are grouplike and
f
and
j
are weak equivalences, so that the diagram is a weak equivalence
g\longrightarrow h
in the arrow category of
\scr{H}
, then
g
is a group completion if and only if
h
is a group completion.

(b)

If
Z
and
W
are grouplike,
g
and
h
are group completions, and
f
is a weak equivalence, then
j
is a weak equivalence.

(c)

If
Y
,
Z
, and
W
are grouplike and
h
and
j
are weak equivalences, then
f
is a group completion if and only if
g
is a group completion.

(d)

If
Y
,
Z
, and
W
are grouplike and
f
and
g
are group completions, then
h
is a weak equivalence if and only if
j
is a weak equivalence.

We regard (ii) as an analog of the two out of three property of weak equivalences.

The examples of
\scr{H}
given in Definitions
4.1
and
4.7
when
\scr{T}
is the category of based spaces should help motivate this abstract definition. Given
\scr{H}
, we now bring a monad
\mathbb{C}
into the picture.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
\mathbb{C}
be a monad whose forgetful functor
\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]\longrightarrow\scr{T}
factors as
\mathbb{U}\circ\mathbb{V}
, where
\mathbb{V}
is a forgetful functor
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]\longrightarrow\scr{H}
.

(a)

Say that a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
Y
is grouplike if
\mathbb{V}Y
is grouplike and say that a map
f
of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras is a group completion if
\mathbb{V}f
is a group completion.

(b)

Define a group completion functor
\mathbb{G}
to be a functor
\mathbb{G}\colon\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]\longrightarrow\scr{H}_{gp}
together with a natural group completion
g\colon\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{C}}Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}Y
in
\scr{H}
for
\mathbb{C}
-algebras
Y
.

Remark 2.8
.

By (i) of
Definition 2.5
,
g
in (b) is a weak equivalence if
Y
is grouplike; by (i), (ii)(c), and (ii)(d) applied to a naturality diagram, a map
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras such that
Y
is grouplike is a group completion if and only if
\mathbb{G}f
is a weak equivalence.

Remark 2.9
.

One might expect that group completions satisfy the universal property that if
g\colon X\longrightarrow Y
is a group completion and
f\colon X\longrightarrow Z
is a map to a grouplike object, then
f
factors uniquely through
g
. We do
not
assume that here. A group completion with such a universal property will enter in
Section 3.4
.

With these definitions in hand, we return to the context of
Section 1.1
.

Assumption D.
We assume the following properties of
\scr{T}
.

(a)

\scr{T}
comes with a category
\scr{H}
with the properties specified in
Definition 2.5
, and the forgetful functor
\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]\longrightarrow\scr{T}
factors through a forgetful functor
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]\longrightarrow\scr{H}
.

(b)

\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z
is a grouplike object of
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
for every object
Z
of
\scr{S}
and there is a group completion functor
(\mathbb{G},g)
, as specified in
Definition 2.7
(b).

2.3.
Section 2.3
: the approximation theorem

As usual, we place ourselves in the context of Assumptions
1.1
and
2.1
. The verifications of Assumptions
2.1
and
2.2
do not require substantial work in any of our examples. The following assumption,
6
6

6

We call this assumption a theorem both because the “approximation theorem” has a long history and because, in contrast with the other assumptions, all of which make sense in greater generality, this assumption requires a theorem specific to the context at hand.
which assumes Assumptions
1.1
,
2.1
, and
2.2
, is by far our most substantial one. It says that the monad
\mathbb{C}
is a homotopical approximation of the monad
\Omega\Sigma
, and we will comment on its proof when we specialize our framework to examples.

Assumption E.
[Approximation theorem] For an object
X\in\scr{T}
, the map

\alpha\colon\mathbb{C}X\longrightarrow\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma X

defined in
Notations 1.4
is a group completion. It is therefore a weak equivalence when
\mathbb{C}X
is grouplike.

We will explain how to go from this result to
Theorem 0.1
in
Section 3.2
, and we will explain how to go from
Theorem 0.1
to
Theorem 0.2
in
Section 3.3
.

Remark 2.10
.

We remark that
\mathbb{C}X
is very often not grouplike. In our topological examples, it is grouplike only when
X
is connected. The only role of the group completion functor
\mathbb{G}
of
Section 2.2
is to serve as a tool that will enable us to deduce the recognition principle from the approximation theorem.

2.4.
Section 2.4
: Simplicial preliminaries

Our proofs of Theorems
0.1
and
0.2
are based on use of the two-sided monadic bar construction. In particular, it gives an especially precise derived approximation
\overline{Y}
of a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
Y
. It is constructed simplicially, and we here give assumptions on simplicial objects in
\scr{T}
and
\scr{S}
that suffice for the construction. The assumptions are redundant. Some are merely formal and others are standard. In our examples, only condition (vii) is substantial. We state the assumptions first and then give comments on them.

Assumption F.
In addition to their being cocomplete and having subcategories of weak equivalences, we assume that
\scr{T}
and
\scr{S}
have standard notions of homotopy. We assume the following results about simplicial objects in
\scr{T}
and
\scr{S}
. We write
s\scr{V}
for the category of simplicial objects in a category
\scr{V}
.

(i)

There are realization functors
|-|\colon s\scr{T}\longrightarrow\scr{T}
and
|-|\colon s\scr{S}\longrightarrow\scr{S}
and they are left adjoints.

(ii)

The functors
|-|
on
s\scr{T}
and
s\scr{S}
preserve homotopies.

(iii)

The functors
|-|
on
s\scr{T}
and
s\scr{S}
preserve weak equivalences between Reedy cofibrant objects.
7
7

7

The name comes from Reedy’s thesis
[
Ree74
]
. The senior author used the word “proper” before Reedy cofibrancy was introduced
[
May72
,
May74
]
.

(iv)

Realization commutes with the (left adjoint) functors
\Sigma
and
\mathbb{C}
. That is, for
K_{*}
in
s\scr{T}
, there are natural isomorphisms

\textstyle{\Sigma|K_{*}|\cong|\Sigma K_{*}|\,\,\text{and}\,\,\mathbb{C}|K_{*}|\cong|\mathbb{C}K_{*}|.}

By
Definition 1.20
, It follows that, for simplicial objects
K_{*}
in
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
,
|K_{*}|
is in
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
and

\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}|K_{*}|\cong|\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}K_{*}|.

(v)

|-|
on
s\scr{S}
takes levelwise
\Omega
-connective objects to
\Omega
-connective objects.

(vi)

For
K_{*}\in s\scr{S}
,
the natural map
\gamma\colon|\Omega K_{*}|\longrightarrow\Omega|K_{*}|
,
which is the adjoint of the composite

\textstyle{\Sigma|\Omega K_{*}|\cong|\Sigma\Omega K_{*}|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{|\varepsilon|}
\textstyle{|K_{*}|,}

is a map of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras.

(vii)

If
K_{*}\in s\scr{S}
is levelwise
\Omega
-connective, then the map
\gamma
is a weak equivalence.

Remark 2.11
.

Note that only (iv) and (vi) mention the monad
\mathbb{C}
. Those two conditions are formal or almost so. Note too that there is no mention of grouplike objects or of group completions. Thus
Section 2.4
is almost entirely concerned with general properties of realization and its specialization to the functors
\Sigma
and
\Omega
. Therefore it is largely independent of our other Assumptions. In our examples, none of the conditions (i) through (vi) present any difficulty, whereas (vii), for spaces, was the hardest result in
[
May72
]
.

Remark 2.12
.

Realization functors
|-|\colon s\scr{W}\longrightarrow\scr{W}
are generally defined by categorical tensor products

|K_{*}|=K_{*}\otimes_{\Delta}\Delta^{v}_{*}.

Here
\Delta
is the simplex category, so that
K_{*}
is a contravariant functor
\Delta\longrightarrow\scr{W}
,
\scr{W}
is tensored and cotensored over some ground category
\scr{V}
, and
\Delta^{v}_{*}
is a covariant simplex functor
\Delta\longrightarrow\scr{V}
. Then realization is a left adjoint with right adjoint
\mathbb{S}
given on
X\in\scr{W}
by

\mathbb{S}(X)_{q}=F(\Delta_{q}^{v},X),

where
F
is the cotensor functor
\scr{V}^{op}\times\scr{W}\longrightarrow\scr{W}
. Taking
\scr{V}=\scr{W}
to be the category of spaces, this is the definition of the realization functor on simplicial spaces and of its right adjoint, which generalizes the total singular complex from simplicial sets to simplicial spaces. This works the same way for good topological categories
\scr{W}
of spectra
[
EKMM97
,
LMS86
,
MM02
,
MMSS01
]
, which are tensored and cotensored over the category
\scr{V}
of spaces. The general version was already observed by Kan
[
Kan58
]
, and there are many other examples.

Remark 2.13
.

We remark on (i)-(vii) of
Section 2.4
in order.

(i)

This holds in all examples where
Remark 2.12
applies.

(ii)

This refers to the standard notion of homotopy in the category of simplicial objects in any category (e.g.
[
May67
, Definitions 5.1]
); the proof for simplicial spaces in
[
May72
, Corollary 11.10]
can easily be formalized.

(iii)

For spaces, this goes back to
[
May74
, Theorem A.4]
. There are many more recent proofs using Reedy cofibrancy.

(iv)

Here we apply given functors levelwise to simplicial objects. Space level proofs are given in
[
May72
, Proposition 12.1]
for suspension and in
[
May72
, Theorem 12.2]
for monads associated to operads, but those results belabor the obvious. As a left adjoint, realization commutes with colimits, and the categorical Fubini’s theorem (e.g.
[
ML98
, Section IX.8]
) applies to commute colimits from which

\Sigma
and
\mathbb{C}
are generally constructed with realization.

(v)

This is a direct verification from the definitions in the examples we know.

(vi)

In the special case of a monad associated to a particular operad, this assumption is proven in
[
May72
, Theorem 12.4]
, but the argument can almost be formalized as follows. The claim is that the following diagram commutes.

\textstyle{\mathbb{C}|\Omega X|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{C}\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{|\mathbb{C}\Omega X|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{|\vartheta|}
\textstyle{|\Omega X|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\Omega|X|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta}
\textstyle{\Omega|X|}

Passing to adjoints and writing
\tilde{\gamma}
for the adjoint of
\gamma
, the diagram becomes

\textstyle{\Sigma\mathbb{C}|\Omega X|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\mathbb{C}\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\Sigma|\mathbb{C}\Omega X|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma|\vartheta|}
\textstyle{\Sigma|\Omega X|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\gamma}}
\textstyle{|\Sigma\Omega X|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{|\varepsilon|}
\textstyle{\Sigma\mathbb{C}\Omega|X|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\vartheta}
\textstyle{\Sigma\Omega|X|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{|X|.}

The rectangle is obtained by applying
\Sigma
to what is in spirit a naturality diagram; it commutes by inspection in examples since
\vartheta
is defined the same way before and after realization. The lower triangle commutes by a chase of adjoints and the upper triangle commutes by definition.

(vii)

As said before, this assumption is substantial. For spaces and the loop functor, it is proven in
[
May72
, Theorems 12.3 and 12.7]
. For
G
-spaces, it is proven in
[
CW91
,
Hau80
]
by fixed point reduction to the nonequivariant case. It is shown how to pass to spectra in
[
May09b
]
, and the same argument works for
G
-spectra
[
GM17
]
.

We will often use the following elementary observation.

Lemma 2.14
.

For a simplicial object
K_{*}
in a category
\scr{T}
and an object
J
of
\scr{T}
, a map
f\colon K_{0}\longrightarrow J
such that
f\circ d_{0}=f\circ d_{1}
induces a map from
K_{*}
to the constant simplicial object at
J
and thus induces a map
\xi\colon|K_{*}|\longrightarrow J
in
\scr{T}
.

We will write
\zeta
instead of
\xi
when it is a homotopy equivalence for formal reasons.

3.
Proofs and variant versions of Theorems
0.1
and
0.2

3.1.
The original version of the recognition principle

For a monad
(\mathbb{C},\mu,\eta)
, a
\mathbb{C}
-functor
(\Sigma,\beta)
and a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
(Y,\theta)
, we have a monadic bar construction
B(\Sigma,\mathbb{C},Y)
, namely the realization of the evident simplicial object in
\scr{S}
with
q
-simplices
\Sigma\mathbb{C}^{q}Y
; its faces and degeneracies are given by
\beta
,
\mu
,

\theta
and
\eta
. The following definition has been implicit throughout.

Definition 3.1
.

For
Y\in\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
, define
\overline{Y}=B(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C},Y)
and
\mathbb{E}Y=\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{Y}
.
We give the functor
Y\mapsto\overline{Y}
the name
\mathrm{Bar}
.

A standard extra degeneracy argument
[
May72
, Proposition 9.8]
, using (ii) of
Section 2.4
and
Lemma 2.14
, proves the following result.

Lemma 3.2
.

For
Y\in\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
, the evident maps
\mathbb{C}^{q+1}Y\longrightarrow Y
induce a natural map
\zeta\colon\overline{Y}\longrightarrow Y
of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras. Viewed as a map in
\scr{T}
, it is a homotopy equivalence with homotopy inverse
\nu\colon Y\longrightarrow\overline{Y}
induced by the maps
\eta\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{q+1}Y
.

Remark 3.3
.

We emphasize that
\eta
and
\nu
are only maps in
\scr{T}
, not in
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
.

Specializing to the
\Sigma
of
Section 1.1
,
Proposition 1.10
gives isomorphisms

\Sigma\mathbb{C}^{q}Y\cong\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}^{q+1}Y.

This gives an isomorphism of simplicial objects in
\scr{S}
,
Remark 1.22
giving the compatibility of zeroth faces. Passing to realizations, this gives the following result.
8
8

8

This is
[
May09b
, (8.6)]
, but it was not given sufficient emphasis there.

Lemma 3.4
.

For
Y\in\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
,
\mathbb{E}Y=\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{Y}
is naturally isomorphic to
B(\Sigma,\mathbb{C},Y)
.

By Assumptions
2.1
and
2.4
(iii), together with our implicit restriction to good objects, this implies the following result.

Lemma 3.5
.

The functor
\mathbb{E}
preserves weak equivalences.

Parenthetically, by taking
Y=\mathbb{C}X
, we have a symmetric variant of
Lemma 3.2
.

Lemma 3.6
.

For
X\in\scr{T}
, the evident maps
\Sigma\mathbb{C}^{q+1}X\longrightarrow\Sigma X
induce a natural map
\zeta\colon\mathbb{E}\mathbb{C}X\longrightarrow\Sigma X
in
\scr{S}
, and it is a homotopy equivalence with homotopy inverse
\nu\colon\Sigma X\longrightarrow\mathbb{E}{\mathbb{C}X}
induced by the maps
\Sigma\mathbb{C}^{q}\eta\colon\Sigma\mathbb{C}^{q}X\longrightarrow\Sigma\mathbb{C}^{q+1}X
.

Observe that the extra degeneracy is seen in the first variable of
B(-,-,-)
, using
\eta
, in
Lemma 3.2
but is seen in the third variable, using the
\mathbb{C}^{q}\eta
, in
Lemma 3.6
.

Lemma 3.4
recovers the description of
\mathbb{E}
first given topologically in
[
May72
]
, and we shall see that the functor
\mathbb{E}
, and others of the same general form, specialize to give all known variants of the operadic recognition principle and several new ones. Interpreted model categorically, as in
Section 3.5
, we can view
\mathbb{E}
as the composite of the well-structured cofibrant approximation
\mathrm{Bar}
and the monadically coequalized functor
\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}
. The equivalent original form
B(\Sigma,\mathbb{C},Y)
views this as a homotopically well-behaved derived approximation of the monadic tensor product
\Sigma\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}Y
.

We shall explain three versions of the recognition principle. The first, proven in this subsection, is a generalized form of the original version in
[
May72
]
. We show in the next subsection how that version implies the more conceptual version stated in
Theorem 0.1
. Finally, we give a new fully conceptual version in subsection
3.4
.

With Assumptions
1.1
,
2.1
,
2.1
and
2.4
, the following diagram of maps of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras compares the identity functor with
\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{E}
.

(3.7)

\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{\overline{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\scriptstyle{B\alpha}
\textstyle{B(\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma,\mathbb{C},Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}B(\Sigma,\mathbb{C},Y).}

Here
\zeta
is a homotopy equivalence in
\scr{T}
and
\gamma
is a weak equivalence. The map
B\alpha
is

B(\alpha,\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})\colon B(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C},Y)\longrightarrow B(\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma,\mathbb{C},Y).

It is a map of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras since
\alpha
is a map of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras. Generalizing
[
May74
, Theorem 2.3(ii)]
, we explain how the approximation theorem,
Section 2.3
, implies that
B\alpha
is a group completion. As said before, this is the only place where the group completion functor
\mathbb{G}
of
Definition 2.5
and
Section 2.2
comes into play.

Proposition 3.8
.

If our Assumptions hold, then
B\alpha
is a group completion.

Proof.

Let
Y
be a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra and consider the following commutative diagram.

\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{B(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C},Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{B\alpha}
\scriptstyle{Bg}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\textstyle{B(\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma,\mathbb{C},Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Bg}
\textstyle{\mathbb{G}Y}
\textstyle{B(\mathbb{G}\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C},Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{B(\mathbb{G}\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\textstyle{B(\mathbb{G}\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma,\mathbb{C},Y)}

By (ii)(a) of
Definition 2.5
, since
g
is a group completion and the
\zeta
are weak equivalences, the middle vertical arrow
Bg=B(g,\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})
is a group completion. By (ii)(d) of
Definition 2.5
,
\mathbb{G}\alpha\colon\mathbb{G}\mathbb{C}X\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma X
is a weak equivalence for any
X\in\scr{T}
since

\alpha\colon\mathbb{C}X\longrightarrow\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma X
and
g\colon\mathbb{C}X\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}\mathbb{C}X
are group completions and

g\colon\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma X\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma X
is a weak equivalence
by (i) of
Definition 2.5
. Therefore
B(\mathbb{G}\alpha)=B(\mathbb{G}\alpha,\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})
is the realization of a levelwise weak equivalence and is thus a weak equivalence. Similarly, the right vertical arrow
Bg
is the realization of a levelwise weak equivalence and is thus a weak equivalence. By (ii)(c) of
Definition 2.5
, it follows that
B\alpha
is a group completion.
∎

Putting things together, the following generalization of the original version of the recognition principle follows from our Assumptions. In view of the long history of applications, we view it as the most calculationally accessible version, although, as we shall see, we now view it as somewhat lacking in conceptual clarity and elegance.

Theorem 3.9

(Recognition principle, original version)
.

The following statements about the maps of
\mathbb{C}
-spaces in
(3.7)
hold.

(i)

The map
\zeta
is a homotopy equivalence with a natural homotopy inverse
\nu
.

(ii)

The map
{B\alpha}
is a group completion and is therefore a weak equivalence if the
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
Y
is grouplike.

(iii)

The map
\gamma
is a weak equivalence.

Therefore the composite

(3.10)

\gamma\circ B\alpha\circ\nu\colon Y\longrightarrow\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{E}Y

is a group completion and is thus a weak equivalence if
Y
is grouplike.

3.2.
A more conceptual version of the recognition principle

To go from the original version to the more conceptual version stated in
Theorem 0.1
, we use the following conceptual reinterpretation
of the composite
\gamma\circ B\alpha
.

Proposition 3.11
.

The composite
\gamma\circ B\alpha
in
(3.7)
can be identified with the unit

\eta_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\overline{Y}\longrightarrow\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{Y}

of the adjunction
(\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}},\Omega_{\mathbb{C}})
.

Proof.

Recalling (vi) of
Section 2.4
, for
K_{*}\in s\scr{S}
we let
\gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\colon|\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}K_{*}|\longrightarrow\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}|K_{*}|
denote
\gamma
regarded as a map of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras and we let
\tilde{\gamma}_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}|\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}K_{*}|\longrightarrow|K_{*}|
denote its adjoint. A small diagram chase from (iv) and (vi) of
Section 2.4
shows that
\tilde{\gamma}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the composite displayed in the middle square of the following diagram, in which the vertical isomorphisms are given by a natural isomorphism displayed in (iv) of
Section 2.4
and by
Proposition 1.10
. The isomorphism at the top right is the isomorphism
\mathbb{E}Y\cong\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{Y}
given by
Lemma 3.4
, and we use that to identify the target of
\tilde{\gamma}_{\mathbb{C}}
with
\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{Y}
and thus to identify the target of
\gamma_{\mathbb{C}}
with
\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{Y}
.

\textstyle{\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}B(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C},Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}B\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}B(\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma,\mathbb{C},Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\gamma}_{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{B(\Sigma,\mathbb{C},Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}B(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C},Y)}
\textstyle{B(\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C},Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{B(\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\eta_{\mathbb{C}})}
\textstyle{B(\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C},Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{B\varepsilon_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{B(\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C},Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{B(\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C},Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}

The left square uses
Lemma 1.29
to identify
\alpha
as
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}
and the right square commutes by inspection of definitions.
The bottom composite is the identity, by a triangle identity. The rightmost vertical isomorphism is the inverse of the leftmost one, so that isomorphism conjugates the bottom composite to the top composite. Therefore the top composite is also the identity. Since
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}
is the adjoint of the identity, this gives the conclusion.
∎

Diagrammatically, we can embed
(3.7)
in the following commutative diagram, which can be thought of as an analogue of
(1.30)
.

(3.12)

\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\overline{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\scriptstyle{B\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{B(\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma,\mathbb{C},X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}B(\Sigma,\mathbb{C},X)}

It follows that the recognition principle in the form stated in
Theorem 0.1
is just a restatement of
Theorem 3.9
. We restate the main point to emphasize the analogy with the monadicity theorem.

Theorem 3.13
.

For a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
Y
,
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\overline{Y}\longrightarrow\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{Y}
is a group completion. If
Y
is grouplike, then
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}
is a weak equivalence. In particular,
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\overline{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z}\longrightarrow\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z}
is a weak equivalence for any
Z\in\scr{S}
.

Parenthetically, note that we can take
Y=\mathbb{C}X
for
X\in\scr{T}
in
(3.7)
. In this case, we find that the approximation theorem can be seen as a special case of the recognition principle. This can be viewed as a generalized version of the Barratt–Priddy–Quillen theorem in topology that identifies the space
QS^{0}=\Omega^{\infty}\Sigma^{\infty}S^{0}
whose homotopy groups are the stable homotopy groups of spheres as a group completion of
\mathbb{C}S^{0}
for an
E_{\infty}
operad
\mathbb{C}
. Up to homotopy,
\mathbb{C}S^{0}
is the disjoint union of a basepoint and the classifying spaces
B{\Sigma_{n}}
of symmetric groups for
n\geq 1
.

Theorem 3.14
.

[Barratt–Priddy–Quillen] For
X\in\scr{T}
, the following diagram of maps of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras commutes.

(3.15)

\textstyle{\overline{\mathbb{C}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\scriptstyle{B\alpha}
\textstyle{B(\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma,\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C}X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\textstyle{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{E}\mathbb{C}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\zeta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma X}

The vertical arrows are given by Lemmas
3.2
and
3.6
and are weak equivalences.

3.3.
The homotopical monadicity theorem

Recall that we regard
Theorem 0.2
as a homotopical analog of
Theorem 1.40
, the categorical monadicity theorem. We define the following categories.

Definition 3.16
.

Let
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]_{gr}\subset\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
be the full subcategory of grouplike
\mathbb{C}
-algebras. Let
\scr{S}_{c}\subset\scr{S}
be the full subcategory of
\Omega
-connective objects.

Assumptions
2.2
and
2.4
(v) imply the following result.

Lemma 3.17
.

The functor
\mathbb{E}
takes values in
\scr{S}_{c}
.

We need the following variant
\xi
of
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{C}}
and of a triangle identity to go from the recognition principle to the homotopical monadicity theorem.

Proposition 3.18
.

For
Z\in\scr{S}_{c}
, there is a natural map

\xi\colon\mathbb{E}\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z=\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z}\longrightarrow Z

such that the weak equivalence
\zeta\colon\overline{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z}\longrightarrow\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z
factors as the composite

(3.19)

\textstyle{\overline{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\xi}
\textstyle{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z.}

Proof.

We apply
Lemma 2.14
to the simplicial object
B_{*}(\Sigma,\mathbb{C},\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z)
and the counit
\varepsilon\colon\Sigma\Omega Z\longrightarrow Z
of the adjunction
(\Sigma,\Omega)
to obtain the promised map

\xi\colon\mathbb{E}\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z=B(\Sigma,\mathbb{C},\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z)\longrightarrow Z.

Lemma 2.14
applies since an easy formal argument as in
[
May72
, Examples 9.5]
shows that
\varepsilon
coequalizes the pair

\textstyle{\Sigma{\mathbb{C}}\Omega Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\vartheta}
\textstyle{\Sigma\Omega Z.}

Lemma 2.14
also gives the diagonal arrow in the following commutative diagram of maps of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras.

\textstyle{B(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C},\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\scriptstyle{B\alpha}
\textstyle{B(\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma,\mathbb{C},\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z}
\textstyle{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}B(\Sigma,\mathbb{C},\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\xi}

By
Proposition 3.11
, this gives the stated factorization of
\zeta
.
∎

The following slightly more precise version of
Theorem 0.2
follows directly.

Theorem 3.20
.

The functors
\mathbb{E}\colon\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]_{gr}\longrightarrow\scr{S}_{c}
and
\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\scr{S}_{c}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]_{gr}
induce inverse equivalences on passage to homotopy categories. Thus every
\Omega
-connective object of
\scr{S}
is weakly equivalent to
\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{Y}
for some
grouplike
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
Y
.

Proof.

For
Y\in\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]_{gr}
, the recognition principle gives a natural weak equivalence between
Y
and
\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{E}Y
. For
Z\in\scr{S}_{c}
, consider
(3.19)
. The map
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}
is a weak equivalence since
\overline{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}Z}
is grouplike. Since the composite
\zeta
is also a weak equivalence, so is
\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\xi
. Since
Z
and
\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{E}(\Omega Z)
are
\Omega
-connective and
\Omega\xi
is a weak equivalence,
\xi
is a weak equivalence.
∎

3.4.
A fully conceptual reinterpretation of Theorems
0.1
and
0.2

Here we reformulate our main theorems in terms of a new conceptual notion of grouplike objects and group completions. We would not know how to prove these results starting just from the new definitions. We can view the notions given in Definitions
2.5
and
2.7
and built into our work via
Section 2.2
as scaffolding leading up to the entirely formal context that is given in this subsection. Here we give restatements of the main results that make no reference to
Section 2.2
, although that is used in their proofs. One can imagine alternative proofs.

We first define
\mathbb{C}
-grouplike objects and
\mathbb{C}
-group completions. The following definition would appear more intuitive if we replaced

\overline{Y}
with
Y
, but we use
\overline{Y}
to get around the fact that we do not know that the functor
\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}
preserves weak equivalences, as pointed out in
Remark 2.2
.

Notation 3.21
.

We abbreviate notation by writing
\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}=\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}
in this section. Recall that, by
Section 2.2
,

\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}
takes values in grouplike
\mathbb{C}
-algebras.

Definition 3.22
.

A
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
Y
is
\mathbb{C}
-grouplike
if

\eta_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\overline{Y}\longrightarrow\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{Y}
is a weak equivalence. A
\mathbb{C}
-group completion
of a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
X
is a map
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras such that
Y
is
\mathbb{C}
-grouplike and
\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{f}
is a weak equivalence, where

\overline{f}=B(\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id},f)\colon B(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C},X)\longrightarrow B(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C},Y).

The idea is illuminated by the following naturality diagram.

(3.23)

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\overline{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\overline{f}}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{f}}
\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{\overline{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{Y}.}

Since the maps
\zeta
are equivalences,
f
is a
\mathbb{C}
-group completion if and only if
\overline{f}
is, and we can interpret the diagram as showing that a
\mathbb{C}
-group completion
f
is weakly equivalent to
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\overline{X}\longrightarrow\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{X}
.

Retaining our general Assumptions, including
Section 2.2
, we have the following comparison theorem.

Theorem 3.24
.

A
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
Y
is grouplike if and only if it is
\mathbb{C}
-grouplike. A map
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras is a group completion if and only if it is a
\mathbb{C}
-group completion.

Proof.

If
Y
is
\mathbb{C}
-grouplike, then
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\overline{Y}\longrightarrow\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{Y}
is a weak equivalence to a grouplike object, hence
\overline{Y}
and therefore
Y
are grouplike. The second statement of
Theorem 3.13
says conversely that a grouplike
\mathbb{C}
-algebra is
\mathbb{C}
-grouplike.

For the second statement of the theorem, consider a map
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras, where
Y
and therefore
\overline{Y}
is grouplike. By the first statement,

\overline{Y}
is also
\mathbb{C}
-grouplike. Now consider the naturality diagram
(3.23)
.
The maps
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}
are group completions by
Theorem 3.13
, hence the right-hand map
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}
is a weak equivalence.
If
f
is a
\mathbb{C}
-group completion, then
\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{f}
is a weak equivalence and
\overline{f}
is a group completion by
(ii)(c) of
Definition 2.5
. Conversely, if
f
is a group completion, then
\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{f}
is a weak equivalence by (ii)(d) of
Definition 2.5
, and that means that
f
is a
\mathbb{C}
-group completion.
∎

Corollary 3.25
.

Taking
\scr{H}
and
\scr{T}
in
Definition 2.5
to both be
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
,
\mathbb{C}
-grouplike objects and
\mathbb{C}
-group completions satisfy the conditions required of a category of grouplike objects and group completions in the category
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
.

Proof.

Restricting attention to
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
-algebras and their maps, the required properties of
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
-grouplike objects
and
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
-group completions are inherited from those of grouplike objects and group completions in
\scr{H}
.
∎

Corollary 3.25
is conceptually satisfactory, especially if one thinks of the functor
\mathrm{Bar}
as giving a highly structured cofibrant approximation, as in
Section 3.5
below, but a direct proof without use of
Theorem 3.24
seems problematic.
In our new language, Theorems
0.1
and
0.2
can be restated as follows.

Theorem 3.26
.

There is a functor
\mathrm{Bar}\colon\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
, written
Y\mapsto\overline{Y}
, and a natural equivalence
\zeta\colon\overline{Y}\longrightarrow Y
such that the unit
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\overline{Y}\longrightarrow\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{Y}
is a
\mathbb{C}
-group completion and is therefore an equivalence if
Y
is
\mathbb{C}
-grouplike.

Theorem 3.27
.

(\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}},\Omega_{\mathbb{C}})
induces an adjoint equivalence from the homotopy category of
\mathbb{C}
-grouplike
\mathbb{C}
-algebras in
\scr{T}
to the homotopy category of
\Omega
-connective objects of
\scr{S}
.

While these theorems are
proven
using any notions of grouplike objects and group completions in
\scr{T}
and
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
, as in Definitions
2.5
and
2.7
and
Section 2.2
, they are now
stated
entirely in terms of conceptual definitions within the category
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
. Moreover, our new definition of a
\mathbb{C}
-group completion is characterized by a universal property in the relevant homotopy categories, as we now show.

Definition 3.28
.

Define
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]_{\mathbb{C}\text{-}gp}
to be the full subcategory of
\mathbb{C}
-grouplike objects in
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
.

Theorem 3.29
.

Let
X
be a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra and
Y
be a
\mathbb{C}
-grouplike
\mathbb{C}
-algebra. The map

\eta_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}\colon\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]_{\mathbb{C}\text{-}gp}(\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{X},Y)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}](\overline{X},Y)

induces a natural isomorphism on passage to homotopy categories.

Intuitively, the theorem says that any map of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras
\overline{X}\longrightarrow Y
factors uniquely up to homotopy through the
\mathbb{C}
-group completion

\eta_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\overline{X}\longrightarrow\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{X}
. This should be compared with
Corollary 1.45
about the categorical monadicity theorem.

Proof.

In view of the equivalence
\zeta\colon\overline{Y}\longrightarrow Y
, we may replace
Y
by
\overline{Y}
in the statement, and we carry out the proof with this replacement. This allows us to use that
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\overline{Y}\longrightarrow\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{Y}
is a weak equivalence and is therefore invertible in the homotopy category. Let us write
[X,Y]
for the set of maps
X\longrightarrow Y
in any given homotopy category.

For
X\in\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
and
Y\in\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]_{\mathbb{C}gp}
, we have the induced map

\eta_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}\colon[\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{X},\overline{Y}]\longrightarrow[\overline{X},\overline{Y}]

given by
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}([g])=[g\circ\eta_{\mathbb{C}}]
. We define

(\eta_{\mathbb{C}}^{*})^{-1}\colon[\overline{X},\overline{Y}]\longrightarrow[\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{X},\overline{Y}]

by
(\eta_{\mathbb{C}}^{*})^{-1}([f])=[\eta_{\mathbb{C}}]^{-1}\circ[\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}f]
.
By the naturality diagram
(3.23)
(with
\overline{f}
there replaced by
f
here),
\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}f\circ\eta_{\mathbb{C}}=\eta_{\mathbb{C}}\circ f
. Passing to homotopy and post-composing with
[\eta_{\mathbb{C}}]^{-1}
, this implies that

(\eta_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}\circ(\eta_{\mathbb{C}}^{*})^{-1})([f])=[f].

Now consider the following diagram, whose top row represents
((\eta_{\mathbb{C}}^{*})^{-1}\circ\eta_{\mathbb{C}}^{*})([g])
.

(3.30)

\textstyle{\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\eta_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}g}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[\eta_{\mathbb{C}}]^{-1}}
\textstyle{\overline{Y}}
\textstyle{\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\overline{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{\mathbb{C}}}

By the unit conditions of the monad
\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}
,
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}\circ\Gamma\eta_{\mathbb{C}}=\operatorname{id}=\mu_{\mathbb{C}}\circ\eta_{\mathbb{C}}
; the middle square commutes by the naturality of
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}
. Thus

\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}g\circ\eta_{\mathbb{C}}=\eta_{\mathbb{C}}\circ g=\eta_{\mathbb{C}}\circ g\circ\mu_{\mathbb{C}}\circ\eta_{\mathbb{C}}.

We claim that
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{X}\longrightarrow\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{X}
is a weak equivalence. Granting the claim, passing to homotopy, and precomposing with the resulting
[\eta_{\mathbb{C}}]^{-1}
, it follows that

[\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}g]=[\eta_{\mathbb{C}}\circ g\circ\mu_{\mathbb{C}}].

Precomposing with
[\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\eta_{\mathbb{C}}]
on
\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{X}
, using the left triangle, and post-composing with
[\eta_{\mathbb{C}}]^{-1}
on

\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{Y}
, this implies that

((\eta_{\mathbb{C}}^{*})^{-1}\circ\eta_{\mathbb{C}}^{*})([g])=[g].

To prove the claim, consider the naturality square

\textstyle{\overline{\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\textstyle{\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\zeta}
\textstyle{\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{X}}

Since
\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{X}
is grouplike, the top arrow
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}
is a weak equivalence. Since
\zeta
is a weak equivalence, one can check that

\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\zeta
is a weak equivalence by inspection and (iii) and (v) of
Section 2.4
. Therefore the bottom arrow
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}
is a weak equivalence.
∎

Remark 3.31
.

We do not have an obvious analogue of part (iii) of
Corollary 1.45
due to the contrasting roles of
\mathbb{C}
and
\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}
here, but note that the diagram
(3.30)
implies that
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}
is the homotopical inverse of

\eta_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{X}\longrightarrow\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{X}
.

3.5.
A model theoretic reinterpretation

In our applications, we are given model structures on
\scr{T}
and
\scr{S}
such that
(\Sigma,\Omega)
is a Quillen adjunction. Moreover, a standard model categorical result (e.g.
[
MP12
, Theorem 16.2.5]
) generally applies to show that
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
is a model category with fibrations and weak equivalences created by the forgetful functor
\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]\longrightarrow\scr{T}
. Then
(\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{C}},\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{C}})
is a Quillen adjunction. Since
\Omega
preserves fibrations and weak equivalences, it is immediate that
\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}
preserves fibrations and weak equivalences. That is, the following result holds.

Proposition 3.32
.

If
(\Sigma,\Omega)
is a Quillen adjunction, then
(\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}},\Omega_{\mathbb{C}})
is a Quillen adjunction.

While thinking model theoretically did not lead us to our present perspective, it does conceptualize the results. When working with spaces or
G
-spaces and using the standard Quillen model structures, the bar construction
\overline{Y}
has the homotopy type of a cofibrant
\mathbb{C}
-algebra under minimal hypotheses
[
ABG+14
, Lemma 5.50]
. It is therefore a cofibrant approximation of
Y
in
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
if we instead use the mixed model structures of
[
MP12
, Section 17]
. Thus we are entitled to think of the functor
\mathbb{E}
of
Definition 3.1
as the composite of a cofibrant approximation and the left adjoint of a Quillen adjunction. In the language of
[
MMSS01
]
, the homotopical monadicity theorem is then given by a connective Quillen equivalence.

Remark 3.33
.

In the example of orbital presheaves in Part 2,
we are led to an illuminating alternative point of view.
There the interest is entirely focused on objects in a model category that are definitely
not
cofibrant and we use classical cellular cofibrant approximation as a tool to convert such examples to well-behaved cofibrant objects that feed naturally into our machine. Nevertheless, it is convenient and perhaps necessary there to continue using the bar construction, even though it would be pointless to consider it as a cofibrant approximation.

4.
Monads associated to operads in iterated loop space theory

We record how classical and equivariant iterated and infinite loop space theory appear in our context.

4.1.
Delooping based spaces

This is the original example and all proofs are in place, as we have sketched. We focus on what is new in the interpretation of our Assumptions.

Here
\scr{T}
and
\scr{S}
are both taken to be the category of based spaces and
(\Sigma,\Omega)
in the general theory is taken to be
(\Sigma^{n},\Omega^{n})
, the
n
-fold suspension and
n
-fold loop space functors. We take
\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{C}_{n}
to be the monad on based spaces associated to an
E_{n}
-operad
\scr{C}=\scr{C}_{n}
that acts naturally on
\Omega^{n}X
for spaces
X
, such as the little
n
-cubes operad, the little
n
-discs operad, or the Steiner operad for
\mathbb{R}^{n}
or the product of one of these with any
E_{\infty}
-operad. As in Remarks
1.2
and
2.1
, to ensure that these monads behave well homotopically, we implicitly restrict attention to nondegenerately based spaces when applying
\Sigma
or
\mathbb{C}
. As said before, basepoint issues are handled carefully in
[
MMO
]
and we shall say no more about them here.

We clearly have a context as specified in
Section 1.1
, and
Section 2.1
holds under the implicit restriction just mentioned. With any of the standard model structures on
\scr{T}
(e.g.
[
MP12
, Chapter 17]
),
(\Sigma^{n},\Omega^{n})
is a Quillen adjunction since
\Omega^{n}
clearly preserves fibrations and acyclic fibrations. We define
\Omega^{n}
-connective to mean
n
-connective, alias
(n-1)
-connected, and then
Section 2.1
is also satisfied. As noted in
Remark 2.11
,
Section 2.4
is almost entirely concerned with realization and its application to the functors
\Sigma
and
\Omega
. The references in
Remark 2.13
show that it is satisfied here. It remains to discuss Assumptions
2.2
and
2.3
.

We start with the following classical definitions, modified very slightly to fit
Definition 2.5
. This gives the context in which the approximation theorem is proven for
n\geq 2
. Let
\mathrm{Gr}
denote the Grothendieck group functor (alias group completion) from abelian monoids to abelian groups and let
i\colon\operatorname{Id}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Gr}
denote the natural map. If
f\colon M\longrightarrow A
is a homomorphism from an abelian monoid to an abelian group, we write
\tilde{f}
for the map of groups
\mathrm{Gr}M\longrightarrow A
such that
\tilde{f}\circ i=f
. We think of the following definition as giving a homologically defined category
\scr{H}_{com}
that satisfies
Definition 2.5
. It is an elaboration of
[
GM17
, Definition 1.6]
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\scr{H}
be the category of homotopy associative Hopf spaces and Hopf maps. It is understood that Hopf spaces are (nondegenerately) based at their unit objects and that Hopf maps are based.
A space in
\scr{H}
is said to be grouplike if
\pi_{0}(X)
is a group. Let
\scr{H}_{com}
be the full subcategory of homotopy commutative Hopf spaces in
\scr{H}
. Define a group completion
f\colon Y\longrightarrow Z
to be a map in
\scr{H}_{com}
such that
Z
is grouplike,
f
induces an isomorphism
\tilde{f}\colon\mathrm{Gr}\pi_{0}(Y)\longrightarrow\pi_{0}(Z)
, and, for any field
k
of coefficients,
f_{*}
localizes the homology of
Y
by inverting
\pi_{0}(Y)\subset H_{*}(Y)
. That is,
f_{*}
induces an isomorphism

\textstyle{\tilde{f}\colon H_{*}(Y)[\pi_{0}(Y)^{-1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H_{*}(Z).}

It suffices to take
k
to be
\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathbb{F}_{p}
for any prime
p
.

The definition just given is designed for use when
n\geq 2
, so that the relevant Hopf spaces are homotopy commutative. We focus on the case
n=1
in
Section 4.2
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Using the notion of group completion given in
Definition 4.1
, the category
\scr{H}_{com}
satisfies the conditions specified in
Definition 2.5
.

Proof.

This is easy, but we give some details in the hopes of making
Definition 2.5
more intuitively sensible than it might have seemed on a first reading. It is obvious that an object of
\scr{H}
that is weakly equivalent to a grouplike object is grouplike.
It is classical that an object
X\in\scr{H}_{com,gp}
is weakly equivalent in
\scr{H}
to
X_{0}\times\pi_{0}(X)
, where
X_{0}
is the component of the identity element
0
of
\pi_{0}(X)
. Recalling that Hopf spaces are simple, it follows from a theorem of Whitehead that a map between objects of
\scr{H}_{com,gp}
is a weak equivalence if and only if it induces isomorphisms on
\pi_{0}
and on
H_{*}
for all fields of coefficients or, equivalently, for integer coefficients. Now (i) of
Definition 2.5
is clear. For (ii), looking back at
(2.6)
, we first assume that
Z
and
W
are grouplike. On passage to
\pi_{0}
or to
H_{*}
, the vertical composites in the following diagrams are either
g_{*}
or
h_{*}
.

\textstyle{\pi_{0}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Gr}\pi_{0}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{f}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{g}_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Gr}\pi_{0}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{h}_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}(W)}

\textstyle{H_{*}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{H_{*}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{H_{*}(X)[\pi_{0}(X)^{-1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{f}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{g}_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{*}(Y)[\pi_{0}(Y)^{-1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{h}_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{*}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{*}(W)}

In (ii)(a),
f
and
j
are weak equivalences, so
f_{*}
, the induced map
\tilde{f}_{*}
, and
j_{*}
are isomorphisms. It is immediate from
2
out of
3
in the bottom squares that
\tilde{g}_{*}
is an isomorphism if and only if
\tilde{h}_{*}
is an isomorphism, which verifies that (ii)(a) is satisfied. In (ii)(b),
g
and
h
are group completions and
f
is a weak equivalence, so
2
out of
3
in the bottom square implies that
j_{*}
is an isomorphism, so that
j
is a weak equivalence. Finally, we assume that
Y
,
Z
, and
W
are grouplike in
(2.6)
. Then the bottom squares of the diagrams above reduce to

\textstyle{\mathrm{Gr}\pi_{0}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{f}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{g}_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}(W)}

\textstyle{H_{*}(X)[(\pi_{0}(X)^{-1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{f}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{g}_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{*}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{*}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{*}(W)}

In (ii)(c),
h_{*}
and
j_{*}
are isomorphisms, hence
\tilde{f}_{*}
is an isomorphism if and only if
\tilde{g}_{*}
is an isomorphism. In (ii)(d),
\tilde{f}_{*}
and

\tilde{g}_{*}
are isomorphisms, hence
h_{*}
is an isomorphism if and only if
j_{*}
is an isomorphism.
∎

When
n\geq 2
, it is clear that a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra is a homotopy associative and commutative Hopf space, so that
\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{C}}
factors through
\scr{H}_{com}
. To verify
Section 2.2
, it remains to construct a group completion functor
(\mathbb{G},g)
as in
Definition 2.7
. Here we follow
[
May72
, Theorem 13.5]
and

[
GM17
, Theorem 1.14(ii)]
. Let
\scr{M}
be the operad
9
9

9

alias
\mathrm{Asso}
, the associativity monad
(with permutations, as usual) whose algebras are topological monoids

[
May72
, Definition 3.1]
. Whichever sequence of compatible operads we choose, the operad
\scr{C}=\scr{C}_{n}
comes with an inclusion of the operad
\scr{C}_{1}
. The operad
\scr{C}_{1}
is an
A_{\infty}
operad as defined in
[
May72
, Definition 3.5]
. Moreover, there is a map of operads
\delta\colon\scr{C}_{1}\longrightarrow\scr{M}
such that the
\Sigma_{j}
-map

\delta(j)\colon\scr{C}_{1}(j)\longrightarrow\scr{M}(j)
is a
\Sigma_{j}
-equivalence for each
j
, so that the induced map of monads
\delta\colon\mathbb{C}_{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}
is given by weak equivalences. The following result is implied by
Lemma 3.2
and
Section 2.4
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
Y
be a
\mathbb{C}_{1}
-algebra. Then

(i)

\zeta\colon B(\mathbb{C}_{1},\mathbb{C}_{1},Y)\longrightarrow Y
is a map of
\scr{C}_{1}
-algebras and a homotopy equivalence with inverse
\nu
.

(ii)

B\delta\colon B(\mathbb{C}_{1},\mathbb{C}_{1},Y)\longrightarrow B(\mathbb{M},\mathbb{C}_{1},Y)
is a weak equivalence.

Notation 4.4
.

Recall that the classifying space
BM
of a topological monoid
M
is the geometric realization of the nerve of
M
, where
M
is viewed as the morphism space of a topological category with a single object. The inclusion of
1
-simplices gives a natural map
\iota\colon\Sigma M\longrightarrow BM
. Its adjoint is a natural map
{\chi}\colon M\longrightarrow\Omega BM
.

Definition 4.5
.

Let
Y
be a
\scr{C}=\scr{C}_{n}
-algebra, and regard
Y
as a
\scr{C}_{1}
-algebra by pullback along
\scr{C}_{1}\longrightarrow\scr{C}_{n}
. Define
\mathbb{F}Y
to be the topological monoid

B(\mathbb{M},\mathbb{C}_{1},Y)
and define
\mathbb{G}Y=\Omega B\mathbb{F}Y
. Note that
\nu
in
Lemma 3.2
is a Hopf map, and define
g\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}Y
to be the natural composite of Hopf maps

\textstyle{g\colon Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu}
\textstyle{B(\mathbb{C}_{1},\mathbb{C}_{1},Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{B\delta}
\textstyle{B(\mathbb{M},\mathbb{C}_{1},Y)=\mathbb{F}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{G}Y}

We recall a version of the Barratt–Quillen group completion theorem.

Theorem 4.6
.

If
M
is a homotopy commutative topological monoid, then the map
\chi\colon M\longrightarrow\Omega BM
is a group completion. If, further,
M
is grouplike, then
\chi
is a weak equivalence. Therefore, for
Y\in\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
,
g\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}Y
is a group completion and is a weak equivalence if
Y
is grouplike.

Proof.

The version in
[
May75
, Theorem 15.1]
assumes that left translation by an element
x
is homotopic to right translation by
x
in both
M
and
\Omega BM
. Clearly that holds for
M
when
M
is homotopy commutative. For
\Omega BM
, that hypothesis is only used to ensure that
\pi_{0}(\Omega BM)=\pi_{1}(BM)
is commutative (see
[
May75
, Lemma15.2]
). This holds when
M
is homotopy commutative since
\pi_{0}(\Omega BM)
is then the Grothendieck group of
\pi_{0}(M)
, by
[
Ram19
, Proposition B.1]
.
10
10

10

We thank Dan Ramras for this reference.
The weak equivalence consequence is immediate from a comparison of fiber sequences that is given in
[
May75
, Theorem 7.6]
.
∎

This applies when
n\geq 2
to complete the verification of
Section 2.2
. To deal with the case
n=1
,
we adopt the following variant of
Definition 4.1
, which is a variant of
[
GM17
, Definition 1.7]
.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
\scr{H}_{m}
be the subcategory of
\scr{H}
whose objects are weakly equivalent to topological monoids and whose maps are weakly equivalent in the arrow category of
\scr{H}
to maps of monoids. A space in
\scr{H}_{m}
is said to be grouplike if
\pi_{0}(X)
is a group. A map
f
in

\scr{H}_{m}
is an
m
-group completion if it is weakly equivalent in the arrow category of
\scr{H}_{m}
to a map
\chi\colon M\longrightarrow\Omega BM
for a topological monoid
M
. The definition makes sense since, for a topological monoid
M
,
\Omega BM
is homotopy equivalent to the Moore loop space
\Lambda BM
, which is a topological monoid.

We leave it as an exercise to prove the following analog of
Lemma 4.2
.

Lemma 4.8
.

Using the notion of
m
-group completion given in
Definition 4.7
, the category
\scr{H}_{m}
satisfies the conditions specified in
Definition 2.5
when
n\geq 1
.

Remark 4.9
.

We could alternatively use the more rigid conceptual notions of
\mathbb{C}_{1}
-grouplike
\mathbb{C}_{1}
-algebras and
\mathbb{C}_{1}
-group completions that are given in
Definition 3.22
, together with
Corollary 3.25
.

Using
m
-group completions and
Definition 4.5
,
Section 2.2
is now immediate for
n\geq 1
. In view of the following result, the
m
-notion is mainly of interest when
n=1
since it can be used interchangeably with the original notion of
Definition 4.1
when
n\geq 2
. Let
\scr{H}_{m,com}
be the full subcategory of homotopy commutative Hopf spaces in
\scr{H}_{m}
.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
f\colon Y\longrightarrow Z
be a map in
\scr{H}_{m,com}
. If
f
is an
m
-group completion, then
f
is a group completion. Conversely, if
f
is also a map of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras (in particular, if
n\geq 2
) and is a group completion, then
f
is an
m
-group completion.

Proof.

If
f
is an
m
-group completion, then it is a group completion by
Theorem 4.6
. For the converse,
f
is equivalent in
\scr{H}_{com}
to
\mathbb{F}Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}Y=\Omega B\mathbb{F}Y
.
∎

It remains to consider the approximation theorem,
Section 2.3
. When
X
is connected, it states that
\alpha=\alpha_{n}\colon\mathbb{C}_{n}X\longrightarrow\Omega^{n}\Sigma^{n}X
is a weak equivalence and is proven in
[
May72
, Theorem 6.1]
for all
n\geq 1
. The proof goes by induction on
n
, using a comparison of fiber sequences

\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{n}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{n}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{n}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{n-1}\Sigma X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{n-1}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{n}\Sigma^{n}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P\Omega^{n-1}\Sigma^{n}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Omega^{n-1}\Sigma^{n}X,}

taking
\alpha_{0}
to be the identify functor on
\Sigma X
to start the induction. The work is in the construction of the contractible space
\mathbb{E}_{n}X
giving the quasi-fibration displayed in the top row. When
n=1
, this weak equivalence is also implied by James’ 1954 paper
[
Jam55
]
that introduced the James construction, which, by historical misuse, we have denoted
\mathbb{M}
.

For general spaces
X
, there is a proof for
n\geq 2
by direct computation of mod
p
homology for all primes
p
, with their Bockstein spectral sequences, by Fred Cohen
[
CLM76
, Theorems 3.1, 3.2 , and 3.12 and Corollary 3.3]
11
11

11

Some small corrections are given by Wellington

[
Wel82
, p. 10]
; the corresponding calculation with rational coefficients is easy and is essentially implied by the Bockstein spectral sequence calculations. There is also a geometric proof by Graeme Segal
[
Seg73
]
. It proceeds by an induction analogous to that for the connected case. For the case
n=1
, we shall explain a proof of the following version in
Section 4.2
.

Theorem 4.11
.

For a
\scr{C}_{1}
-space
Y
,
\alpha_{1}\colon\mathbb{C}_{1}Y\longrightarrow\Omega\Sigma Y
is an
m
-group completion.

The following theorem is now an immediate specialization of our general results. It is essentially a restatement of results first proven in
[
May72
,
May74
]
and generalized equivariantly in
[
GM17
]
.

Theorem 4.12
.

Take
\scr{C}=\scr{C}_{n}
. For
\scr{C}
-spaces
Y
, the unit
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}
induces a group completion if
n\geq 2
(an
m
-group completion if
n\geq 1
) and therefore a
\mathbb{C}
-group completion

Y\longrightarrow\Omega^{n}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{E}Y\cong\Omega^{n}_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma^{n}_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{Y}.

Moreover,
n
-connective based spaces
Z
are naturally equivalent to coequalized
n
-fold suspensions
\Sigma^{n}_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\Omega^{n}_{\mathbb{C}}Z}
.

4.2.
Moore loop spaces and
1
-fold loop spaces

It is classical that the
1
-fold loop space
\Omega X
is equivalent to the Moore loop space
\Lambda X
, which is a topological monoid, and that, for connected
X
,
\Omega\Sigma X
is equivalent to the James construction on
X
, which we have denoted
\mathbb{M}X
. Largely following Thomason
[
Tho79
]
and, more closely, Fiedorowicz
[
Fie84
]
, we put this into a conceptual framework that elucidates the case
n=1
of
Section 4.1
. We should say that there is a wealth of further information in
[
Tho79
,
Fie84
]
that we shall not describe. At the end, we very briefly indicate how the ideas here are related to McDuff’s proof
[
McD79
]
that all connected spaces are weakly equivalent to classifying spaces of discrete monoids, which is itself a variant of the proof by Kan and Thurston
[
KT76
]
that all connected spaces are equivalent to plus constructions of classifying spaces of discrete groups.

In this subsection,
\scr{T}=\scr{S}
is the category of based spaces.

Definition 4.13
.

Define
\scr{V}
to be the category of based spaces
\pi\colon X\longrightarrow[0,\infty)
over
[0,\infty)
such that
\pi^{-1}(0)=\ast
. We take
0
to be the basepoint of
[0,\infty)
, and we use the notation
(X,\pi)
for
\pi\colon X\longrightarrow[0,\infty)
.

We first recall the Moore loop space functor
\Lambda^{v}\colon\scr{T}\longrightarrow\scr{V}
and its left adjoint
\Sigma_{v}\colon\scr{V}\longrightarrow\scr{T}
and then use an adjunction
(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R})
between
\scr{V}
and
\scr{T}
to relate
(\Sigma_{v},\Lambda^{v})
to
(\Sigma,\Omega)
via a composite adjunction. The Moore loop space functor as usually understood is
\Lambda=\mathbb{L}\Lambda^{v}\colon\scr{T}\longrightarrow\scr{T}
, and we shall
write
\Xi=\Sigma_{v}\mathbb{R}\colon\scr{T}\longrightarrow\scr{T}
to match. Up to isomorphism, these are
\Omega
and
\Sigma
and are therefore also adjoint.
We then relate these adjunctions to two monads on
\scr{T}
and to two related monads on
\scr{V}
and finally show how these all fit together to give a conceptual understanding of
Theorem 4.11
.

Definition 4.14
.

Define
\Lambda^{v}\colon\scr{T}\longrightarrow\scr{V}
by letting
\Lambda^{v}X
be the set of pairs
(f,s)
, where
s\in[0,\infty)
and
f\colon[0,\infty)\longrightarrow X
is a continuous map such that
f(0)=\ast
and
f(t)=\ast
for all
t\geq s
. Topologize
\Lambda^{v}X
as a subspace of

\mathrm{Map}([0,\infty),X)\times[0,\infty)
and define
\pi(f,s)=s
. Define
\Sigma_{v}\colon\scr{V}\longrightarrow\scr{T}
by

\Sigma_{v}(X,\pi)=X\times[0,{\infty})/\{(x,t)|t=0\ \ \text{or}\ \ t\geq\pi(x)\}.

Both
\Lambda^{v}
and
\Sigma_{v}
extend to morphisms in obvious ways.

The lemmas from here to the diagram
(4.27)
are all easily verified from the definitions. Almost all are parts of
[
Fie84
, Lemmas 6.2, 6.6, and 6.7]
.

Lemma 4.15
.

(\Sigma_{v},\Lambda^{v})
is an adjoint pair of functors between
\scr{V}
and
\scr{T}
.

Definition 4.16
.

Define
\mathbb{L}\colon\scr{V}\longrightarrow\scr{T}
by
\mathbb{L}(X,\pi)=X
. Define
\mathbb{R}\colon\scr{T}\longrightarrow\scr{V}
by letting
\mathbb{R}(X)
be the subspace

\mathbb{R}(X)=(\ast,0)\cup\{(x,s)|x\in X\ \ \text{and}\ \ s>0\}

of
X\times[0,\infty)
and letting
\pi\colon\mathbb{R}(X)\longrightarrow[0,\infty)
be the projection,
\pi(x,s)=s
.

Lemma 4.17
.

(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R})
is an adjoint pair of functors. For
(X,\pi)\in\scr{V}
, the unit
\eta\colon(X,\pi)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}(X,\pi)
sends
x\in X
to
(x,\pi(x))
. For
X\in\scr{T}
, the counit
\varepsilon\colon\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}X\longrightarrow X
sends
(x,s)
to
x
. Both
\eta
and
\varepsilon
are homotopy equivalences.

Lemma 4.18
.

For
(X,\pi)\in\scr{V}
, define a natural homeomorphism

\iota\colon\Sigma\mathbb{L}X\longrightarrow\Sigma_{v}X

by
\iota(x,t)=(x,t\pi(x))
for
t\in I
. Its inverse is given by

\iota^{-1}(x,t)=(x,t/\pi(x))
for
t\in[0,\pi(x)]
. For
X\in\scr{T}
, define a natural isomorphism
\mathbb{R}\Omega X\cong\Lambda^{v}X
by
letting
(f,s)\in\mathbb{R}\Omega X
correspond to
(g,s)\in\Lambda^{v}X
if
g(ts)=f(t)
for
t\in I
.

Lemma 4.19
.

(\Sigma_{v},\Lambda^{v})
is isomorphic to the composite adjunction
(\Sigma\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R}\Omega)
.

Nothing said above refers to products on loop spaces.

Definition 4.20
.

We say that
(Y,\pi)
is a monoid in
\scr{V}
if
Y
is a monoid with unit
\ast
and
\pi
is a morphism of monoids, where
[0,\infty)
is regarded as a monoid under addition. Let
\mathrm{Mon}[\scr{T}]
and
\mathrm{Mon}[\scr{V}]
denote the categories of monoids in
\scr{T}
and monoids in
\scr{V}
. Clearly
\mathbb{L}
restricts to a functor
\mathbb{L}\colon\mathrm{Mon}[\scr{V}]\longrightarrow\mathrm{Mon}[\scr{T}]
.

Lemma 4.21
.

For
X\in\scr{T}
,
\Lambda^{v}X
is a monoid in
\scr{V}
with product

(f,s)(g,t)=(f+g,s+t),

where
f+g
is
f
on
[0,s]
and
g
on
[s,s+t]
, hence
\Lambda X=\mathbb{L}\Lambda^{v}X
is a monoid in
\scr{T}
.

Lemma 4.22
.

If
M
is a monoid in
\scr{T}
, then
\mathbb{R}M
is a submonoid of
(M\times[0,\infty),\pi)
in
\scr{V}
. The adjunction

(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R})
restricts to an adjunction between
\mathrm{Mon}[\scr{V}]
and
\mathrm{Mon}[\scr{T}]
.

Turning to monads, we have the following general observation. It applies to any adjunction
(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R})
and does not require
\varepsilon\colon\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Id}
to be an isomorphism, although later we will be most interested in the case when it is.

Lemma 4.23
.

If
\mathbb{C}
is a monad in
\scr{T}
, then
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}
is a monad in
\scr{V}
with unit

\mathbb{R}\eta_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{L}\colon\operatorname{Id}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}
and product

\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\varepsilon}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}\mu_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{L}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}.}

If
(X,\theta)
is a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra, then
\mathbb{R}X
is a
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}
-algebra with action

\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\varepsilon}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}\theta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}X.}

Again letting
\scr{M}
be the associativity operad, recall that its associated monad on
\scr{T}
sends
X
to the free monoid
\mathbb{M}X
on
X
with unit
\ast
. Let
\eta=\eta_{\mathbb{M}}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}X
be the unit,
\eta(x)=x
. Then
\mathbb{M}
is the James construction, but we continue our historical abuse of notation with the following redefinition.

Definition 4.24
.

Define the James construction
\mathbb{J}
to be the monad
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{M}\mathbb{L}
on
\scr{V}
.

Remark 4.25
.

Fiedorowicz instead defines
\mathbb{J}
to be the free monoid monad
\mathbb{F}
on
\scr{V}
.
12
12

12

In
[
Fie84
]
,
\mathbb{J}
is used for both
\mathbb{M}
and his
\mathbb{J}
. We find a notational distinction to be helpful.
It is defined by

\mathbb{F}(X,\pi)=(\mathbb{M}X,\tilde{\pi})
, where
\tilde{\pi}\colon\mathbb{M}X\longrightarrow[0,\infty)
is the unique map of monoids
such that
\tilde{\pi}\circ\eta_{\mathbb{M}}=\pi
. From the point of view of
\scr{V}
, this is the obvious sensible choice. However,
\scr{V}
is introduced only to elucidate structure in
\scr{T}
. By freeness, there is a unique map of monoids

\nu\colon\mathbb{F}(X,\pi)\longrightarrow\mathbb{J}(X,\pi)
such that
\nu\circ\eta_{\mathbb{F}}=\eta_{\mathbb{J}}
. These give a map of monads
\mathbb{F}\longrightarrow\mathbb{J}
, and it is an equivalence. Another way of seeing
\nu
is to view the identity map
\mathbb{M}X\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}X
for
X\in\scr{T}
as a map of monoids

\mathbb{L}\mathbb{F}(X,\pi)\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}\mathbb{L}(X,\pi)
.
Its adjoint is our equivalence

\nu\colon\mathbb{F}(X,\pi)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathbb{M}\mathbb{L}(X,\pi)=\mathbb{J}(X,\pi)

of monoids on
\scr{V}
. Our
\mathbb{J}
is essential to the conceptual comparison between structure in
\scr{V}
and structure in
\scr{T}
that is our focus, hence we will never use
\mathbb{F}
.

From here, we abbreviate notation by setting
\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{C}_{1}
. As noted before, we have a map
\delta\colon\scr{C}\longrightarrow\scr{M}
of operads and thus an induced map
\delta\colon\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}
of monads. By pullback of actions, it induces a functor

\delta^{*}\colon\mathbb{M}[\scr{T}]\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
. The monad
\mathbb{D}
of the following definition is new. It gives a convenient conceptual intermediary between
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{J}
.
13
13

13

Use of it can simplify the proofs of other results of
[
Tho79
,
Fie84
]
not considered here.
Examples like it will be central to Part II.

Definition 4.26
.

Define
\mathbb{D}
to be the monad
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}
on
\scr{V}
and define
\delta\colon\mathbb{D}\longrightarrow\mathbb{J}
to be the induced map
\mathbb{R}\delta\mathbb{L}
of monads on
\scr{V}
. By pullback of actions, it induces a functor

We explain its proof, but without giving full details.

The following equalities are immediate from
\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}=\operatorname{Id}
and our definitions.

\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L},\ \ \mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C},\ \ \mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{C}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mathbb{J}=\mathbb{R}\mathbb{M}\mathbb{L},\ \ \mathbb{J}\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{R}\mathbb{M},\ \ \mathbb{L}\mathbb{J}\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{M}.

Under the isomorphism
\mathbb{R}\Omega\Sigma\mathbb{L}\cong\Lambda^{v}\Sigma_{v}
,
\eta
for
\Lambda^{v}\Sigma_{v}
factors as the composite

\textstyle{\operatorname{Id}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}\eta\mathbb{L}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\Omega\Sigma\mathbb{L}}

and
\eta\mathbb{R}=\operatorname{id}\colon\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{R}
. Using these, we find that the top two squares make sense and commute in the
following diagram. Since the relationship between

and
\mathbb{J}
is formally the same as the relationship between

and
\mathbb{D}
,
the bottom two squares commute by the same arguments. It is formal that the middle two squares commute. We write

, and

The top squares identify
\mathbb{R}\alpha_{\mathbb{C}}
with
\alpha_{\mathbb{D}}
, the middle squares say that

\alpha_{\mathbb{J}}\circ\delta=\alpha_{\mathbb{D}}
, and the bottom squares identify
\mathbb{R}\alpha_{\mathbb{M}}
with
\alpha_{\mathbb{J}}
. We repeat that
\delta\colon\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}
is a natural equivalence because

\scr{C}(j)\longrightarrow\scr{M}(j)
is a

-equivalence for all

\delta\colon\mathbb{D}\longrightarrow\mathbb{J}
is a
natural equivalence. Therefore

holds if the composite

\alpha_{\mathbb{M}}=\vartheta_{\mathbb{M}}\circ\mathbb{M}\eta
on the bottom row is an
m
-group completion. Fiedorowicz
[

]
has given an ingenious argument from here, starting from an insight of Thomason
[
