]
. We shall explain the idea but not give full details.

Let
\mathrm{Mon}
be the category of monoids in
\scr{T}
. Below, monoids mean topological monoids with unit as basepoint (assumed to be non-degenerate). Recall
Notation 4.4
. The following idea comes from
[
Tho79
]
.

Definition 4.31
.

Say that a monoid
N
is grouplike if
\pi_{0}(N)
is a group. Clearly this holds if and only if
N
is
m
-grouplike as a Hopf space. Say that a map

f\colon M\longrightarrow N
of monoids, where
N
is grouplike, is a
{mon}
-group completion if the induced map
Bf\colon BM\longrightarrow BN
is a weak equivalence.

Lemma 4.32
.

If
f\colon M\longrightarrow N
is a
{mon}
-group completion, then
f
is an

m
-group completion as a map of Hopf spaces.

Proof.

This follows from the commutative diagram

\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\chi}
\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi}
\textstyle{\Omega BM\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega Bf}
\textstyle{\Omega BN.}

If
Bf
is a weak equivalence, then so is
\Omega Bf
. Since
N
is grouplike, comparison of the path space fibration of
BN
with the universal quasi-fibration
EN\longrightarrow BN
(e.g.
[
May75
, Theorem 7.6]
) shows that
\chi
for
N
is a weak equivalence. Therefore

f
is weakly equivalent to
\chi
for
M
in the arrow category of
\scr{H}_{m}
.
∎

Thus to prove that

is connected,

is a weak equivalence by the following result,

[

.

Theorem 4.34
.

The top composite
\iota\circ\Sigma\eta_{\mathbb{M}}
in
(4.33)
is a weak equivalence.

Brief Sketch.

The total singular complex
SM
of a monoid
M
is a simplicial discrete monoid. Standard properties of geometric realization as in
Section 2.4
reduce the proof to the case when
X
is discrete, which is proven in
[
Fie84
, Lemma 6.9]
by use of the analog for monoids (
[
Fie84
, Theorem 4.1]
) of a result of J.H.C. Whitehead
[
Whi39
]
(restated as
[
Fie84
, Theorem 4.0]
) for groups. The result for monoids describes when
B
takes pushouts of discrete monoids to pushouts (up to homotopy equivalence) of spaces, and that allows an induction up skeleta.
∎

The cited result of Whitehead and its analog for monoids underlie the results of Kan and Thurston and of McDuff
[
KT76
,
McD79
]
, respectively, and the latter is given a simplified proof
[
Fie84
, Theorem 3.5]
using
[
Fie84
, Theorem 4.1]
. Fiedorowicz

[
Fie84
, Theorem 6.15]
also gives a curious homological group completion result for the map

. That result is a partial non-commutative generalization of
Theorem 4.6
.

4.3.
Delooping based G-spaces for finite groups
G

Here

We take

Definition 4.35
.

A Hopf
G

G

G

are the identity.

Definition 4.36
.

Let
\scr{H}_{G}
be the category of
G

G

is

The definition just given is designed for use when

Definition 4.37
.

Let

.

By passage to fixed points, using

We say that a

Theorem 4.42
.

However, we are interested in the case of general

.
We restate
[
GM17
, Theorem 1.11]
. It restricted to the Steiner operad
\scr{K}_{V}
, but it applies to our
\scr{C}_{V}
.

Theorem 4.43

(The approximation theorem)
.

If
\mathbb{R}\subset V
,
\alpha_{V}\colon\mathbb{C}_{V}X\longrightarrow\Omega^{V}\Sigma^{V}X
is an
m
-group completion.
Therefore, if
\mathbb{R}^{2}\subset V
,
\alpha_{V}\colon\mathbb{C}_{V}X\longrightarrow\Omega^{V}\Sigma^{V}X
is a group completion.

We are very far from a computational proof along the lines of Cohen’s nonequivariant proof. The proof discussed in
[
GM17
]
is due to Rourke and Sanderson
[
RS00
]
and starts from Segal’s nonequivariant proof and their “compression theorem”. There is further work to be done here.

Finally, we comment on
Section 2.4
. Geometric realization commutes with finite products and pullbacks and therefore commutes with finite limits. Passage to fixed points is a finite limit, so realization on
G
-spaces commutes with passage to fixed points. From here, the verification that realization satisfies parts (i) through (vi) of
Section 2.4
presents no difficulty, as indicated in Remarks
2.11
and
2.13
. Part (vii) was proven by Hauschild
[
Hau80
]
by reduction to the nonequivariant case, and the proof also appears in
[
CW91
, pp. 495-496]
.

Thus all assumptions are satisfied, with more detailed verifications available in
[
GM17
]
and the other cited sources. We remark that

\mathbb{C}S^{0}\simeq\amalg_{j\geq 0}F(V,j)/\Sigma_{j},

where
F(V,j)
is the configuration
G
-space of (ordered)
j
-tuples of distinct points of
V
.
It is surprising how little these naturally occurring equivariant configuration spaces have been studied.
We have the following analog of
Theorem 4.12
.

Theorem 4.44
.

Take
\scr{C}=\scr{C}_{V}
. For
\scr{C}
-spaces
Y
, the unit
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}
induces a group completion if
\mathbb{R}^{2}\subset V
(an
m
-group completion if

\mathbb{R}\subset V
) and therefore a
\mathbb{C}
-group completion

Y\longrightarrow\Omega^{V}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{E}Y\cong\Omega^{V}_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma^{V}_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{Y}.

Moreover,
V
-connective based
G
-spaces
Z
are naturally equivalent to coequalized
V
-fold suspensions
\Sigma^{V}_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\Omega^{V}_{\mathbb{C}}X}
.

Remark 4.45
.

It is proven in
[
MMO
]
that the results of this subsection apply equally well to compact Lie groups
G
, provided that we restrict attention to just those representations
V
all of whose isotropy groups have finite index in
G
.

Remark 4.46
.

For a given
V
, define
\scr{F}_{V}
to be the family of subgroups
H
of
G
such that
G/H
embeds in
V
. If we restrict to those
H\in\scr{F}_{V}
when defining the group completion property and
\Omega^{V}
-connectivity, then, using the Steiner operad for
V
, the proof goes through to give a family version of
Theorem 4.44
.

4.4.
Moore loop
G
-spaces and
1
-fold loop
G
-spaces

In this subsection, we adapt
Section 4.2
to prove
Theorem 4.42
. We stay in the context of
Section 4.3
with
n=1
, taking
\scr{C}
and
\mathbb{C}
to be
\scr{C}_{\mathbb{R}^{1}}
and
\mathbb{C}_{\mathbb{R}^{1}}
, except that we no longer require
G
to be finite; it can be any topological group here. We add in
G\scr{V}=G\scr{T}/[0,\infty)
, where
G
acts trivially on
[0,\infty)
. As far as we know, nothing at all of the equivariant version of
Section 4.2
appears in the literature. However we claim that nearly all of it adapts without change equivariantly and, where change is needed, passage to fixed point spaces does the trick.

We use the same notations equivariantly for the equivariant versions of all of the functors and transformations in
Section 4.2
. The introductory paragraphs apply verbatim. We adapt
Definition 4.14
by letting
G
act on Moore loops
just as it acts on ordinary loops:
(gf)(t)=gf(t)
. We define a
G
-monoid
M
to be a
G
-space which is a monoid such that
g(mn)=gm\cdot gn
. Then all of the definitions and results recorded in
4.14
through

4.30
apply exactly as written but with
\scr{T}
and
\scr{V}
replaced by
G\scr{T}
and
G\scr{V}
and with monoids understood to be
G
-monoids.

We then have the category
G\mathrm{Mon}
of
G
-monoids and
G
-monoid maps. For a
G
-monoid
M
, we define the classifying
G
-space
BM
and the natural
G
-maps
\iota\colon\Sigma M\longrightarrow BM
and
\chi\colon M\longrightarrow\Omega BM
exactly as in
Notation 4.4
. That is,
G
-acts through its action of
G
on
M
and nothing else changes.
16
16

16

We ignore consideration of what
BM
classifies since that is irrelevant here.
We write out the changes to
Definition 4.31
needed from here.

Definition 4.47
.

Say that a
G
-monoid
N
is grouplike if
N
is
m
-grouplike as a Hopf
G
-space. Say that a map

f\colon M\longrightarrow N
of
G
-monoids, where
N
is grouplike, is a
{G{mon}}
-group completion if the induced
G
-map
Bf\colon BM\longrightarrow BN
is a weak equivalence of
G
-spaces.

The proof of
Lemma 4.32
applies to show that if
f
is a
G
-group completion, then
f
is an
m
-group completion as a map of Hopf
G
-spaces. Then the diagram
(4.33)
applies to reduce the proof of
Theorem 4.42
to the following statement.

Theorem 4.48
.

For a
G
-space
X
, the composite
G
-map

(4.49)

\textstyle{\Sigma X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\eta_{\mathbb{M}}}
\textstyle{\Sigma\mathbb{M}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{B\mathbb{M}X}

is a weak equivalence.

This holds nonequivariantly by
Theorem 4.34
. Thus the following result implies that it also holds equivariantly, as required.

Theorem 4.50
.

Let
X
be a based
G
-space and
M
be a
G
-monoid.

(i)

(\Omega X)^{H}
is naturally isomorphic to
\Omega(X^{H})
.

(ii)

(\Sigma X)^{H}
is naturally isomorphic to
\Sigma(X^{H})
.

(iii)

(\mathbb{M}X)^{H}
is naturally isomorphic to
\mathbb{M}(X^{H})
.

(iv)

(BM)^{H}
is naturally isomorphic to
B(M^{H}).

(v)

After passage to
H
-fixed points, the composite
(4.49)
is isomorphic to

\textstyle{\Sigma X^{H}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\eta_{\mathbb{M}}}
\textstyle{\Sigma\mathbb{M}X^{H}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{B\mathbb{M}X^{H}.}

Proof.

Remember that basepoints are assumed to be non-degenerate when that is needed. It is needed here since we use the standard fact that passage to
H
-fixed points preserves pushouts, one leg of which is a
G
-cofibration. Clearly (i) holds and it is now also clear that (ii) holds. Let
\sigma_{n}\colon sX^{n}\subset X^{n+1}
denote the space of points one coordinate of which is the basepoint, and similarly for
M
. Then
\sigma_{n}
is a
G
-cofibration.
For (iii),
\mathbb{M}X
is filtered by word length, and
F_{n+1}\mathbb{M}X
is obtained from
F_{n}X
and
X^{n+1}
by a unit identification given by a pushout diagram, one leg of which is
\sigma_{n}
. For (iv),
BM
is the geometric realization of a simplicial
G
-space with
n
-simplices
M^{n}
. As such, it is filtered and
F_{n+1}BM
is obtained from
F_{n}BM
and
X^{n+1}
by a pushout diagram, one leg of which is the product of
\sigma_{n}
and the identity map of
\Delta_{n+1}
. The maps
\eta_{M}
and
\iota
are each the inclusion of the first filtration, hence (v) follows.
∎

Parenthetically, while we have not pursued the details, in view of the result of McDuff
[
McD79
]
and its reproof by Fiedorowicz
[
Fie84
]
, it seems plausible that the following equivariant generalization holds.
17
17

17

Sunny Zhang has since proven this
[
Zha23
]
.

Conjecture 4.51
.

Let
G
be a discrete group. Then any
G
-connected based
G
-space
X
is weakly equivalent to
BM(X)
for some discrete
G
-monoid
M(X)
.

As in the nonequivariant proof of
[
Fie84
, Theorem 3.5]
, this holds if the classifying space of any topological
G
-monoid is equivalent to the classifying space of some discrete
G
-monoid, as is proven nonequivariantly in
[
Fie84
, Theorem 3.4]
.

4.5.
Spectra and the adjunction
(\Sigma^{\infty},\Omega^{\infty})

To pass from iterated loop space theory to infinite loop space theory, it is necessary to choose a good target category of spectra. This is not the place for a full treatment, but we must explain our choice. If one is primarily interested in formal properties of the stable homotopy category, one can modernize Boardman’s original construction
18
18

18

M. J. Boardman. Unpublished 1964 thesis.
of that category, for example by using Lurie’s
\infty
-categories. That in effect ignores the point-set level precision that is our focus and eliminates all hope of using classical adjunctions as in our
Section 1.1
. One might next try to use one’s favorite category of spectra that is symmetric monoidal under the smash product. There are several good choices, such as symmetric spectra, orthogonal spectra, and EKMM
S
-modules
[
MMSS01
,
EKMM97
]
, and there are comparisons among them showing that each gives rise to a category equivalent to Boardman’s original stable homotopy category. But there is a conundrum. By a result of Lewis
[
Lew91
]
(or see
[
May09b
, Theorem 11.1]
), any such choice is incompatible with an adjunction
(\Sigma^{\infty},\Omega^{\infty})
such that the unit for the smash product is the sphere spectrum
S=\Sigma^{\infty}S^{0}
.

The precursor
[
LMS86
]
of
[
EKMM97
]
gives the category
\scr{S}
of spectra that we shall use. As said before, it is the only choice we know of in which the achingly elementary
Section 1.1
makes sense. It is not symmetric monoidal in the usual sense, but it is the term
\scr{S}_{1}
of a symmetric monoidal graded category of spectra.
19
19

19

A paper in progress by Bryce Goldman gives a full treatment of this old idea.

The smash product in every known symmetric monoidal category of spectra is obtained by suitably internalizing an “external smash product”
\scr{S}_{1}\times\scr{S}_{1}\longrightarrow\scr{S}_{2}
of a symmetric monoidal graded category of spectra. A first formalization of this point of view is in
[
LMS86
]
. We shall find in Part 3 that the operadic internalization developed in
[
LMS86
, Chapter VIII]
is perfectly suited for the multiplicative elaboration of the present theory.

We here recall the categories of (LMS) prespectra and spectra from
[
LMS86
]
and exhibit the adjunction
(\Sigma^{\infty},\Omega^{\infty})
. We let
U=\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
with its standard inner product. Define an indexing space to be a finite dimensional subspace of
U
with the induced inner product. A (coordinate free) prespectrum
T
consists of based spaces
TV
and based maps

\sigma\colon\Sigma^{W-V}TV\longrightarrow TW

with adjoints

\tilde{\sigma}\colon TV\longrightarrow\Omega^{W-V}TW

for
V\subset W
. Here
W-V
is the orthogonal complement of
V
in
W
and
S^{W-V}
is its one point compactification;

\tilde{\sigma}
must be the identity when
V=W
, and the obvious transitivity condition must hold when
V\subset W\subset Z
. A prespectrum
T
is an inclusion prespectrum if each map
\tilde{\sigma}
is an inclusion. It is a spectrum if each map
\tilde{\sigma}
is a homeomorphism. We then usually write
E
rather than
T
.

A map
f\colon T\longrightarrow T^{\prime}
of prespectra consists of based maps
f_{V}\colon TV\longrightarrow T^{\prime}V
such that the diagram

\textstyle{TV\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\sigma}}
\textstyle{T^{\prime}V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\sigma}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{W-V}TW\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega^{W-V}f_{W}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{W-V}T^{\prime}W}

commutes when
V\subset W
. We let
\scr{P}
and
\scr{S}
denote the category of prespectra and its full subcategory of spectra. The inclusion
\ell\colon\scr{S}\longrightarrow\scr{P}
has a left adjoint spectrification functor
L\colon\scr{P}\longrightarrow\scr{S}

[
LMS86
, Theorem I.2.2]
. When
T
is an inclusion prespectrum,

(LT)(V)=\operatorname{colim}_{V\subset W}\Omega^{W-V}TW,

where the colimit is taken over the maps

\Omega^{W-V}\tilde{\sigma}\colon\Omega^{W-V}TW\longrightarrow\Omega^{W-V}\Omega^{Z-W}TZ\cong\Omega^{Z-V}TZ

This makes sense since, for
V\subset W
,

LT(V)\cong\operatorname{colim}_{W\subset Z}\Omega^{W-V}\Omega^{Z-W}TZ\cong\Omega^{W-V}\operatorname{colim}_{W\subset Z}\Omega^{Z-W}TZ=\Omega^{W-V}TW.

We may restrict attention to any cofinal set of indexing spaces
\scr{V}
in
U
;
we require
0
to be in
\scr{V}
and we require the union of the
V
in
\scr{V}

to be all of
U
. Up to isomorphism, the category
\scr{S}
is independent of the
choice of
\scr{V}
. The default is
\scr{V}=\scr{A}\ell\ell
. We can define
prespectra and spectra in the same way in any countably infinite dimensional real inner product space
U
.
The default is
U=\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
.

For a based space
X
, we have an obvious suspension prespectrum
\{\Sigma^{V}X\}
with
V^{th}
space
\Sigma^{V}X
. The maps
\sigma
are the evident identifications
\Sigma^{W-V}\Sigma^{V}X\cong\Sigma^{W}X
, and their adjoints
\tilde{\sigma}
are inclusions. We define

\Sigma^{\infty}X=L\{\Sigma^{V}X\}.

More explicitly, define

QX=\operatorname{colim}\Omega^{V}\Sigma^{V}X,

where the colimit runs over the maps

\Omega^{V}\tilde{\sigma}\colon\Omega^{V}\Sigma^{V}X\longrightarrow\Omega^{V}\Omega^{W-V}\Sigma^{W-V}\Sigma^{V}X\cong\Omega^{W}\Sigma^{W}X.

Then the
V
th space of
\Sigma^{\infty}X
is
Q\Sigma^{V}X
. Let
\eta\colon X\longrightarrow QX
be the natural inclusion.

For a spectrum
E
, we define
\Omega^{\infty}E=E(0)
; we usually write it as
E_{0}
. The functors
\Sigma^{\infty}

and
\Omega^{\infty}
are adjoint;
QX
is
\Omega^{\infty}\Sigma^{\infty}X
, and
\eta

is the unit of the adjunction. The counit
\varepsilon\colon\Sigma^{\infty}\Omega^{\infty}E\longrightarrow E
is adjoint to the map of prespectra

\{\Sigma^{V}E_{0}\}\longrightarrow\ell E
which at level
V
is
\sigma\colon\Sigma^{V}E_{0}\longrightarrow EV
. We sometimes write

\Gamma^{\infty}=\Omega^{\infty}\Sigma^{\infty}
for the associated monad.

Definition 4.52
.

Spectra have homotopy groups, and a map of spectra is a weak equivalence if it induces an isomorphism on homotopy groups.

For
Section 1.1
, we recall the following definition.

Definition 4.53
.

An operad
\scr{C}
of spaces is an
E_{\infty}
operad if each
\scr{C}(n)
is
\Sigma_{n}
-free and contractible. A space with an action of some
E_{\infty}
-operad is an
E_{\infty}
space.

We take
\mathbb{C}
to be the monad associated to the infinite Steiner operad
\scr{K}_{\infty}
, as in
[
May09b
, Section 3]
or
[
GM17
]
. It is an
E_{\infty}
operad. By passage to colimits from the actions of the Steiner operads
\scr{K}_{V}
on
V
-fold loop spaces,
\scr{K}_{\infty}
acts naturally on the infinite loop spaces
E_{0}=\Omega^{\infty}E
. We could equally well take
\mathbb{C}
to be the monad associated to any
E_{\infty}
operad that acts naturally on infinite loop spaces, such as the product of any
E_{\infty}
operad with
\scr{K}_{\infty}
. This puts us in the context of
Section 1.1
.

It is very easy to generalize the context just established to
G
-spectra for a finite group
G
. Everything said so far applies almost verbatim. We replace
\scr{T}
by
G\scr{T}
, so replace spaces and maps by
G
-spaces and
G
-maps, and we replace
U
by a
G
-universe
U_{G}
. This means that
U_{G}
is the sum of countably many copies of each of a chosen set of irreducible representations of
G
; we insist that the trivial representation be in our set. We then take our indexing spaces
V
to be finite dimensional sub
G
-inner product spaces of
U_{G}
.
A universe
U_{G}
is complete if it contains all irreducible representations of
G
. The
G
-spectra are then said to be genuine. A universe
U_{G}=U
is trivial if
G
acts trivially, in which case we just see spectra with
G
-actions. Such
G
-spectra are said to be classical, or naive. We write
G\scr{S}
for the category of genuine
G
-spectra indexed on a complete universe
U_{G}
and the
G
-maps of
G
-spectra.

The trivial universe
U
is a sub-universe of any other universe
U_{G}
. We define the
H
-fixed point spectrum of a genuine
G
-spectrum
E
by first restricting
E
to the subuniverse
U
and then taking
H
-fixed points levelwise. That is,

E^{H}(V)=E(V)^{H}

where
G
acts trivially on
V
. Homotopical properties of
G\scr{S}
are inherited from those of
\scr{S}
by passage to fixed points.

Definition 4.54
.

A map
f\colon D\longrightarrow E
of
G
-spectra is a weak equivalence if each
f^{H}\colon D^{H}\longrightarrow E^{H}
is a weak equivalence of spectra.

We use the following definition to define
E_{\infty}
operads of
G
-spaces.

Definition 4.55
.

Let
\mathbb{F}_{n}
denote the family of all subgroups
\Gamma
of
G\times\Sigma_{n}
such that

\Gamma\cap\Sigma_{n}=\{e\}
. Each such
\Gamma
is the graph
\Gamma_{\alpha}=\{(h,\alpha(h))\,|\,h\in H\}\subset G\times\Sigma_{n}

of some homomorphism
\alpha\colon H\longrightarrow\Sigma_{n}
, where
H\subset G
. Taking
\alpha
to be trivial, we see
that
H\in\mathbb{F}_{n}
for all
n
and all
H\subset G
.

Definition 4.56
.

An operad
\scr{C}
of
G
-spaces is an
E_{\infty}
operad if each
\scr{C}(n)
is
\Sigma_{n}
-free and if
\scr{C}(n)^{\Gamma}
is contractible for all
\Gamma\in\mathbb{F}_{n}
. A
G
-space with an action of some
E_{\infty}
-operad is an
E_{\infty}

G
-space.

With these definitions in place, everything said above works in the same way to place us in the context of
Section 1.1
.

4.6.
From
G
-spaces to genuine
G
-spectra for finite groups
G

We prove analogs of Theorems
4.12
and
4.44
for spectra and
G
-spectra here. We view the nonequivariant case as the case when
G
is the trivial group. We follow the modernized sketch of the recognition principle that is given non-equivariantly in
[
May09b
, Section 9]
. That is made equivariant and compared with the orthogonal
G
-spectrum machine in
[
GM17
, Sections 2.3 and 2.4]
. We restrict to a complete universe and delete it from the notation. We take
\scr{T}
in the general theory to be the category
G\scr{T}
of based
G
-spaces.
20
20

20

The caveat in
Remark 1.2
concerning non-degenerate basepoints applies.
We take
\scr{S}
to be the category
G\scr{S}
of (genuine)
G
-spectra and take
(\Sigma,\Omega)
to be
(\Sigma^{\infty},\Omega^{\infty})
.

We have placed ourselves in the context of
Section 1.1
in the previous subsection. With the weak equivalences defined there, we have
Section 2.1
. In fact, by
[
EKMM97
, Theorem VII.4.4]
,
G\scr{S}
is a model category, and it is again clear that
(\Sigma^{\infty},\Omega^{\infty})
is a Quillen adjunction since
\Omega^{\infty}
preserves fibrations and acyclic fibrations. Just as for spaces and
G
-spaces, all
G
-spectra are fibrant objects. Model categories with this property are often especially convenient since there is no need to keep track of how fibrant replacement behaves with respect to constructions of interest. This feature holds for our
G
-spectra and can be viewed as the reason that
\Omega^{\infty}
behaves well both formally and homotopically.
This feature does not hold for symmetric or orthogonal
G
-spectra.

Nonequivariantly, we define a spectrum to be
\Omega^{\infty}
-connective if it is connective in the usual sense that its homotopy groups in negative degrees are zero. Similarly, we define a
G
-spectrum to be
\Omega^{\infty}
-connective, abbreviated to connective, if each of its fixed point spectra is connective. Then
Section 2.1
holds.

We define group completion as in the
G
-space example. The definition now is for
E_{\infty}

G
-spaces and is the same as for
E_{V}
-
G
-spaces: it is given by homological group completion on
H
-fixed point spaces for
H\subset G
.
From here it is implicit or explicit in
[
GM17
]
that Assumptions
2.2
,
2.3
and
2.4
hold.

Theorem 4.57
.

For
E_{\infty}

G
-spaces
Y
, the unit
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}
induces a group completion and therefore a
\mathbb{C}
-group completion

Y\longrightarrow\Omega^{\infty}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{E}Y\cong\Omega^{\infty}_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma^{\infty}_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{Y},

and any connective
G
-spectrum
E
is equivalent to
\Sigma^{\infty}_{\mathbb{C}}\Omega^{\infty}_{\mathbb{C}}E
.

Remark 4.58
.

The theory here adapts without change for a general
G
-universe
U_{G}
, giving an analog of
Theorem 4.57
, provided that we take
\mathbb{C}
to be the monad associated to the Steiner operad
\scr{C}
for the universe
U_{G}
.

Remark 4.59
.

The discussion of
G
-spectra in
Section 4.5
summarizes the restriction to finite groups
G
of the theory of
G
-spectra developed for general compact Lie groups in
[
LMS86
]
. Just as in
Remark 4.45
,
Theorem 4.57
generalizes to compact Lie groups
G
provided that we restrict attention to representations
V
of
G
whose isotropy groups have finite index in
G
. Here we use a universe defined using only such representations and we use a Steiner operad
\scr{C}
for a compact Lie group
G
that is the colimit of Steiner operads
\scr{C}_{V}
for such
V
. Details are in
[
MMO
, Section 9.3]
.

Remark 4.60
.

For any topological group
G
,
21
21

21

Again, we require
e\in G
to be a non-degenerate basepoint to avoid pathology.
we can use the trivial
G
-universe and a non-equivariant
E_{\infty}
operad, viewed as
G
-trivial, to obtain the analog of
Theorem 4.57
for classical (alias naive)
G
-spectra. These are just spectra, as defined nonequivariantly, with actions of
G
on component spaces and equivariant structure maps such that.
E_{n}\longrightarrow\Omega E_{n+1}
is a
G
-homeomorphism. All of our assumptions hold. The approximation theorem is obtained from its nonequivariant version by passage to fixed points.

Part II

Composite adjunctions

5.
The general theory of composite adjunctions

5.1.
The categorical context and Assumptions A, B, and C

We start with a given instance of the context described in
Section 1.1
. Thus we have an adjunction

(\Sigma,\Omega)
between categories
\scr{T}
and
\scr{S}
and a monad
\mathbb{C}
on
\scr{T}
. We have the formal consequences described in

Section 1
. We assume the homotopical Assumptions B through F of
Section 2
, and we have the conclusions of
Section 3
.

Assumption A
com
.
We assume given a second adjunction

(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R})
from a third cocomplete category
\scr{V}
to
\scr{T}
and we assume that
\varepsilon\colon\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Id}
is an isomorphism. We also assume given a monad
\mathbb{D}
on

\scr{V}
together with a natural isomorphism
\omega\colon\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}
of functors
\scr{T}\longrightarrow\scr{V}
that is compatible with the units and products,
\eta
and
\mu
, of
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
in the sense that the following diagrams commute.
22
22

22

In the language of
[
May09b
, Definition 14.1]
, these diagrams say that
(\mathbb{R},\omega)
is a lax map of monads
\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}
.

\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta\mathbb{R}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}\eta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}}

\textstyle{\mathbb{D}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{D}\omega}
\scriptstyle{\mu\mathbb{R}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega\mathbb{C}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}\mu}
\textstyle{\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}}

Remark 5.1
.

The condition that
\varepsilon\colon\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Id}
is an isomorphism plays an important role in our main examples. We say we have a weak composite adjunction context when
\varepsilon
is only a weak equivalence. The isomorphism condition is familiar categorically. It means that
\scr{T}
is a reflective subcategory of
\scr{V}
. In particular,
\mathbb{R}
is full and faithful, the categorical monad
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}
is idempotent (its
\mu
is an isomorphism), and the adjunction is monadic. Intuitively,
\mathbb{D}
-algebras in
\scr{V}
are more general than
\mathbb{C}
-algebras in
\scr{T}
, so that a recognition principle for the new context is more general than the one for the original context. Nevertheless, in our examples, we will show how to transform the more general context into the original one. See
Remark 5.20
.

We give a categorical elaboration of
Section 5.1
in
Section 5.2
. We answer the following natural question here and in
Section 5.4
, and we then summarize our conclusions in
Section 5.5
. We show in
Section 5.6
how an example of our (weak) composite adjunction context applies to transform topological examples of
Section 1.1
to simplicial ones.

Question 5.2
.

Assuming that
(\Sigma,\Omega)
and
\mathbb{C}
satisfy Assumptions A through F, when do
(\Sigma\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R}\Omega)
and
\mathbb{D}
also satisfy them?

The following diagram gives the picture. It is a composite analog of
(1.12)
.

(5.3)

\textstyle{\scr{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{L}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{D}}}
\textstyle{\scr{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\scr{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{(\mathbb{R}\Omega)_{\mathbb{D}}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathbb{D}[\scr{V}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{L}}
\scriptstyle{(\Sigma\mathbb{L})_{\mathbb{D}}}

We emphasize that the dotted arrow
\mathbb{L}
usually does not exist, but our context makes sense nevertheless. Its companion arrow
\mathbb{R}
does always exist since we shall see in (i) of
Proposition 5.11
that
\mathbb{R}
takes
\mathbb{C}
-algebras in
\scr{T}
to
\mathbb{D}
-algebras in
\scr{V}
. Therefore the arrow
(\mathbb{R}\Omega)_{\mathbb{D}}=\mathbb{R}\circ\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}
exists. This proves the following result.

Lemma 5.4
.

Section 1.1
holds with
(\Sigma,\Omega)
and
\mathbb{C}
replaced by
(\Sigma\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R}\Omega)
and
\mathbb{D}
.

We call this the composite adjunction context.
We have maps
\alpha
and
\beta
as in
Section 1.2
for this context, hence
Definition 1.20
and
Proposition 1.3
construct the left adjoint, denoted
(\Sigma\mathbb{L})_{\mathbb{D}}
, of
(\mathbb{R}\Omega)_{\mathbb{D}}
.

Remark 5.5
.

When the dotted arrow left adjoint
\mathbb{L}
to
\mathbb{R}
exists, the curved arrows might as well be erased since we then have both

(\mathbb{R}\Omega)_{\mathbb{D}}=\mathbb{R}\circ\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\ \ \text{and}\ \ (\Sigma\mathbb{L})_{\mathbb{D}}=\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}\circ\mathbb{L}.

We require the following analog of
Section 2.1
.

Assumption B
com
.
We assume that
\scr{V}
has a class of weak equivalences satisfying the two out of three property, and we say that a map
f
in
\mathbb{D}[\scr{V}]
is a weak equivalence if
\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{D}}f
is a weak equivalence in
\scr{V}
. We assume that a map
f
in
\scr{T}
is a weak equivalence if and only if
\mathbb{R}f
is a weak equivalence in
\scr{V}
and that the functor
\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{D}}
(hence also
\mathbb{D}
) preserves weak equivalences, at least under restriction to good objects as in
Remark 1.2
.

Remark 5.6
.

In contrast to
Section 2.1
, where
\Sigma
was assumed to preserve weak equivalences, we do not assume that the functor
\mathbb{L}
preserves weak equivalences. It will do so in the categories of operators context. It will not do so in the orbital presheaf context, but it will do so there on restriction to cofibrant objects in view of
Lemma 5.14
and
Proposition 7.2
.

Lemma 5.7
.

By composition,
Section 5.1
together with
Section 2.1
for the adjunction
(\Sigma,\Omega)
and monad
\mathbb{C}
ensures that
Section 2.1
holds for the composite adjunction
(\Sigma\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R}\Omega)
and the monad
\mathbb{D}
, except that
\Sigma\mathbb{L}
need not preserve weak equivalences in general.

Lemma 5.8
.

Define the
\mathbb{R}\Omega
-connective objects in
\scr{S}
to be the
\Omega
-connective objects. Then
Section 5.1
together with
Section 2.1
for the adjunction
(\Sigma,\Omega)
ensures that
Section 2.1
holds for our composite adjunction
(\Sigma\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R}\Omega)
.

We will come back to the remaining Assumptions in
Section 5.4
.

5.2.
Relations between monads
\mathbb{C}
in
\scr{T}
and
\mathbb{D}
in
\scr{V}

We ignore
(\Sigma,\Omega)
in this subsection but assume given the adjunction
(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R})
such that
\varepsilon\colon\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Id}
is an isomorphism. We relate monadic data in
\scr{T}
and
\scr{V}
, assuming throughout that we are given monads
\mathbb{C}
on
\scr{T}
and
\mathbb{D}
on
\scr{V}
together with an isomorphism
\omega\colon\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}
that satisfies the compatibility diagrams of
Section 5.1
.

In the special case of categories of operators, results like these were first observed in
[
MT78
, §6]
and were later elaborated in
[
May09a
, Appendix A]
, but our statements here are adapted to our present general context. The proofs are formal inspections of definitions and straightforward diagram chases. These results are purely categorical and have nothing to do with group completion or the approximation theorem. Note that part (ii) is in part a specialization of
Lemma 4.23
, but now using that
\varepsilon
is an isomorphism.

Proposition 5.9
.

The following conclusions relate the monads
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
.

(i)

The functor
\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}
on
\scr{T}
is naturally isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}
via the composite

\textstyle{\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{L}\omega}
\textstyle{\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon\mathbb{C}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}}

and therefore inherits a monad structure from that of
\mathbb{C}
. Moreover, under this isomorphism, the following compatibility diagrams commute.

\textstyle{\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{L}\eta\mathbb{R}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Id}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}}

\textstyle{\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{L}\mu\mathbb{R}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\eta\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}}

(ii)

The functor
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}
on
\scr{V}
is a monad with product and unit induced from those
of
\mathbb{C}
via the composites

\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\varepsilon}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}\mu\mathbb{L}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}}

and

\textstyle{\operatorname{Id}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}\eta\mathbb{L}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}.}

Moreover, the isomorphism
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\varepsilon\colon\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}
is compatible with the units and products of

\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}
and
\mathbb{C}
in the sense specified in
Section 5.1
.

(iii)

The composite

\textstyle{\iota:\mathbb{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{D}\eta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega\mathbb{L}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}}

is a morphism of monads in
\scr{V}
.

Remark 5.10
.

This result says in particular that the “conjugate monad”
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}
is the terminal monad in
\scr{V}
such that
\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}
is compatibly isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}
. There usually are other such monads that are more relevant to the applications. In the category of operators context, others have long been known and are standard. In the orbital presheaf context, others are difficult to construct. In the sequel
[
KMZ24
]
, we will rework a construction of Costenoble and Waner
[
CW91
]
to obtain such
\mathbb{D}
in that case. Those
\mathbb{D}
are the examples that lead to applications.

Proposition 5.11
.

The following conclusions relate
\mathbb{C}
-algebras to
\mathbb{D}
-algebras. Let
X
be in
\scr{T}
and
Y
be in
\scr{V}
.

(i)

If
X
is a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra with action
\theta\colon\mathbb{C}X\longrightarrow X
, then
\mathbb{R}X
is a
\mathbb{D}
-algebra with action
the composite

\textstyle{\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}\theta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}X}

and
\varepsilon\colon\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}X\longrightarrow X
is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras.

(ii)

If
\mathbb{R}X
is a
\mathbb{D}
-algebra with action
\psi\colon\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}X\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}X
, then
X
is a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra with action

\mathbb{L}\psi\colon\mathbb{C}X\cong\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}X\longrightarrow\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}X\cong X

and the action
\psi
factors as the composite

\textstyle{\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}X\cong\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\psi}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}X.}

(iii)

If
\mathbb{D}\cong\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}
and
Y
is a
\mathbb{D}
-algebra with action
\theta\colon\mathbb{D}Y\longrightarrow Y
, then
\mathbb{L}Y
is a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra with action

\mathbb{L}\theta\colon\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}Y\cong\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{L}Y.

Therefore the dotted arrow
\mathbb{L}
in
(5.3)
exists when
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}
.

Proposition 5.12
.

If
(F,\lambda)
is a
\mathbb{C}
-functor in some category
\scr{Z}
, then
F\mathbb{L}\colon\scr{V}\longrightarrow\scr{Z}
is an

\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}
-functor in
\scr{Z}
with action the composite

\textstyle{F\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F\varepsilon\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}}
\textstyle{F\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda\mathbb{L}}
\textstyle{F\mathbb{L}.}

Therefore, by pullback,
F\mathbb{L}
is a
\mathbb{D}
-functor in
\scr{Z}
with action the composite

\textstyle{F\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\eta}
\textstyle{F\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F\mathbb{L}\omega\mathbb{L}}
\textstyle{F\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F\varepsilon\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}}
\textstyle{F\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda\mathbb{L}}
\textstyle{F\mathbb{L}.}

5.3.
Special and strictly special objects of
\scr{V}

We will see in
Remark 6.55
that the first part of the following definition is standard in the context of categories of operators. The second part will play a central role in both that context and the context of orbital presheaves.

Definition 5.13
.

We say that an object
Y
of
\scr{V}
is
special
if the unit

\eta\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}Y
is a weak equivalence, and we let
\scr{V}_{s}
be the full subcategory of special objects of
\scr{V}
.
We say that
Y
is
strictly special
if
\eta
is an isomorphism, and we let
\scr{V}_{ss}
be the full subcategory of strictly special objects of
\scr{V}
.

Section 5.1
and the naturality of
\eta
imply the following conclusion.

Lemma 5.14
.

The functor
\mathbb{L}
preserves weak equivalences when restricted to strictly special objects of
\scr{V}
.

The condition that
\varepsilon\colon\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Id}
is an isomorphism has the following implication.

Lemma 5.15
.

The functor
\mathbb{R}
takes objects of
\scr{T}
to strictly special objects of
\scr{V}
. Therefore

(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R})
restricts to an adjoint equivalence relating
\scr{V}_{ss}
to
\scr{T}
.

Proof.

By the triangle identity, the following composite is the identity for
X\in\scr{T}
.

\textstyle{\mathbb{R}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}X}

Therefore
\eta\colon\mathbb{R}X\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}X
is an isomorphism.
∎

Remark 5.16
.

In our examples, we will show, non-trivially, that it suffices to focus on strictly special objects of
\scr{V}
. Then
Corollary 5.18
below, which is implied by the following lemma and Propositions
5.9
and

5.11
, always justifies taking
\mathbb{D}
to be
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}
when applying the machine. This holds even though the examples of interest come from actions of quite different monads
\mathbb{D}
, as pointed out in

Remark 5.10
.

Lemma 5.17
.

If
Y\in\scr{V}
is strictly special, then
\mathbb{D}Y
is strictly special.

Proof.

Consider the following commutative naturality diagram.

\textstyle{\mathbb{D}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{D}\eta}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\eta}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}Y}

By the triangle identity, the rightmost vertical arrow
\eta
is an isomorphism with inverse
\mathbb{R}\varepsilon
. Therefore the other vertical arrows
\eta
are also isomorphisms.
∎

Restricting to strictly special objects, we can fill in the dotted arrow
\mathbb{L}
of
(5.3)
, and it then preserves weak equivalences by
Lemma 5.14
.

Corollary 5.18
.

Restricted to strictly special objects, the monads
\mathbb{D}
and
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}
are isomorphic and take values in strictly special objects. The adjoint equivalence
(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R})
between

\scr{V}_{ss}
and
\scr{T}
restricts to an adjoint equivalence between
\mathbb{D}[\scr{V}_{ss}]
and
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
.

We conclude this section by describing a natural equivalence between the homotopy categories of
\scr{V}_{s}
and
\scr{V}_{ss}
, assuming some of
Section 5.4
below. This result is discussed in more detail for categories of operators, which is where we will use it, in
[
May09a
]
, so we will be brief. Observe that, by
Proposition 5.12
,
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}
is a
\mathbb{D}
-functor with values in
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
.

Proposition 5.19
.

For a
\mathbb{D}
-algebra
Y
, define
\overline{Y}_{\mathbb{C}}
to be the strictly special
\mathbb{D}
-algebra

\overline{Y}_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathbb{R}B(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L},\mathbb{D},Y).

If
Y
is special, then
Y
is naturally weakly equivalent as a
\mathbb{D}
-algebra to
\overline{Y}_{\mathbb{C}}
. For a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
X
,
\mathbb{R}X
is weakly equivalent as a
\mathbb{D}
-algebra to
\overline{\mathbb{R}X}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

Define
\overline{\eta}
to be either of the equal composites in the following diagram.

\textstyle{\mathbb{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{D}\eta}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\eta}

We then have the following natural maps.

\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{B(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{D},Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\scriptstyle{B(\overline{\eta},\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})}
\textstyle{B(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L},\mathbb{D},Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{\mathbb{R}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}B(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L},\mathbb{D},Y)=\overline{Y}_{\mathbb{C}}}

Here
\gamma_{\mathbb{R}}
is defined in
Definition 5.26
and discussed in
Remark 5.28
. In the context of categories of operators,
\gamma_{\mathbb{R}}
is a natural isomorphism because realization commutes with products. As usual,
\zeta
is an equivalence. When
Y
is special, we can deduce that
\overline{\eta}
is a weak equivalence; the diagrams in the proof of
Lemma 5.17
are relevant. Then
B(\overline{\eta},\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})
is a weak equivalence by
Section 5.4
. The weak equivalence of the last statement is the composite

\textstyle{\overline{\mathbb{R}X}_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathbb{R}B(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L},\mathbb{D},\mathbb{R}X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}B(\operatorname{id},\overline{\eta},\operatorname{id})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}B(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R}X)\cong\mathbb{R}B(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C},X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}X.}

The isomorphism is a specialization of the easy
Corollary 5.35
below.
∎

Remark 5.20
.

We can apply the equivalences of
Proposition 5.19
to transform
(5.3)
to a diagram in which the category
\scr{V}
is replaced by
\scr{V}_{ss}
. In the transformed diagram
\mathbb{L}
exists and preserves weak equivalences. Then the units of both pairs
(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R})
are given by weak equivalences. In effect, up to natural weak equivalence, the transformed diagram entirely reduces the composite adjunction context to the original context of the small right triangle. However, we do not know how to effect the corresponding reduction in the richer multiplicative context of
Section 9
.

5.4.
Assumptions D, E, and F in the composite adjunction context

We shall be a little informal here since the verifications differ in ways that we shall indicate briefly but that will become clearer when we explain the relevant concrete specializations of our composite adjunction context. The framework is the same in all contexts, but the specifics of group completion and realization vary. The approximation theorem,
Section 2.3
, is the main point, and that works the same way in all contexts.

Our discussion of Assumptions
2.2
and
2.3
focuses on the following diagram. Write
\alpha_{com}
and

\vartheta_{com}
for the maps
\alpha
and
\vartheta
in the composite adjunction context. The following diagram describes them in terms of
\alpha
and
\vartheta
in our original context of
Section 1.1
. We write
\eta
generically for the units of both adjunctions and monads.

(5.21)

\textstyle{\mathbb{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{D}\eta}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{D}\eta}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{com}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}\Omega\Sigma\mathbb{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\Omega\Sigma\mathbb{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}\alpha}

\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}\vartheta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\Omega\Sigma\mathbb{L}}

The upper left square applies
\mathbb{D}
to the unit of a composite adjunction, the lower left square is a naturality diagram, and the right rectangle exhibits the definition of
\vartheta_{com}
in terms of
\vartheta
.

When applied to special objects of
\scr{V}
, the top left vertical arrow
\mathbb{D}\eta
is a weak equivalence, and it is an isomorphism when applied to strictly special objects. Due to the appearance of
\mathbb{D}\eta
in the diagram, we can only expect our recognition principle and characterization theorems to hold when we restrict attention to special objects. This restriction also effects our discussion of group completions.

Remark 5.22
.

In our examples, the objects of
\scr{V}
are functors
\scr{J}\longrightarrow\scr{W}
for some small domain category
\scr{J}
and some homotopical target category
\scr{W}
. In
Section 6
,
\scr{W}
is the category
\scr{T}
of our original adjunction. However, in
Section 7
, our original
\scr{T}
is based
G
-spaces for a finite group
G
, whereas
\scr{W}
is based spaces. Therefore, even though both examples fit perfectly into the general framework here, they are very different in practice. In the former, interest focuses on special objects. In the latter, interest focuses on non-special objects, but in
Proposition 7.2
we shall show model theoretically that a cellular approximation functor
\Gamma
can be used to approximate all objects by strictly special objects. Nothing like that holds in the categories of operators context; see
Remark 7.1
.

Definition 5.23
.

Suppose that the objects of
\scr{V}
are functors
\scr{J}\longrightarrow\scr{W}
and that grouplike objects of
\scr{W}
and group completions in
\scr{W}
have been defined using subcategories
\scr{H}
and
\scr{H}_{gp}
of
\scr{W}
, as in
Definition 2.5
. We transfer these notions to
\scr{V}
levelwise. We define
\scr{H}
and
\scr{H}_{gp}
in
\scr{V}
to be the subcategories of functors
Y
such that each
Y(j)
is in
\scr{H}
or is in

\scr{H}_{gp}
. We say that a functor
Z
in
\scr{V}
is grouplike if each
Z(j)
is grouplike and we say that a natural transformation
f\colon Y\longrightarrow Z
is a group completion if each
f\colon Y(j)\longrightarrow Z(j)
is a group completion. With these definitions, it is immediate in our examples that
\mathbb{R}\colon\scr{T}\longrightarrow\scr{V}_{s}
preserves grouplike objects and group completions.

It is also immediate in our examples that the forgetful functor
\mathbb{D}[\scr{V}]\longrightarrow\scr{V}
takes (levelwise) values in
\scr{H}
. Assume that
\scr{W}
has a group completion functor
(\mathbb{G},g)
as in
Definition 2.7
. In the categories of operators context of
Section 6
, where
\scr{W}=\scr{T}
is the target of
\mathbb{L}
, we restrict to special objects and define
(\mathbb{G}_{\scr{V}_{s}},g_{\scr{V}_{s}})
by setting

\mathbb{G}_{\scr{V}_{s}}=\mathbb{R}\mathbb{G}\mathbb{L}\colon\mathbb{D}[\scr{V}_{s}]\longrightarrow\scr{H}_{gp}

and letting
g_{\scr{V}_{s}}\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{\scr{V}_{s}}Y
be the composite

\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}g\mathbb{L}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{G}\mathbb{L}Y.}

In the orbital presheaves context of
Section 7
, where
\scr{W}
is not the target
\scr{T}
of
\mathbb{L}
, we restrict to strictly special objects and define
(\mathbb{G}_{\scr{V}_{ss}},g_{\scr{V}_{ss}})
by setting

(\mathbb{G}_{\scr{V}_{ss}}Y)(j)=\mathbb{G}(Y(j))

and letting
g_{\scr{V}_{ss}}\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{\scr{V}_{ss}}Y
be given levelwise by
g\colon Y(j)\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}Y(j)
for
j\in\scr{J}
.

Lemma 5.24
.

With the definitions just given,
Section 2.2
for our composite adjunction
(\mathbb{R}\Omega,\Sigma\mathbb{L})
follows from
Section 2.2
for our original adjunction
(\Sigma,\Omega)
in both the categories of operators and orbital presheaves contexts, provided we restrict to the category
\scr{V}_{s}
in the former case and to the category
\scr{V}_{ss}
in the latter.

Sketch.

We see that
(\mathbb{R}\Omega)_{\scr{D}}
takes values in
\scr{V}_{ss}
by
Lemma 5.15
. It takes grouplike values since
\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}
takes grouplike values in
\scr{T}
and
\mathbb{R}
takes grouplike objects in
\scr{T}
to grouplike objects in
\scr{V}
.
∎

Lemma 5.25
.

On special objects,
Section 2.3
(the approximation theorem) holds for the composite adjunction
(\mathbb{D}\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R}\Omega)
and the monad
\mathbb{D}
.

Proof.

This follows directly from
Section 2.3
for our original adjunction
(\Sigma,\Omega)
and the monad
\mathbb{C}
via the diagram
(5.21)
. The implication uses that
\mathbb{R}
preserves grouplike objects and group completions, as follows directly from the definitions in the contexts to which we shall specialize.
∎

Finally, we consider
Section 2.4
. In contexts such as those of
Remark 5.22
, we define realization on
s\scr{V}
levelwise and see that the various parts of
Section 2.4
for our composite adjunction mostly follow directly from their analogs for our original adjunction
(\Sigma,\Omega)
. But we state the requirement for (i)-(iv) formally.

Assumption F
com
.
We assume that parts (i) through (iii) of
Section 2.4
hold with
\scr{T}
replaced by
\scr{V}
and that, replacing (iv), the functors
\mathbb{L}\colon\scr{V}\longrightarrow\scr{T}
and
\mathbb{D}\colon\scr{V}\longrightarrow\scr{V}
commute with realization.

Part (v) only concerns
\scr{S}
, hence it holds by
Section 2.4
. For (vi) and (vii) we use the following definition, lemma, and remark in our main examples. Here we use that

\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}\cong\mathrm{\mathrm{Id}}
.

Definition 5.26
.

Let
J_{*}\in s\scr{T}
. Define
\gamma_{\mathbb{R}}\colon|\mathbb{R}J_{*}|\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}|J_{*}|
to be the adjoint

\textstyle{|\mathbb{R}J_{*}|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}|\mathbb{R}J_{*}|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}\iota}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}|J_{*}|}

of the isomorphism

\textstyle{\iota\colon\mathbb{L}|\mathbb{R}J_{*}|\cong|\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}J_{*}|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{|\varepsilon|}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{|J_{*}|.}

Lemma 5.27
.

If
\gamma_{\mathbb{R}}
is an isomorphism and
J_{*}
is a simplicial
\mathbb{C}
-algebra in
\scr{T}
, then
|\mathbb{R}J_{*}|
is a

\mathbb{D}
-algebra in
\scr{V}_{ss}
such that
\gamma_{\mathbb{R}}
is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{D}
-algebras.

Proof.

By
Lemma 5.15
,
\mathbb{R}
takes values in
\scr{V}_{ss}
. Visibly
\eta\colon|\mathbb{R}J_{*}|\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}|\mathbb{R}J_{*}|
is the composite

\textstyle{|\mathbb{R}J_{*}|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{|\eta|}
\textstyle{|\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}J_{*}|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{\mathbb{R}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}|\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}J_{*}|\cong\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}|\mathbb{R}J_{*}|}

and is therefore an isomorphism. Thus
|\mathbb{R}J_{*}|
is in
\scr{V}_{ss}
. It is a
\mathbb{D}
-algebra such that
\gamma_{\mathbb{R}}
is a map of

\mathbb{D}
-algebras by use of the isomorphism
\mathbb{D}|\mathbb{R}J_{*}|\cong|\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}J_{*}|
.
∎

Remark 5.28
.

In our space level examples in Sections
6
and
7
,
\gamma_{\mathbb{R}}
is an isomorphism for entirely different non-formal reasons. In
Section 6
,
\mathbb{R}
is given by cartesian powers, and realization commutes with products. In
Section 7
,
\mathbb{R}
is given by passage to fixed points, and realization commutes with passage to fixed points for Reedy cofibrant simplicial
G
-spaces.

Lemma 5.29
.

Let
K_{*}\in\scr{S}
and consider the natural map
|\mathbb{R}\Omega K_{*}|\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\Omega|K_{*}|
, namely the composite

(5.30)

\textstyle{|\mathbb{R}\Omega K_{*}|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{\mathbb{R}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}|\Omega K_{*}|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}\gamma}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\Omega|K_{*}|.}

If
\gamma_{\mathbb{R}}
is an isomorphism, then it is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{D}
-algebras in
\scr{V}_{ss}
. The map
\mathbb{R}\gamma
is a map of
\mathbb{D}
-algebras in
\scr{V}
, and it is a weak equivalence if
K_{*}
is levelwise
\Omega
-connective.

Proof.

The first statement is immediate from
Lemma 5.27
. For the second statement,
\gamma
is a map of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras
by (vi) of
Section 2.4
for
(\Sigma,\Omega)
, hence
\mathbb{R}\gamma
is a map of
\mathbb{D}
-algebras by (i) of
Proposition 5.11
;
\mathbb{R}\gamma
is a weak equivalence since
\gamma
is a weak equivalence by (vii) of
Section 2.4
and
\mathbb{R}
preserves weak equivalences.
∎

5.5.
The composite adjunction machine

Replacing the adjunction
(\Sigma,\Omega)
with the composite adjunction
(\Sigma\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R}\Omega)
, Theorems
0.1
and
0.2
specialize schematically as follows. As explained above, we must restrict attention to special objects of
\scr{V}
in the context of categories of operators. We can and will restrict to strictly special objects in the context of orbital presheaves.

Theorem 5.31
.

There is a functor
\mathrm{Bar}\colon\mathbb{D}[\scr{V}]\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}[\scr{V}]
, written
Y\mapsto\overline{Y}
, and a natural equivalence
\zeta\colon\overline{Y}\longrightarrow Y
. If
Y
is special, the unit
\eta_{\mathbb{D}}\colon\overline{Y}\longrightarrow(\mathbb{R}\Omega)_{\mathbb{D}}(\Sigma\mathbb{L})_{\mathbb{D}}\overline{Y}
is a group completion and is therefore an equivalence if
Y
is grouplike.

Theorem 5.32
.

\big((\Sigma\mathbb{L})_{\mathbb{D}},(\mathbb{R}\Omega)_{\mathbb{D}}\big)
induces an adjoint equivalence from the homotopy category of special grouplike
\mathbb{D}
-algebras in
\scr{V}
to the homotopy category of
\Omega
-connective objects of
\scr{S}
.

The idea is illuminated by the following diagram, which is the composite adjunction special case of the diagram

(3.12)
. Here
Y
is in
\mathbb{D}[\scr{V}]
and must be special for the maps in the square to have the properties listed in our Assumptions.

(5.33)

\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\overline{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\scriptstyle{B\alpha_{com}}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{\mathbb{D}}}
\textstyle{B((\mathbb{R}\Omega)_{\mathbb{D}}\Sigma\mathbb{L},\mathbb{D},Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{R}\Omega)_{\mathbb{D}}(\Sigma\mathbb{L})_{\mathbb{D}}\overline{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{R}\Omega)_{\mathbb{D}}B(\Sigma\mathbb{L},\mathbb{D},Y)}

The following result, which starts from the map of monads
\iota
in (iii) of
Proposition 5.9
, shows that, up to homotopy, when considering special objects of
\scr{V}
it is in principle possible to restrict attention to the monad
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}
, rather than using a general monad
\mathbb{D}
satisfying
Section 5.1
, in the results above.

Proposition 5.34
.

Define
\iota^{*}\colon\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}[\scr{V}_{s}]\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}[\scr{V}_{s}]
by pullback of actions along
\iota\colon\mathbb{D}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}
. Then
\iota^{*}
induces a equivalence on passage to homotopy categories.

Proof.

We define a derived inverse
\iota_{*}\colon\mathbb{D}[\scr{V}_{s}]\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}[\scr{V}_{s}]
by

\iota_{*}X=B(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L},\mathbb{D},X)

for
\mathbb{D}
-algebras
X
. Here the right action of
\mathbb{D}
on
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}
is the composite

\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}\iota}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}}

On special objects,
\iota\colon\mathbb{D}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}
is a weak equivalence. The following diagrams display
weak equivalences
\iota^{*}\iota_{*}\simeq\operatorname{id}
and
\iota_{*}\iota^{*}\simeq\operatorname{id}
.

\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{B(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{D},X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\scriptstyle{B(\iota,\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})}
\textstyle{\iota^{*}B(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L},\mathbb{D},X)}

\textstyle{B(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L},\mathbb{D},\iota^{*}Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{B(\operatorname{id},\iota,\operatorname{id})}
\textstyle{B(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L},Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\textstyle{Y}

Here
X
is a special
\mathbb{D}
-algebra and
Y
is a special
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}
-algebra.
∎

Part (iii) of
Proposition 5.9
, together with Propositions
5.11
and
5.12
, also leads to the following comparison of bar constructions. It shows that our bar constructions constructed from
\mathbb{C}
are special cases of those constructed from
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}
.

Corollary 5.35
.

For
\mathbb{C}
-functors
F
and
\mathbb{C}
-algebras
X
,

B(F,\mathbb{C},X)\cong B(F\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R}X)

Proof.

Used iteratively, the isomorphism
\varepsilon\colon\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Id}
induces isomorphisms

F\mathbb{L}(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L})^{q}\mathbb{R}X\longrightarrow F\mathbb{C}^{q}X

on
q
-simplices, and these isomorphisms commute with faces and degeneracies.
∎

Restricting to strictly special objects of
\scr{V}
, we have
\mathbb{D}\cong\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}
by
Corollary 5.18
. Here Propositions
5.11
and
5.12
lead to commutation results relating bar constructions constructed from
\mathbb{C}
to bar constructions constructed from
\mathbb{D}
.

Corollary 5.36
.

For
\mathbb{C}
-functors
F
and strictly special
\mathbb{D}
-algebras
Y
,

B(F,\mathbb{C},\mathbb{L}Y)\cong B(F\mathbb{L},\mathbb{D},Y)

Proof.

Used iteratively, the isomorphism
\eta\colon\mathbb{D}^{i}Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}^{i}Y
given by
Corollary 5.18

induces isomorphisms

F\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}^{q}Y\longrightarrow F\mathbb{L}(\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L})^{q}Y\cong F(\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R})^{q}\mathbb{L}Y\cong F\mathbb{C}^{q}\mathbb{L}Y

on
q
-simplices, and these isomorphisms commute with faces and degeneracies.
∎

Corollary 5.37
.

If
Y
is a strictly special
\mathbb{D}
-algebra, then

B(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C},\mathbb{L}Y)\cong\mathbb{L}B(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{D},Y)

In particular, for a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
X
,

B(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C},X)\cong B(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C},\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}X)\cong\mathbb{L}B(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{D},\mathbb{R}X)

and therefore, applying
\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbb{R}\overline{X}=\mathbb{R}B(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C},X)\cong B(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{D},\mathbb{R}X)=\overline{\mathbb{R}X}

Proof.

Taking
F=\mathbb{C}
and using the isomorphism

\textstyle{\mathbb{L}\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{L}\mathbb{C}\eta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{L}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathbb{D}\mathbb{L}}

of functors on
\scr{V}_{ss}
, we see the first statement as the specialization

B(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C},\mathbb{L}Y)\cong B(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L},\mathbb{D},Y)\cong B(\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D},\mathbb{D},Y)\cong\mathbb{L}B(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{D},Y)

of
Corollary 5.36
. Since
\mathbb{R}X
is strictly special by
Lemma 5.15
and
\mathbb{L}
commutes with realization,
the second statement follows.
∎

Using this, we show in the rest of this section that the machine starting from
\mathbb{C}
-algebras
X
is equivalent to the machine starting from
\mathbb{D}
-algebras
\mathbb{R}X
. The starting point is the following commutative diagram, which follows from
(5.21)
by an adjoint diagram chase.

(5.38)

\textstyle{\Sigma\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{com}\mathbb{R}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\varepsilon\mathbb{R}}
\textstyle{\Sigma\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\varepsilon}
\textstyle{\Sigma\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\Sigma}

Lemma 5.39
.

The functors
(\Sigma\mathbb{L})_{\mathbb{D}}\mathbb{R}
and
\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}
on
\mathbb{C}
-algebras in
\scr{T}
are isomorphic.

Proof.

This follows from
Proposition 5.11
, the diagram
(5.38)
, and a comparison of coequalizer diagrams.
∎

Proposition 5.40
.

For
\mathbb{C}
-algebras
X
in
\scr{T}
, the unit
\eta\colon\operatorname{Id}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}
induces a natural isomorphism

\tilde{\eta}\colon\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{D}}\mathbb{R}X\longrightarrow\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{C}}X.

Proof.

By (i) of
Proposition 5.9
, the isomorphisms
\varepsilon\colon\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Id}
and
\omega\colon\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}
induce an isomorphism
\mathbb{C}\cong\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}
. Using iterated insertions of
\eta
and
Lemma 5.15
,
we obtain natural isomorphisms

\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}^{q}\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow(\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R})^{q}

and therefore

\Sigma\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}^{q}\mathbb{R}X\longrightarrow\Sigma(\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R})^{q}X\cong\Sigma\mathbb{C}^{q}X.

A check of faces and degeneracies shows that these are the maps on
q
-simplices of a simplicial map. On realization, this map gives the isomorphism

\tilde{\eta}\colon B(\Sigma\mathbb{L},\mathbb{D},\mathbb{R}X)\longrightarrow B(\Sigma,\mathbb{C},X)

We must apply this with
X
replaced by
\overline{X}
, and
Corollary 5.37
gives that
\overline{\mathbb{R}X}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}\overline{X}
.
∎

The following corollary relates to the missing dotted arrow
\mathbb{L}
in
(5.3)
.

Corollary 5.41
.

When restricted to grouplike objects,
\mathbb{R}\colon\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}[\scr{V}]
induces an equivalence
of homotopy categories.

Proof.

By Theorems
0.2
and
5.32
,
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{D}}
induce equivalences from the homotopy categories of grouplike
\mathbb{C}
-algebras and grouplike
\mathbb{D}
-algebras to the homotopy category of connective objects of

\scr{S}
, hence the conclusion is immediate from the previous proposition. In effect,
\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{D}}^{-1}\circ\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{C}}
on our homotopy categories.
∎

5.6.
From a topological machine to a simplicial machine

In this brief section, we illustrate the general theory of composite adjunctions by showing how it works to translate any topological context into a simplicial one. Of course, our general theory could well start in a simplicial context. However, in Part 1, our examples all started with
\scr{T}
being the category of based spaces or based
G
-spaces. We focus on based spaces
\scr{T}
, but with obvious modifications the following discussion also applies with
\scr{T}
replaced by
G\scr{T}
.

We consider the following diagram. The top right triangle is any given instance of
Section 1.1
in which
\scr{T}
figures, and
(T,S)
is the standard geometric realization and total singular complex adjunction between based simplicial sets and based spaces. We take
\mathbb{D}=T\mathbb{C}S
as our monad on based simplicial sets. We do not have the dotted arrow
T
, but by
Proposition 1.3
we do have the left adjoint

(\Sigma T)_{\mathbb{D}}
.

(5.42)

\textstyle{s\mathrm{Set}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{D}}}

\scriptstyle{\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{D}}}
\scriptstyle{T}
\scriptstyle{(\Sigma T)_{\mathbb{D}}}

This is a weak example of
(5.3)
since the counit
\varepsilon\colon TS\longrightarrow\operatorname{Id}
is a weak equivalence but not an isomorphism. The unit
\eta\colon\operatorname{Id}\longrightarrow ST
is also a weak equivalence (by the standard definition of weak equivalences of simplicial sets), so all simplicial sets are special. Here all homotopical assumptions for the composite adjunction follow directly from their counterparts for the given top right triangle. We find that Theorems
5.31
and
5.32
specialize to give the following result.

Theorem 5.43
.

The recognition principle and homotopical monadicity theorems for the adjunction
(\Sigma T,S\Omega)
and the monad
\mathbb{D}
follow directly from the recognition principle and homotopical monadicity theorem for the adjunction
(\Sigma,\Omega)
and the monad
\mathbb{C}
.

6.
The specialization to categories of operators

6.1.
The cast of characters

Categories of operators were introduced in
[
MT78
]
in order to define a common generalization of operadic and Segal style infinite loop space theory.
23
23

23

The notion of an
\infty
-operad
[
Lur17
, 2.1]
is a modern reinterpretation and generalization.

Nonequivariantly,
[
MT78
]
already used that categories of operators have associated monads. The equivariant generalization was worked out in detail in
[
MMO
]
, which uses the Steiner operad to give an explicit concrete proof that the equivariant versions of the operadic and Segalic infinite loop space machines are equivalent. We shall concentrate on what is new and on how previous work fits into our composite adjunction context. What is new is equivariant, so we write things that way, but our framework is already interesting nonequivariantly. Largely following
[
MMO
]
, we begin by introducing the following categorical cast of characters.

(6.1)

\Sigma

\Lambda

\Pi

\scr{F}

\scr{C}

\mathbb{C}

\scr{D}

\mathbb{D}

\Sigma_{G}

\Lambda_{G}

\Pi_{G}

\scr{F}_{G}

\scr{C}_{G}

\mathbb{C}_{G}

\scr{D}_{G}

\mathbb{D}_{G}

The characters
\scr{C}_{G}
and
\mathbb{C}_{G}
are new. They fill a conceptual gap that has long puzzled the senior author.

Definition 6.2
.

We define categories and inclusions
\Sigma\subset\Lambda\subset\Pi\subset\scr{F}
. All have objects the set of based finite sets
\mathbf{n}=\{0,1,\cdots,n\}
for
n\geq 0
, where
\mathbf{n}
has basepoint
0
. All have morphisms given by based functions. These functions are restricted as follows.

(i)

\Sigma
: bijections

(ii)

\Lambda
: injections

(iii)

\Pi
: functions
\phi\colon\mathbf{m}\longrightarrow\mathbf{n}
such that
|\phi^{-1}\{j\}|
is
0
or
1
for
1\leq j\leq n

(iv)

\scr{F}
: all based functions
\mathbf{m}\longrightarrow\mathbf{n}

Note that
\Pi
adds projections to
\Lambda
, since
|\phi^{-1}\{0\}|
is not limited in size in (iii).

We adopt the following from
[
MMO
, Convention 1.4]
.

Convention 6.3
.

For a group
G
and a homomorphism
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow\Sigma_{n}
, define
\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}
to be the based
G
-set specified by letting
G
act on
\mathbf{n}
by
g\cdot i=\alpha(g)(i)
for
1\leq i\leq n
. Conversely, a based
G
-action on
\mathbf{n}
determines a
G
-homomorphism

\alpha
by the same formula. Every based finite
G
-set with
n
non-basepoint elements is isomorphic to one of the form
\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}
for some
\alpha
. We understand based finite
G
-sets to be of this form throughout.

By a
G
-category we understand a category enriched in
G
-sets.

Definition 6.4
.

We define
G
-categories and inclusions
\Sigma_{G}\subset\Lambda_{G}\subset\Pi_{G}\subset\scr{F}_{G}
. All have objects the set of based finite
G
-sets
\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}
for
n\geq 0
and all homomorphisms
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow\Sigma_{n}
. All have morphisms given by based functions, with
G
acting by conjugation. That is, for a based function
f\colon\mathbf{m}^{\alpha}\longrightarrow\mathbf{n}^{\beta}
,

(gf)(i)=\beta(g)f\alpha(g^{-1})(i)\ \ \text{for}\ \ 1\leq i\leq m.

These functions are restricted as follows.

(i)

\Sigma_{G}
: bijections

(ii)

\Lambda_{G}
: injections

(iii)

\Pi_{G}
: functions
\phi\colon\mathbf{m}^{\alpha}\longrightarrow\mathbf{n}^{\beta}
such that

|\phi^{-1}\{j\}|
is
0
or
1
for
1\leq j\leq n

(iv)

\scr{F}_{G}
: all based functions
\mathbf{m}^{\alpha}\longrightarrow\mathbf{n}^{\beta}

Convention 6.5
.

Just as
\Sigma_{n}=\Sigma(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n})
, we define
\Sigma_{\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}}
to be the group
\Sigma(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha},\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})
of automorphisms of
\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}
in the
G
-category
\Sigma_{G}
. It is the group
\Sigma_{n}
equipped with the action of
G
given by conjugation by
\alpha
.

Lemma 6.6
.

Define
\alpha_{c}\colon G\longrightarrow Aut(\Sigma_{n})
to be conjugation by
\alpha
,

\alpha_{c}(g)(\sigma)=\alpha(g)\circ\sigma\circ\alpha(g)^{-1}.

Then
\alpha_{c}
is a homomorphism of groups. The action of
G
on
\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}
extends to an action of the semi-direct product

\Sigma_{n}\rtimes_{\alpha_{c}}G
via

(g,\sigma)(i)=\sigma\big(\alpha(g)(i)\big).

Proof.

For the first statement, it is easily checked that

\alpha_{c}(g)(\sigma\tau)=\alpha_{c}(g)(\sigma)\alpha_{c}(g)(\tau)\ \ \text{and}\ \ \alpha_{c}(gh)=\alpha_{c}(g)\big(\alpha_{c}(h)\big).

Thus each
\alpha_{c}(g)
is an automorphism of
\Sigma_{n}
and
\alpha_{c}
is a group homomorphism. For the second statement, recall that

\Sigma_{n}\rtimes_{\alpha_{c}}G
is the set
\Sigma_{n}\times G
with the product

(\sigma,g)(\tau,h)=(\sigma\alpha_{c}(g)(\tau),gh).

It is easily checked that

\big((\sigma,g)(\tau,h))(i)=(\sigma,g)\big((\tau,h)(i)\big).\qed

Lemma 6.7
.

The categories of
Definition 6.2
embed in the respective categories of
Definition 6.4
by identifying

\mathbf{n}
with
\mathbf{n}^{\varepsilon_{n}}
, where
\varepsilon_{n}\colon G\longrightarrow\Sigma_{n}
is the trivial homomorphism.

Remark 6.8
.

It is clear that the category
\scr{F}
and its subcategories
\Sigma
,
\Lambda
, and
\Pi
are symmetric monoidal under the wedge operation
\mathbf{m}\vee\mathbf{n}\cong\mathbf{m+n}
that identifies
\mathbf{m}
with the first
m
and
\mathbf{n}
with the last
n
positive elements of
\mathbf{m+n}
, in order. Similarly,
\scr{F}_{G}
and its subcategories
\Sigma_{G}
,
\Lambda_{G}
, and
\Pi_{G}
are symmetric monoidal. In fact, they are permutative, meaning that they are symmetric strict monoidal. In the multiplicative theory, we will implicitly use that they are bipermutative
[
May77
, Section 5.3]
24
24

24

The corrections to
[
May77
]
in
[
May82
]
do not affect that section.
with multiplicative product given by the smash product of finite based sets, where the positive degree elements of
\mathbf{mn}
are identified with pairs
(i,j)
with
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n
, ordered lexicographically.

6.2.
Categories of operators
\scr{D}
and
\scr{D}_{G}

In our cast of characters
Section 6.1
,
\scr{C}
is an operad in
G\scr{U}
for some bicomplete cartesian monoidal ground category
\scr{U}
,
\scr{D}
is its associated category of operators over
\scr{F}
and
\scr{D}_{G}
is its associated category of operators over
\scr{F}_{G}
. We recall these notions and their relationships and then introduce the new
G
-operad
\scr{C}_{G}
.
25
25

25

Here we change notation from
[
MMO
]
, which used
\scr{C}_{G}
to denote an operad of
G
-spaces. We will reserve the notation
\scr{C}_{G}
for our new definition.
A bit paradoxically, to understand both the conceptual role of
\scr{C}_{G}
and the details of its definition, it seems best to start with categories of operators. However,
\scr{C}_{G}
will play a much larger role in the sequel
[
KMZ24
]
, where more details will be given.

We must use a bit of enriched category theory in this section, and we use the following notations.

Notation 6.9
.

We say that a category
\scr{M}
enriched in
\scr{V}
is a
\scr{V}
-category, where
\scr{V}
is a given symmetric monoidal category with product
\otimes
and unit object
\mathcal{I}
. We have morphism objects, categorically denoted
\underline{\scr{M}}(X,Y)
, in
\scr{V}
with composition maps

\underline{\scr{M}}(Y,Z)\otimes\underline{\scr{M}}(X,Y)\longrightarrow\underline{\scr{M}}(X,Z)
in
\scr{V}
. The underlying category
\scr{M}
has morphism sets
\scr{M}(X,Y)=\scr{V}(\mathcal{I},\underline{\scr{M}}(X,Y))
. We focus on the cartesian monoidal category
\scr{V}=G\scr{T}
, and then
\scr{M}(X,Y)
generally takes values in
\scr{T}
rather than just in sets. Such “double enrichment” is discussed in
[
GMR19
]
.

Definition 6.10
.

A category of operators
26
26

26

Here and in

.
