Remark 6.12
.

Since
\scr{D}_{G}
is reduced, each
\scr{D}_{G}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{n}^{\beta})
is based with basepoint the component of the unique map

\mathbf{m}^{\alpha}\longrightarrow\mathbf{0}\longrightarrow\mathbf{n}^{\beta}
. In line with
Remark 1.2
, when
G\scr{T}
is based
G
-spaces
[
MMO
, Addendum 4.6]
adds in cofibration
conditions that are automatically satisfied by categories of operators constructed from operads.

The full subcategory
\scr{D}\subset\scr{D}_{G}
whose objects are the trivial
G
-sets
\mathbf{n}
is a category of operators over
\scr{F}
, also denoted
\mathbb{U}\scr{D}_{G}
. Conversely, we can prolong a
CO

\scr{D}
over
\scr{F}
to a
G
-
CO

\scr{D}_{G}
over
\scr{F}_{G}
, also denoted
\mathbb{P}\scr{D}
. Moreover, as noted in
[
GMMO23
, Proposition 6.9]
, up to isomorphism all
\scr{D}_{G}
can be constructed in this fashion.

Construction 6.13
.

Let
\scr{D}
be a category of operators over
\scr{F}
. We define a prolonged
G
-category of operators

\scr{D}_{G}=\mathbb{P}\scr{D}
over
\scr{F}_{G}
whose full subcategory of objects
\mathbf{n}
is
\scr{D}
. The morphism object

\scr{D}_{G}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{n}^{\beta})
in
G\scr{T}
is the underlying object
\scr{D}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
of
\scr{U}
with
G
-action induced by conjugation and the original
G
-action on
\scr{D}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
. Explicitly, for
f\in\scr{D}_{G}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{n}^{\beta})
,

g\cdot f=\beta(g)\circ(gf)\circ\alpha(g^{-1});

We check that
g\cdot(h\cdot f)=(gh)\cdot f
using that
G
acts trivially on permutations since they
are in the image of
\Pi
. Composition and identity maps are inherited from
\scr{D}
and are appropriately equivariant.

Corollary 6.14
.

Applied to
\scr{F}
, the construction reconstructs
\scr{F}_{G}
, and it restricts to reconstruct

\Sigma_{G}
,
\Lambda_{G}
, and
\Pi_{G}
from
\Sigma
,
\Lambda
, and
\Pi
, respectively.

6.3.
Operads
\scr{C}
and
G
-operads
\scr{C}_{G}

The
\scr{D}
and hence
\scr{D}_{G}
of interest are constructed from operads. We will not repeat the complete definition of an operad from
[
May72
]
or
[
May97
]
. The following recollection focuses on operads in
G\scr{U}
, the category of unbased
G
-spaces, although we could work with
G
-objects in other categories.

Definition 6.15
.

Recall that an operad
\scr{C}
in
G\scr{U}
consists of objects
\scr{C}(n)\in G\scr{U}
, where
\scr{C}(n)
has a left action by
G
and a right action by
\Sigma_{n}
that commute with each other, together with a unit
G
-map
\operatorname{id}\colon\ast\longrightarrow\scr{C}(1)
and structure

G
-maps

\gamma\colon\scr{C}(k)\times\scr{C}(j_{1})\times\cdots\times\scr{C}(j_{i})\longrightarrow\scr{C}(j),

where
j=j_{1}+\cdots+j_{k}
, which are associative, unital, and equivariant as specified in
[
May72
,
May97
]
. We say that
\scr{C}
is reduced if
\scr{C}(0)=\ast
, and we restrict attention to reduced operads henceforward.

Notation 6.16
.

Let
\phi_{n}\colon\mathbf{n}\longrightarrow\mathbf{1}
be the based function that sends all
i\geq 1
to
1
. Observe that
\scr{F}
is generated by
\Pi
and the
\phi_{n}
and that

(6.17)

\phi_{k}\circ(\phi_{j_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee\phi_{j_{k}})=\phi_{j},

where
j=j_{1}+\cdots+j_{k}
. Similarly, let
\phi_{n}^{\alpha}\colon\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}\longrightarrow\mathbf{1}
be the based function that sends all
i\geq 1
to
1
and observe that
\scr{F}_{G}
is generated by
\Pi_{G}
and the
\phi_{n}^{\alpha}
.

Warning 6.18
.

There is no general analogue to
(6.17)
of the form

\phi_{\mathbf{k}^{\beta}}\circ(\phi_{\mathbf{j}_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}}\vee\cdots\vee\phi_{\mathbf{j}_{k}^{\alpha_{k}}})=\phi_{\mathbf{j}^{\gamma(\beta;\vee\alpha_{r})}}

since there is no way to define a homomorphism
\gamma(\beta;\vee\alpha_{r})\colon G\longrightarrow\Sigma_{j}
that gives the target as a sensible finite
G
-set without a relationship between
\beta
and the
\alpha_{r}
.

Thinking of the based finite
G
-set
\mathbf{j}_{r}^{\alpha_{r}}
as the
r
th ordered block in the wedge sum

\mathbf{j}^{\vee\alpha_{r}}=\mathbf{j}_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee\mathbf{j}_{k}^{\alpha_{k}},

we see what that relationship must be.

Definition 6.19
.

Say that
(\beta,\{\alpha_{r}\})
is
composable if
j_{r}=j_{s}
and
\alpha_{r}=\alpha_{s}
whenever

\beta(g)(r)=s
for some
g\in G
, where
r,s\in\{1,\cdots,k\}
. Then define

\gamma(\beta;\vee\alpha_{r})\colon G\longrightarrow\Sigma_{j}

by letting

\gamma(\beta;\vee\alpha_{r})(g)(i)=\alpha_{s}(g)(i)

when
\beta(g)(r)=s
; on the left,
i
is in the
r
th ordered block of
j_{r}
letters; on the right,
i
is in the
s
th ordered block of

j_{r}=j_{s}
letters.

Definition 6.20
.

Let
\scr{C}
be an operad in
G\scr{U}
. We construct a
CO
over
\scr{F}
,
which we denote by
\mathfrak{D}(\scr{C})
, abbreviated
\scr{D}
when there is no risk of confusion. Similarly, we write

\scr{D}_{G}=\mathfrak{D}_{G}(\scr{C})
for the associated prolonged
G
-CO over
\scr{F}_{G}
. The morphism objects in
G\scr{T}
of
\scr{D}
are

{\scr{D}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=\coprod_{\phi\in\scr{F}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})}\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\scr{C}(\phi_{j})

with
G
-action induced by the
G
-actions on the
\scr{C}(n)
. Here
\phi_{j}=|\phi^{-1}(j)|
.
Write elements in the form
(\phi,c)
, where
c=(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})
.
For
(\phi,c)\colon\mathbf{m}\longrightarrow\mathbf{n}
and
(\psi,d)\colon\mathbf{k}\longrightarrow\mathbf{m}
, define

(\phi,c)\circ(\psi,d)=\big(\phi\circ\psi,\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\gamma(c_{j};\prod_{\phi(i)=j}d_{i})\sigma_{j}\big).

Here
\gamma
denotes the structural maps of the operad. The
d_{i}
with
\phi(i)=j

are ordered by the natural order on their indices
i

and
\sigma_{j}
is that permutation of
(\phi\circ\psi)_{j}
letters which converts
the natural ordering of
(\phi\circ\psi)^{-1}(j)
as a subset of
\{1,\dots,k\}
to
its ordering obtained by regarding it as
\coprod_{\phi(i)=j}\psi^{-1}(i)
, so ordered
that elements of
\psi^{-1}(i)
precede elements of
\psi^{-1}(i^{\prime})
if
i<i^{\prime}
and
each
\psi^{-1}(i)
has its natural ordering as a subset of
\{1,\dots,k\}
.
The identity element in
\scr{D}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n})
is
(id,\operatorname{id}^{n})
, where
\operatorname{id}
on
the right is the unit element in
\scr{C}(1)
. The map
\xi\colon\scr{D}\longrightarrow\scr{F}

sends
(\phi,c)
to
\phi
. The inclusion
\iota\colon\Pi\longrightarrow\scr{D}
sends

\phi\colon\mathbf{m}\longrightarrow\mathbf{n}
to
(\phi,c)
, where
c_{i}=\operatorname{id}\in\scr{C}(1)
if
\phi(i)=1
and

c_{i}=\ast\in\scr{C}(0)
if
\phi(i)=0
.

Example 6.21
.

The commutativity operad
\scr{N}
has
n
th space a point for all
n
.
We think of it as a
G
-trivial
G
-operad. Then
\scr{F}=\mathfrak{D}(\scr{N})
, again regarded
as
G
-trivial.

Definition 6.22
.

Conversely, suppose given a CO
\scr{D}
over
\scr{F}
such that the iterated wedge sum in
\scr{F}
of
Remark 6.8
is covered by an analogous associative, unital, and equivariant “wedge sum” in
\scr{D}
:
27
27

27

Following the notation for
\scr{F}
, we write
\vee
, but we do not consider basepoints in the
\scr{D}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
.

(6.23)

\textstyle{\scr{D}(\mathbf{j}_{1},\mathbf{1})\times\cdots\times\scr{D}(\mathbf{j}_{k},\mathbf{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vee}
\scriptstyle{\xi^{k}}
\textstyle{\scr{D}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi}
\textstyle{\scr{F}(\mathbf{j}_{1},\mathbf{1})\times\cdots\times\scr{F}(\mathbf{j}_{k},\mathbf{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vee}
\textstyle{\scr{F}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k})}

This condition holds in the examples we know. We then construct an operad
\mathfrak{C}(\scr{D})=\scr{C}
, called the operad associated to
\scr{D}
.
We define
\scr{C}(n)
to be the component of
\phi_{n}
in
\scr{D}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{1})
with its given left action by
G
and with right action by
\Sigma_{n}
induced by composition with
\Sigma_{n}\subset\Pi(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n})
. We define
\operatorname{id}\in\scr{C}(1)
via the identity morphism of
\scr{D}(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1})
, and we define the structural maps
\gamma
to be the restrictions to the components of
\phi_{k}
and the
\phi_{j_{r}}
of the composites

(6.24)

\textstyle{\scr{D}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{1})\times\scr{D}(\mathbf{j}_{1},\mathbf{1})\times\cdots\times\scr{D}(\mathbf{j}_{k},\mathbf{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\times\vee}
\textstyle{\scr{D}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{1})\times\scr{D}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\scr{D}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{1}).}

Since
\scr{C}(n)
lies over
\phi_{n}
, it is unbased, so in
G\scr{U}
.

Remark 6.25
.

This gives a functor
\mathfrak{C}(-)
from the full subcategory of those categories of operators over
\scr{F}
in
G\scr{T}
which satisfy the assumption of
(6.23)
to the category of operads in
G\scr{U}
. This functor is left adjoint to the functor
\mathfrak{D}(-)
of
Definition 6.20
. The unit of the adjunction is given by the evident identifications
\scr{C}=\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{D}(\scr{C}))
. The counit
\varepsilon\colon\mathfrak{D}\mathfrak{C}(\scr{D})\longrightarrow\scr{D}
is given by use of
\vee
. It is an isomorphism in the examples we care about, but presumably not in general.

Warning 6.26
.

There is no general analogue to
(6.24)
for
G
-categories of operators over

\scr{F}_{G}
. When we start with general finite
G
-sets, the target and source of the middle term are different.

To get around this problem, we define the following twisted product.

Definition 6.27
.

For composable
(\beta,\{\alpha_{r}\})
, let
\prod_{r\in\mathbf{k}^{\beta}}\scr{D}_{G}(\mathbf{j}_{r}^{\alpha_{r}},\mathbf{1})
be the space
\prod_{1\leq r\leq k}\scr{D}_{G}(\mathbf{j}_{r}^{\alpha_{r}},\mathbf{1})
with twisted
G
-action given in precise analogy with
the definition of
\gamma(\beta;\vee\alpha_{r})
in
Definition 6.19
so as to ensure that
\vee
on

\prod_{r\in\mathbf{k}^{\beta}}\scr{F}_{G}(\mathbf{j}_{r}^{\alpha_{r}},\mathbf{1})
lands in

\scr{F}_{G}(\mathbf{j}^{\gamma(\beta;\vee\alpha_{r})},\mathbf{k}^{\beta})
.
In analogy with
Definition 6.22
, we require that the iterated wedge sum in
\scr{F}_{G}
be covered by an
associative, unital, and equivariant wedge sum in
\scr{D}_{G}
, as in
(6.23)
:

(6.28)

\textstyle{\prod_{r\in\mathbf{k}^{\beta}}\scr{D}_{G}(\mathbf{j}_{r}^{\alpha_{r}},\mathbf{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vee}
\scriptstyle{\xi^{k}}
\textstyle{\scr{D}_{G}(\mathbf{j}^{\gamma(\beta;\vee\alpha_{r})},\mathbf{k}^{\beta})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi}
\textstyle{\prod_{r\in\mathbf{k}^{\beta}}\scr{F}_{G}(\mathbf{j}_{r}^{\alpha_{r}},\mathbf{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vee}
\textstyle{\scr{F}_{G}(\mathbf{j}^{\gamma(\beta;\vee\alpha_{r})},\mathbf{k}^{\beta})}

We then define
\gamma_{G}
to be the composite

(6.29)

\textstyle{\scr{D}_{G}(\mathbf{k}^{\beta},\mathbf{1})\times\prod_{r\in\mathbf{k}^{\beta}}\scr{D}_{G}(\mathbf{j}_{r}^{\alpha_{r}},\mathbf{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times\vee}
\textstyle{\scr{D}_{G}(\mathbf{k}^{\beta},\mathbf{1})\times\scr{D}_{G}(\mathbf{j}^{\gamma(\beta;\vee\alpha_{r})},\mathbf{k}^{\beta})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\scr{D}_{G}(\mathbf{j}^{\gamma(\beta;\vee\alpha_{r})},\mathbf{1}),}

With
G
acting through
\gamma(\beta;\vee\alpha_{r})
,
\gamma_{G}
is then a
G
-map.

We can now give a definition of
G
-operads
\scr{C}_{G}
that allows a comparison analogous to that of
Remark 6.25
. The problems explained in Warnings
6.18
and
6.26
and the answers to those problems given in Definitions
6.19
and
6.27
pave the way.

Definition 6.30
.

A
G
-operad
\scr{C}_{G}
in
G\scr{U}
consists of
G
-objects
\scr{C}_{G}(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})
in
G\scr{U}
for all
\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}
, where

\scr{C}_{G}(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})
has a left action by
G
and a right action by
\Sigma_{n}
that together give a right action of the semidirect product

\Sigma_{n}\rtimes_{\alpha_{c}}G
, together with a unit
G
-map
\operatorname{id}\colon\ast\longrightarrow\scr{C}_{G}(\mathbf{1})
and structure

G
-maps

\gamma_{G}\colon\scr{C}_{G}(\mathbf{k}^{\beta})\times\scr{C}_{G}(\mathbf{j}_{1}^{\alpha_{1}})\times\cdots\times\scr{C}_{G}(\mathbf{j}_{k}^{\alpha_{k}})\longrightarrow\scr{C}_{G}(\mathbf{j}^{\gamma(\beta;\vee\alpha_{r})})

for composable
(\beta,\{\alpha_{r}\}_{1\leq r\leq k})
. We require the
\gamma_{G}
to be both

\Sigma_{k}\rtimes_{\beta_{c}}G\ \ \text{and}\ \ (\Sigma_{j_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Sigma_{j_{k}})\rtimes_{(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{k})_{c}}G

equivariant, where these groups act on the source of
\gamma
via their actions on its coordinates and where they act on the target via embeddings as subgroups of
\Sigma_{j}
via block permutations and permutations within blocks.
The
\gamma_{G}
must be associative and unital for composable data, as specified in
[
May97
, Definition 1]
. We say that
\scr{C}_{G}
is reduced if
\scr{C}_{G}(\mathbf{0})=\ast
, and we restrict attention to reduced
G
-operads henceforward.

Proposition 6.31
.

A
G
-operad
\scr{C}_{G}
in
G\scr{U}
restricts on
G
-trivial based finite
G
-sets
\mathbf{n}
to an
operad
\scr{C}=\mathbb{U}\scr{C}_{G}
in
G\scr{U}
. Conversely, an operad
\scr{C}
in
G\scr{U}
prolongs to a
G
-operad
\scr{C}_{G}=\mathbb{P}\scr{C}
in
G\scr{U}
.

Proof.

The prolongation is analogous to the prolongation from
\scr{D}
to
\scr{D}_{G}
spelled out in

Construction 6.13
, starting from the definition
\scr{C}_{G}(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})=\scr{C}(n)^{{\alpha}}
, with notation as in
Definition 6.38
.
∎

The evident analogue of
Remark 6.25
holds and we have the following diagram of categories and adjunctions.

(6.32)

\textstyle{\text{Operads in}\ G\scr{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{D}(-)}
\textstyle{G\text{-Operads in}\ G\scr{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{D}_{G}(-)}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{U}}
\textstyle{CO^{\prime}s\ \text{over}\ \scr{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{C}(-)}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}
\textstyle{G\text{-}CO^{\prime}s\ \text{over}\ \scr{F}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{U}}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{C}_{G}(-)}

We summarize our conclusions.

Proposition 6.33
.

In the adjunctions in
(6.32)
, the composites

\mathbb{U}\mathbb{P},\ \ \mathfrak{C}(-)\mathfrak{D}(-)\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mathfrak{C}_{G}(-)\mathfrak{D}_{G}(-)

are the identity, and the composite
\mathbb{P}\mathbb{U}
is naturally isomorphic
to the identity. Moreover, the square of left adjoints and the square of right adjoints commute.

6.4.
The associated monads
\mathbb{D}
and
\mathbb{D}_{G}

The ground category of the monads
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{C}_{G}
is
G\scr{T}
. The following notations and definition specify the ground categories of the monads
\mathbb{D}
and
\mathbb{D}_{G}
. Recall from
Notation 6.9
that
G\scr{T}(X,Y)
denotes the based space of
G
-morphisms
X\longrightarrow Y

and that
\underline{G\scr{T}}(X,Y)
denotes the based
G
-space of morphisms
X\longrightarrow Y
in the enriched category
\underline{G\scr{T}}
.
Note that

G\scr{T}(X,Y)=\underline{G\scr{T}}(X,Y)^{G}.

Remark 6.34
.

In line with
Remark 1.2
,
[
MMO
]
used
G\scr{U}_{*}
when working formally with general based
G
-spaces and used
G\scr{T}
when thinking homotopically and restricting to nondegenerately based
G
-spaces. Again, the distinction is carefully handled in
[
MMO
]
, and we find the use of two notations distracting in the conceptual framework on which we are focusing.

Definition 6.35
.

Let
\Pi[G\scr{T}]=\Pi[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
be the category of functors
\Pi\longrightarrow G\scr{T}
or, equivalently, the category of
G
-functors
\Pi\longrightarrow\underline{G\scr{T}}
. The equality holds since
G
acts trivially on
\Pi
so that a
G
-functor
X

from
\Pi
to
\underline{G\scr{T}}
must take values fixed under
G
, that is, equivariant maps. We call
X
a
\Pi
-
G
-object in
\scr{T}
.
Analogously, let
\Pi_{G}[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
be the category of
G
-functors
\Pi_{G}\longrightarrow\underline{G\scr{T}}
. We call
X
a
\Pi_{G}
-
G
-object in
\scr{T}
. Restriction to the trivial
\mathbf{n}
gives a forgetful functor
\mathbb{U}\colon\Pi_{G}[\underline{G\scr{T}}]\longrightarrow\Pi[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
.

Remark 6.36
.

For reasons as in
Remark 1.2
,
[
MMO
, Definitions 2.3 and 2.33]
adds in a cofibration condition.

Proposition 6.37
.

The forgetful functor
\mathbb{U}\colon\Pi_{G}[\underline{G\scr{T}}]\longrightarrow\Pi[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
has a left adjoint prolongation functor
\mathbb{P}
, and
(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{U})
is an adjoint equivalence. It restricts to an adjoint equivalence between

\Lambda[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
and
\Lambda_{G}[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
.

Proof.

This is the formal part of
[
MMO
, Theorem 2.38]
. For

X\in\Pi[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
,
\mathbb{P}X
is given by the categorical tensor product

(\mathbb{P}X)(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})=\Pi_{G}(-,\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})\otimes_{\Pi}X.

Since
\mathbb{U}
is full and faithful, the unit
\eta\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{U}\mathbb{P}X
is the evident identification.
The counit
\varepsilon\colon\mathbb{P}\mathbb{U}Y\longrightarrow Y
is an isomorphism by a Yoneda type verification using the identity function on
\mathbf{n}
regarded as an element of
\Pi_{G}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})
. The statement with
\Pi
and
\Pi_{G}
replaced by
\Lambda
and

\Lambda_{G}
is proven in the same way.
∎

The following definition, which is
[
MMO
, Definition 1.3]
, leads to combinatorially explicit descriptions of
\mathbb{P}X
and of

\varepsilon^{-1}\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}\mathbb{U}Y
.

Definition 6.38
.

Let
X
be a
(G\times\Sigma_{n})
-space and
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow\Sigma_{n}
be a homomorphism. We define
X^{\alpha}
to be the
G
-space with underlying space
X
and with a new
G
-action
\cdot_{\alpha}
given by

g\cdot_{\alpha}x=(g,\alpha(g))\cdot x
. Thus
X^{\alpha}
is
X
with
G
-action twisted by
\alpha
. Said another way
X^{\alpha}
is just notation for
X
with its action by the graph subgroup
\Gamma_{\alpha}=\{(g,\alpha(g)\}
of
G\times\Sigma_{n}
, pulled back along the isomorphism
\pi^{-1}\colon G\longrightarrow\Gamma_{\alpha}
, where
\pi\colon\Gamma_{\alpha}\longrightarrow G
is the projection. Thus, on passage to fixed point spaces,

(X^{\alpha})^{G}\cong X^{\Gamma_{\alpha}}.

Definition 6.39
.

The monads
\mathbb{D}
in
\Pi[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
and
\mathbb{D}_{G}
in
\Pi_{G}[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
are given by the categorical tensor products

(6.40)

(\mathbb{D}X)(\mathbf{n})=\scr{D}(-,\mathbf{n})\otimes_{\Pi}X\ \ \text{and}\ \ (\mathbb{D}_{G}Y)(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})=\scr{D}_{G}(-,\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})\otimes_{\Pi_{G}}Y

for a
\Pi
-
G
-functor
X
and a
\Pi_{G}
-
G
-functor
Y
. Here
\scr{D}(-,\mathbf{n})
and
\scr{D}_{G}(-,\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})
are the functors represented by
\mathbf{n}
and
\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}
. Explicitly, these tensor products are the coequalizers

(6.41)

\textstyle{\coprod_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{m}}\scr{D}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\times\Pi(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{m})\times X(\mathbf{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coprod_{k}\scr{D}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{n})\times X(\mathbf{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{D}X)(\mathbf{n})}

(6.42)

\textstyle{\underset{\mathbf{k}^{\gamma},\mathbf{m}^{\beta}}{\coprod}\scr{D}_{G}(\mathbf{m}^{\beta},\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})\times\Pi_{G}(\mathbf{k}^{\gamma},\mathbf{m}^{\beta})\times Y(\mathbf{k}^{\gamma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underset{\mathbf{k}^{\gamma}}{\coprod}\scr{D}_{G}(\mathbf{k}^{\gamma},\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})\times Y(\mathbf{k}^{\gamma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{D}_{G}Y)(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}).}

The parallel arrows are given by composition in
\scr{D}
or in
\scr{D}_{G}
and the action of
\Pi
on
X
or of
\Pi_{G}
on
Y
.
The products
\mu\colon\mathbb{D}\mathbb{D}\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}
and
\mu\colon\mathbb{D}_{G}\mathbb{D}_{G}\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{G}

are derived from the composition in
\scr{D}
or
\scr{D}_{G}
and thus from the structure maps
\gamma

of
\scr{C}
when
\scr{D}=\mathfrak{D}(\scr{C})
. The unit maps
\eta
are derived from the identity morphisms in these categories and thus from the unit
\operatorname{id}\in\scr{C}(1)
. We define a
\scr{D}
-
G
-algebra to be a

\mathbb{D}
-algebra in
\Pi[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
and we define a

\scr{D}_{G}
-
G
-algebra to be a
\mathbb{D}_{G}
-algebra in
\Pi_{G}[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
.
We let
\mathbb{D}\big[\Pi[\underline{G\scr{T}}]\big]
and
\mathbb{D}_{G}\big[\Pi_{G}[\underline{G\scr{T}}]\big]
denote the respective categories of algebras. Restriction to trivial
\mathbf{n}
gives a forgetful functor

\mathbb{U}\colon\mathbb{D}_{G}\big[\Pi_{G}[\underline{G\scr{T}}]\big]\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}\big[\Pi[\underline{G\scr{T}}]\big]
.

Ignoring cofibration conditions, the following definition is the formal part of
[
MMO
, Definitions 4.7 and 4.8]
.

Definition 6.43
.

Define a
\scr{D}
-
G
-space to be a
G\scr{T}
-functor
\scr{D}\longrightarrow\underline{G\scr{T}}
. Define
a
\scr{D}_{G}
-
G
-space to be a
G\scr{T}
-functor
\scr{D}_{G}\longrightarrow\underline{G\scr{T}}
. In both cases, maps are
G\scr{T}
-natural transformations.

Again ignoring cofibration conditions, the following result is
[
MMO
, Proposition 5.17]
. Its proof is an immediate comparison of definitions.

Proposition 6.44
.

The category of
\scr{D}
-
G
-spaces is isomorphic to the category of
\mathbb{D}
-algebras in
\Pi[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
. The category of
\scr{D}_{G}
-
G
-spaces is isomorphic to the category of
\mathbb{D}_{G}
-algebras in
\Pi_{G}[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
.

The following result is
[
MMO
, Theorem 4.11]
. The proof there is a detailed combinatorial inspection of definitions starting from
Proposition 6.37
, but we give a quick conceptual argument.

Proposition 6.45
.

The prolongation functor
\Pi[G\scr{T}]\longrightarrow\Pi_{G}[G\scr{T}]
extends to a prolongation functor

\mathbb{P}\colon\mathbb{D}\big[\Pi[\underline{G\scr{T}}]\big]\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{G}\big[\Pi_{G}[\underline{G\scr{T}}]\big]
left adjoint to the forgetful functor
\mathbb{U}\colon\mathbb{D}_{G}\big[\Pi_{G}[\underline{G\scr{T}}]\big]\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}\big[\Pi[\underline{G\scr{T}}]\big]
, and
(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{U})
is an adjoint equivalence.

Proof.

The functor
\mathbb{P}
is defined on
\mathbb{D}
-algebras
Y
by the categorical tensor product

(\mathbb{P}Y)(\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})=\scr{D}_{G}(-,\mathbf{n}^{\alpha})\otimes_{\Pi}Y(-),

where the variable
(-)
runs over the objects
\mathbf{m}
of
\scr{D}
.
It is clear from the definition that
\mathbb{P}Y
is naturally a
\scr{D}_{G}
-
G
-space. By
Proposition 6.44
, that means that
\mathbb{P}Y
is a
\mathbb{D}_{G}
-algebra.
The adjunction is immediate.

Since

\mathbb{U}
is full and faithful, for a
\mathbb{D}
-algebra
X
the unit
\eta\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{U}\mathbb{P}X
is the evident identification. Consider the counit
\varepsilon\colon\mathbb{P}\mathbb{U}Z\longrightarrow Z
for a
\mathbb{D}_{G}
-algebra
Z
. Here
Z
is given by a split coequalizer as in
(1.18)
. Since
\mathbb{P}\mathbb{U}
preserves split coequalizers, it is immediate by comparison of coequalizers that
\varepsilon
is an isomorphism if it is an isomorphism when
Z=\mathbb{D}_{G}Y
for a
\Pi_{G}
-
G
-space
Y
. Moreover, by
Proposition 6.37
, we may assume without loss of generality that
Y=\mathbb{P}X
, where
X
is a
\Pi
-
G
-space and
\mathbb{P}
here is the prolongation functor of that result. Not inserting notation for variables, we have the following formal identification.

\mathbb{D}_{G}\mathbb{P}X=\scr{D}_{G}\otimes_{\Pi_{G}}(\Pi_{G}\otimes_{\Pi}X)\cong\scr{D}_{G}\otimes_{\Pi}X\cong\scr{D}_{G}\otimes_{\scr{D}}(\scr{D}\otimes_{\Pi}X)=\mathbb{P}\mathbb{D}X.

Therefore
\varepsilon\colon\mathbb{P}\mathbb{U}\mathbb{D}_{G}\mathbb{P}X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}X

can be identified with
\varepsilon\colon\mathbb{P}\mathbb{U}\mathbb{P}\mathbb{D}X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}\mathbb{D}X,

which is an isomorphism with inverse
\mathbb{P}\eta
by a triangle identity.
∎

6.5.
The associated monads
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{C}_{G}

We turn to the monads associated to operads
\scr{C}
and
G
-operads
\scr{C}_{G}
. In contrast to the case of categories of operators, these monads are special cases rather than general cases of certain categorical tensor products. We start with the general definition. Recall
\Lambda
and
\Lambda_{G}
from Definitions
6.2
and
6.4
. Fix an operad
\scr{C}
of
G
-spaces and a
G
-operad
\scr{C}_{G}
, which we can take to be
\mathbb{P}(\scr{C})
. Let
\scr{D}=\mathfrak{D}(\scr{C})
and

\scr{D}_{G}=\mathfrak{D}_{G}(\scr{C}_{G})\cong\mathbb{P}\mathfrak{D}(\scr{C})
.

Lemma 6.46
.

The operad
\scr{C}
restricts to a contravariant functor
\scr{C}\colon\Lambda\longrightarrow G\scr{U}
. The
G
-operad
\scr{C}_{G}
restricts to a contravariant functor
\scr{C}_{G}\colon\Lambda_{G}\longrightarrow\underline{G\scr{U}}
.

Proof.

The first statement goes back to
[
May72
, Construction 2.4]
; details are recalled in
Definition 8.4
below; an analogous explicit equivariant description is given in
[
KMZ24
]
. The action can be described implicitly by the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\scr{C}(n)\times\Lambda(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\subset}
\textstyle{\scr{C}(m)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\subset}
\textstyle{\scr{D}(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{1})\times\scr{D}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\scr{D}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{1}).}

That is, the restriction to
\scr{C}(n)\times\Lambda(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
of the displayed composition in
\scr{D}
lands in
\scr{C}(m)
and gives the action. A proof of the second statement works the same way, using the analogous diagram

\textstyle{\scr{C}_{G}(\mathbf{n}^{\beta})\times\Lambda_{G}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{n}^{\beta})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\subset}
\textstyle{\scr{C}_{G}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\subset}
\textstyle{\scr{D}_{G}(\mathbf{n}^{\beta},\mathbf{1})\times\scr{D}_{G}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{n}^{\beta})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\scr{D}_{G}(\mathbf{m}^{\alpha},\mathbf{1}).}

Definition 6.47
.

Let
\Lambda[G\scr{T}]=\Lambda[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
be the category of functors
\Lambda\longrightarrow G\scr{T}
and let
\Lambda_{G}[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
be the category
G
-functors
\Lambda_{G}\longrightarrow\underline{G\scr{T}}
. Define functors

\mathbb{D}\colon\Lambda[\underline{G\scr{T}}]\longrightarrow G\scr{T}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mathbb{D}_{G}\colon\Lambda_{G}[\underline{G\scr{T}}]\longrightarrow G\scr{T}

by the categorical tensor products defined on
X\in\Lambda[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
and
Y\in\Lambda_{G}[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
by

\mathbb{D}X=\scr{C}\otimes_{\Lambda}X\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mathbb{D}_{G}Y=\scr{C}_{G}\otimes_{\Lambda_{G}}Y.

The following surprising identification is proven by a comparison of coequalizer diagrams that is given in
[
KMZ24
]
.

Proposition 6.48
.

The functor
\mathbb{D}
can be identified with the composite
\mathbb{D}_{G}\circ\mathbb{P}
.

The general functor
\mathbb{D}
was used in
[
CMT78
,
MT78
]
, but it restricts to a monad on
G\scr{T}
. We use the following definition to show that
\mathbb{D}_{G}
also restricts to a monad on
G\scr{T}
. For immediate purposes, it suffices to consider
\Lambda
and
\Lambda_{G}
, but we will shortly also need
\Pi
and
\Pi_{G}
. We use different notations because the conceptual roles of
\Lambda
and
\Lambda_{G}
are quite different from the roles of
\Pi
and
\Pi_{G}
.

Definition 6.49
.

We define
G\scr{T}
-functors

\mathbb{R}\colon G\scr{T}\longrightarrow\Pi[\underline{G\scr{T}}]\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mathbb{R}_{G}\colon G\scr{T}\longrightarrow\Pi_{G}[\underline{G\scr{T}}].

For
X\in G\scr{T}
, we let
(\mathbb{R}X)(\mathbf{n})=X^{n}
. For a based function
f\colon\mathbf{m}\longrightarrow\mathbf{n}
such that
|f^{-1}(j)|=0
or
1
for
j>0
, we let

(\mathbb{R}X)(f)\colon X^{m}\longrightarrow X^{n}
be the map that sends
(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
to
(y_{1},\cdots y_{j})
, where
y_{j}=x_{f^{-1}(j)}
if
f^{-1}(j)>0
and
y_{j}=\ast
if
j\notin\operatorname{im}(f)
. We define
\mathbb{R}_{G}=\mathbb{P}\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathbb{P}\colon\Pi[\underline{G\scr{T}}]\longrightarrow\Pi_{G}[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
is defined in
Proposition 6.37
. We define

\mathbb{Q}\colon G\scr{T}\longrightarrow\Lambda[\underline{G\scr{T}}]\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mathbb{Q}_{G}\colon G\scr{T}\longrightarrow\Lambda_{G}[\underline{G\scr{T}}]

by restricting from
\Pi
to
\Lambda
and from
\Pi_{G}
to
\Lambda_{G}
in the targets of
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{R}_{G}
.

The following specialization of
Definition 6.38
, which is
[
MMO
, Example 1.5]
, makes
\mathbb{R}_{G}X
more explicit.

Example 6.50
.

Let
X
be a based
G
-object and consider the based
(G\times\Sigma_{n})
-object
X^{n}
. Then we have the identification

(X^{n})^{\alpha}=X^{\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}}
, where
X^{\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}}
denotes the set of based maps
\mathbf{n}^{\alpha}\longrightarrow X
with
G
acting by conjugation. Explicitly, this is the cartesian power
X^{n}
with twisted action by
G
given by

g(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=(gx_{\alpha(g^{-1})(1)},\dots,gx_{\alpha(g^{-1})(n)}).

Notation 6.51
.

Restricting to
\Lambda
and
\Lambda_{G}
, we write

\mathbb{C}X=\mathbb{D}\mathbb{Q}X\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mathbb{C}_{G}X=\mathbb{D}_{G}\mathbb{Q}_{G}X.

for based
G
-spaces
X
.

Proposition 6.52
.

The functor
\mathbb{C}\colon G\scr{T}\longrightarrow G\scr{T}
is a monad with product
\mu
induced by the structure maps
\gamma
of
\scr{C}
and unit
\eta
induced by the unit
\operatorname{id}:\ast\longrightarrow\scr{C}(1)
. The functor
\mathbb{C}_{G}\colon G\scr{T}\longrightarrow G\scr{T}
is a monad with product
\mu
induced by the structure maps
\gamma
of
\scr{C}_{G}
and unit
\eta
induced by the unit
\operatorname{id}:\ast\longrightarrow\scr{C}_{G}(1)
.

The first statement goes back to
[
May72
, Construction 2.4]
, with later generalizations of context. The analog for
\mathbb{C}_{G}
is not as obvious since the restriction of the structure map
\gamma_{G}
to composable tuples makes it appear that the product
\mu
on
\mathbb{C}_{G}
is only partially defined. However,
Proposition 6.48
saves the day. More details
are given in
[
KMZ24
]
.

We complete the formal picture with the following comparisons of ground categories and of monads.

Definition 6.53
.

Define
G\scr{T}
-functors

\mathbb{L}\colon\Pi[\underline{G\scr{T}}]\longrightarrow G\scr{T}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mathbb{L}_{G}\colon\Pi_{G}[\underline{G\scr{T}}]\longrightarrow G\scr{T}

by evaluating functors on
\mathbf{1}
.

Lemma 6.54
.

The pairs
(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R})
and
(\mathbb{L}_{G},\mathbb{R}_{G})
are adjunctions such that
\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}=\operatorname{id}
and
\mathbb{L}_{G}\mathbb{R}_{G}=\operatorname{id}
. Moreover,
just as
\mathbb{P}\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{R}_{G}
, so also
\mathbb{U}\mathbb{R}_{G}=\mathbb{R}
, where
(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{U})
is as in
Proposition 6.37
.

Remark 6.55
.

The units of these adjunctions are denoted

\delta\colon\operatorname{id}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \delta_{G}\colon\operatorname{id}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}_{G}\mathbb{L}_{G}

since, for
X\in\Pi[\scr{T}]
, the coordinates of the maps
\delta\colon X(n)\longrightarrow X(1)^{n}
are induced by delta functions
\mathbf{n}\longrightarrow\mathbf{1}
. These maps are called Segal maps since their importance was first noticed by Segal
[
Seg74
]
, who coined the word “special” to indicate when they are equivalences.

Lemma 6.56
.

We have an isomorphism
\omega\colon\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}
of functors
G\scr{T}\longrightarrow\Pi[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
and an isomorphism

\omega_{G}\colon\mathbb{D}_{G}\mathbb{R}_{G}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}_{G}\mathbb{C}_{G}
of functors
G\scr{T}\longrightarrow\Pi_{G}[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
.

Lemmas
6.54
and
6.56
were proven nonequivariantly in
[
MT78
, Lemma 5.7 and Section 6]
. Equivariantly, ignoring
\mathbb{C}_{G}
, they were proven in
[
MMO
, Lemma 2.8 and Propositions 5.21 and 5.22]
28
28

28

[
MMO
, Proposition 5.20]
states that
\mathbb{L}
takes
\mathbb{D}
-algebras to
\mathbb{C}
-algebras, which is false, but Propositions 5.21 and 5.22 do not use that and are correct as stated; remember that
\mathbb{C}_{G}
in
[
MMO
]
is what we are denoting by
\mathbb{C}
here.
. The proof of the second part of
Lemma 6.56
is entirely similar to that of the first. In both parts, the diagrams of
Section 5.1
are easily seen to commute.

The small right-hand triangle of the top diagram is the
G
-spectrum level special
case of
(1.12)
that is discussed in
Section 4.6
. Via Propositions
6.37
and
6.45
, we think
of the prolongation and forgetful functor adjunction
(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{U})
as mapping the
entire top diagram equivalently to the entire bottom diagram. Since the monads
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{C}_{G}
on

are isomorphic, we think of

(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{U})
as identifying the small right-hand triangles of the two diagrams.
Section 4.6
applies equally well to both, compatibly.

The monads
\mathbb{D}
and
\mathbb{D}_{G}
in the left trapezoids have different but equivalent ground categories
\Pi[G\scr{T}]=\Pi[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
and

\Pi_{G}[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
, and they have equivalent categories of algebras.
From the formal point of view of
Section 5.1
, the adjunction
(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{U})
therefore gives an equivalence between the top diagram and the bottom diagram.

However, remember that we must start with chosen subcategories of weak equivalences in
\Pi[G\scr{T}]
and in

\Pi_{G}[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
, as in
Section 5.1
. We recall from
[
MMO
]
how these can and must be defined in order for

to induce an equivalence between these subcategories from a homotopical point of view. In fact we have two choices, one leading to genuine
G
-spectra for finite
G
and the other leading to classical
G
-spectra for general topological groups
G
, as in
Remark 4.60
.

Since
\Sigma\subset\Pi
,
X(\mathbf{n})
and
X(\mathbf{1})^{n}
are
(G\times\Sigma_{n})
-spaces and
\delta\colon X(\mathbf{n})\longrightarrow X(\mathbf{1})^{n}
is a map of

-

. The following definitions are part of
[

, Definitions 2.14 and 2.35]
. Recall
Definition 4.55
.

Definition 6.58
.

(i)

A map
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
of
(G\times\Sigma_{n})
-spaces is an
\mathbb{F}_{n}
-equivalence if
f^{\Lambda}\colon X^{\Lambda}\longrightarrow Y^{\Lambda}
is a weak equivalence for each
\Lambda\in\mathbb{F}_{n}
.

(ii)

A map
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
of
\Pi
-

-level equivalence if

is an

.

(iii)

A map
f
of
\scr{D}

is a

is a level

-
G

Using these notions of weak equivalence, we rename things in the relevant special cases of

.

Definition 6.59
.

(i)

The following results are

Lemma 6.60
.

If

-

Lemma 6.61
.

Let
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y

-
G

is
\mathbb{F}_{\bullet}

-special.

We defined

-level equivalences and
\mathbb{F}_{\bullet}
-special

Lemma 6.62
.

A map

of

f_{n}

are weak

of

.
A

This leads to the following comparison, which is
[

Theorem 6.63
.

The following statements hold.

(i)

A map
f\colon Y\longrightarrow Z
of
\Pi_{G}
-
G

G

-

(ii)

A

-

A map

Theorem 6.66
.

(i)

If

From here, the discussion in Sections
5.1
and
5.4
makes clear that our Assumptions all hold. We specialize Theorems
5.31
and
5.32
to the second diagram of
(6.57)
. The analogous theorems hold for the first diagram, compatibly with respect to

.

Theorem 6.67
.

There is a functor
\mathrm{Bar}\colon\mathbb{D}_{G}\big[\Pi_{G}[\underline{G\scr{T}}]\big]\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{G}\big[\Pi_{G}[\underline{G\scr{T}}]\big]
, written
Y\mapsto\overline{Y}
, and a natural equivalence
\zeta\colon\overline{Y}\longrightarrow Y
. If
Y
is special, the unit

\eta_{\mathbb{D}_{G}}\colon\overline{Y}\longrightarrow(\mathbb{R}_{G}\Omega^{\infty})_{\mathbb{D}_{G}}(\Sigma^{\infty}\mathbb{L}_{G})_{\mathbb{D}_{G}}\overline{Y}

is a group completion and is therefore an equivalence if
Y
is grouplike.

Theorem 6.68
.

\big((\Sigma^{\infty}\mathbb{L})_{\mathbb{D}_{G}},(\mathbb{R}\Omega^{\infty})_{\mathbb{D}_{G}}\big)
induces an adjoint equivalence from the homotopy category of special grouplike
\mathbb{D}_{G}
-algebras in
\Pi_{G}[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
to the homotopy category of connective
G
-spectra.

Remark 6.69
.

As in
Remark 4.59
and
[
MMO
, Section 9.3]
, everything above applies equally well to compact Lie groups
G
, provided that we restrict attention to representations of
G
all of whose isotropy groups have finite index in
G
.

Following up
Remark 4.60
, there is a second specialization of
(6.57)
, where we now start with a nonequivariant
E_{\infty}
operad
\scr{C}
as defined in
Definition 4.53
, regarded as an operad of
G
-trivial
G
-spaces. While the notion of an
\mathbb{F}_{\bullet}
-special
\Pi
-
G
-space is only of real interest when

is finite, the following definitions apply to any topological group
G
.
29
29

29

Remember that we require
e\in G
to be a non-degenerate basepoint to avoid pathology.
They should be compared with Definitions
6.58
and
6.59
.

Definition 6.70
.

A map
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
of
\Pi
-spaces is a
level
G
-equivalence
if each
f_{n}\colon X(\mathbf{n})\longrightarrow Y(\mathbf{n})
is a weak

-
G

-equivalence if its underlying map of
\Pi
-
G
-spaces is a level
G
-equivalence.

Definition 6.71
.

A
\Pi
-space
X
is classically (or naively) special if
\delta\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}X
is a level
G
-equivalence.
A
\scr{D}
-
G
-space
X
is classically (or naively) special if its underlying
\Pi
-
G
-space is classically special.

The
\Pi_{G}
and
\scr{D}_{G}
-
G
-space analogues of the previous two definitions are of no great interest. They are obtained just by forgetting down to
\Pi
-
G
-spaces and applying the definitions just given. With these definitions, the two diagrams of
(6.57)
are tautologically equivalent homotopically, as well as equivalent formally. Focusing
on the first diagram, we have classical (or naive) analogs of Theorems
6.67
and
6.68
. These apply to arbitrary (non-degenerately based) groups
G
and refer to classical (or naive)
G
-spectra as defined in

Remark 4.60
.

Theorem 6.72
.

There is a functor
\mathrm{Bar}\colon\mathbb{D}\big[\Pi[\underline{G\scr{T}}]\big]\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}\big[\Pi[\underline{G\scr{T}}]\big]
, written
Y\mapsto\overline{Y}
, and a natural equivalence
\zeta\colon\overline{Y}\longrightarrow Y
. If
Y
is classically special, the unit

\eta_{\mathbb{D}}\colon\overline{Y}\longrightarrow(\mathbb{R}\Omega^{\infty})_{\mathbb{D}}(\Sigma^{\infty}\mathbb{L})_{\mathbb{D}}\overline{Y}

is a group completion and is therefore an equivalence if
Y
is grouplike.

Theorem 6.73
.

\big((\Sigma^{\infty}\mathbb{L})_{\mathbb{D}},(\mathbb{R}\Omega^{\infty})_{\mathbb{D}}\big)
induces an adjoint equivalence from the homotopy category of special grouplike
\mathbb{D}
-algebras in
\Pi[\underline{G\scr{T}}]
to the homotopy category of connective classical
G
-spectra.

Remark 6.74
.

Using orthogonal rather than Lewis-May
G
-spectra, the Segal and operadic machines are proven to be equivalent in
[
MMO
]
. This means that when we regard
\scr{F}_{G}
-
G
-spaces as examples of
\scr{D}_{G}
-
G
-spaces for a category of operators associated to an
E_{\infty}

G
-operad over
{\scr{K}_{G}}
, application of the Segal and operadic machines gives equivalent orthogonal
G
-spectra. One can compare the operadic machine with orthogonal
G
-spectra as output with the operadic machine here by using the comparison of orthogonal
G
-spectra to Lewis-May
G
-spectra in
[
MM02
, Chapter IV]
. That equivalence is multiplicative
[
MM02
, Theorem IV.1.1]
.

This gives a multiplicative Segal machine: apply the operadic machine given by

B(\Sigma^{\infty}\mathbb{L}_{G},\mathbb{D}_{G},-)
to
\scr{F}_{G}
-
G
-spaces regarded as
\mathbb{D}_{G}
-algebras by pullback along the projection
\scr{D}_{G}\longrightarrow\scr{F}_{G}
. From this standpoint, there is no need for the Segal machine as developed in
[
MMO
]
. The group completion property is already there from our operadic point of view. Retrospectively, in situations where our axioms are satisfied, there seems to be little advantage to the Segal machine. It is symmetric monoidal, directly, as proven in
[
GMMO19
]
, but that does not accept the weakly structured input that is needed for the applications.

7.
The specialization to categories of orbital presheaves

7.1.
The orbital presheaf context and a comparison of contexts

Let
G\scr{O}
denote the orbit category of a finite group
G
. Its objects are the orbits
G/H
and its morphisms are the
G
-maps. Let
G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]
denote the category of contravariant functors, alias orbital presheaves, from
G\scr{O}
to the category
\scr{T}
of based spaces. It is a long established fact
[
May96
,
MM02
]
that, with appropriate model structures, the category
G\scr{T}
of based
G
-spaces is Quillen equivalent to
G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]
. The right adjoint
\mathbb{R}\colon G\scr{T}\longrightarrow G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]
sends a
G
-space
X
to its fixed point functor, so that
(\mathbb{R}X)(G/H)=X^{H}
. The left adjoint
\mathbb{L}
sends an orbital presheaf
\scr{X}
to the
G
-space
\scr{X}(G/e)
. Clearly
\mathbb{L}\mathbb{R}=\operatorname{id}
.

In contrast to earlier parts of this paper, model structures will play a vital role in this section. The weak equivalences in
G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]
are the levelwise weak equivalences, namely the maps
\scr{X}\longrightarrow\scr{Y}
such that each
\scr{X}(G/H)\longrightarrow\scr{Y}(G/H)
is a weak equivalence of based spaces. Thus, by definition, a map
f
in
G\scr{T}
is a weak equivalence if and only if

\mathbb{R}f
is a weak equivalence in
G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]
. In contrast, as said in
Remark 5.6
,
\mathbb{L}
does not preserve weak equivalences: it takes a weak equivalence of orbital presheaves to a map of
G
-spaces that is a weak equivalence of underlying spaces. Such weak equivalences are sometimes called Borel equivalences since only Borel cohomology, not Bredon cohomology, is invariant with respect to them.

As we shall recall in the next subsection, it is a startling feature of the present context that
\mathbb{L}
and
\mathbb{R}
restrict to inverse isomorphisms between the respective categories of cofibrant objects. We shall heavily exploit that fact. It implies that cofibrant orbital presheaves
\scr{X}
are strictly special: the unit
\eta\colon\scr{X}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\scr{X}
is an isomorphism. In the previous section, the interesting
\Pi
-
G
-spaces were those that are special, so that
\eta
is a weak equivalence, and strictly special objects gave no new information beyond that of the original adjunction of
Section 1.1
. The situation here is entirely different, and the following remark may help illuminate why. Here we return for the moment to
Section 5.1
.

Remark 7.1
.

For comparison of contexts, consider the diagram

\textstyle{\Gamma Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{\Gamma Y}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\Gamma Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\zeta}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{Y}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}Y}

where
Y\in\scr{V}
and
\Gamma
denotes a cellular cofibrant approximation functor, so that
\zeta
is a weak equivalence. The diagram makes sense in both the categories of operators and the orbital presheaves contexts and the functor
\mathbb{R}
preserves weak equivalences in both contexts. In the category of operators context,
\mathbb{L}
also preserves weak equivalences. Thus the diagram just tells us that
Y
is special if and only if
\Gamma Y
is special, which is the interesting case, and here there is no reason to make use of
\Gamma
.

In the orbital presheaves context, the interesting case is when the presheaf
Y
is
not
special, so generally
not
cofibrant, and then

\Gamma
turns any
Y
into the weakly equivalent and strictly special
\Gamma Y
. There is no contradiction since
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\zeta
is generally not a weak equivalence. We regard
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}Y
as wrong and
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{L}\Gamma Y
as right.

Here classical cofibrant approximation plays a new and surprising role. The composite
\mathbb{L}\Gamma
is a substitute for the Elmendorf construction of a
G
-space from an orbital presheaf. That construction and its monadic elaboration were central features of the paper of Costenoble and Waner
[
CW91
]
, which was the first to study the orbital presheaf context. While the Elmendorf construction is not needed with our treatment here, the monads of
[
CW91
]
are fundamental to the applications. Because
\mathbb{L}
does not preserve weak equivalences, the monad
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}
is of no direct use beyond illumination of the theory; it only becomes usable after application of either cofibrant approximation or the Elmendorf construction to transform interesting orbital presheaves to equivalent orbital presheaves of the form
\mathbb{R}X
.

We have a new version of the Elmendorf construction, a new version of the Costenoble–Waner monads, and new applications, including a new operadic characterization of Mackey functors and a construction of the
G
-spectra of units of
E_{\infty}
ring
G
-spectra. Although that theory feeds into the present conceptual theory, it is of sufficient independent interest that we present it in the companion paper
[
KMZ24
]
. In this section, we focus on the novelty of using cofibrant approximation to convert the orbital presheaves of interest, which are not strictly special, to equivalent orbital presheaves that are strictly special and feed into our machine.

7.2.
Model categories and cellular orbital presheaves

We use the standard model structures. The fibrations and weak equivalences in
G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]
are the levelwise Serre fibrations and weak equivalences, and a map
f
in
G\scr{T}
is a fibration or weak equivalence if and only if
\mathbb{R}f
is a fibration or weak equivalence. These model structures are cofibrantly generated, in fact compactly generated in the sense of
[
MP12
, 15.2.1]
. The generating cofibrations
\scr{I}
and generating acyclic cofibrations
\scr{J}
in
G\scr{T}
are obtained by adjoining the identity map of disjoint
G
-fixed basepoints to the standard unbased generating cofibrations and acyclic cofibrations

\textstyle{G/H\times S^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\subset}
\textstyle{G/H\times D^{n}\ \ \text{and}\ \ G/H\times D^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{0}}
\textstyle{G/H\times D^{n}\times I.}

By definition, the generating cofibrations and generating acyclic cofibrations of
G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]
are obtained by applying
\mathbb{R}
to those of
G\scr{T}
. Therefore they are in bijective correspondence via
\mathbb{R}
, with inverse
\mathbb{L}
. Cell
G
-spaces are constructed using disjoint unions, pushouts, and sequential unions. Since passage to fixed points commutes with pushouts one leg of which is a
G
-cofibration (in the classical sense) and clearly commutes with unions,
\mathbb{R}
takes cellular objects to cellular objects, as does
\mathbb{L}
. Of course, as functors,
\mathbb{L}
and
\mathbb{R}
also preserve retractions. Since the cofibrant objects in either of our categories are the retracts of the cellular objects, this and
Lemma 5.15
are all there is to the proof of the following result.

Proposition 7.2
.

The adjunction
(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R})
between
G\scr{T}
and
G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]
is a Quillen equivalence that restricts to an isomorphism of categories between the full subcategories of cofibrant
G
-spaces and cofibrant orbital presheaves. In particular, every cofibrant orbital presheaf is strictly special.

Now return to the context of
Section 4.6
. We choose to focus on spectra for definiteness. Everything also works in the context of
Section 4.3
. Thus we have the adjunction
(\Sigma^{\infty},\Omega^{\infty})
and the monad
\mathbb{C}
associated to an
E_{\infty}
operad that acts on
\Omega^{\infty}E
for all
G
-spectra
E
. We have the adjunction
(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R})
, and we assume that we also have a monad
\mathbb{D}
in
G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]
that satisfies
Section 5.1
. The obvious choice is
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}
, in which case
\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{D}\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}
.

Remark 7.3
.

Other choices will be given in the sequel
[
KMZ24
]
. For any operad
\scr{C}
in
G\scr{T}
, we will there use the operad
\scr{C}_{G}
to construct a monad
\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{pre}}
in
G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]
such that
\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{pre}}\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{pre}}
satisfies the compatibility conditions of
Section 5.1
. Assuming that
\scr{C}
acts on
\Omega^{\infty}E
for
G
-spectra
E
,
\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{pre}}
acts on
\mathbb{R}\Omega^{\infty}E
. As said before, it is the monads
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{C}^{pre}
that lead to applications.

Up to isomorphism, when we restrict to the category
G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]_{ss}
of strictly special orbital presheaves, any choice of
\mathbb{D}
restricts to the canonical choice
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{L}
. With that restriction,
Corollary 5.18
gives that we have the dotted arrow functor
\mathbb{L}
in
(5.3)
and that the following result holds.

Proposition 7.4
.

The adjoint equivalence
(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R})
between
G\scr{T}
and
G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]_{ss}
restricts to an adjoint equivalence between
\mathbb{C}[G\scr{T}]
and
\mathbb{D}\big[G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]_{ss}\big]
.

Note that an equivalence of a model category
\scr{M}
with another category
\scr{N}
induces a model structure on
\scr{N}
such that the equivalence is also a Quillen equivalence. Using that, we can upgrade
Proposition 7.4
to Quillen equivalences that are given by actual equivalences of categories.

Indeed, the categories
\mathbb{C}[G\scr{T}]
and
\mathbb{D}\big[G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]\big]
have standard structures of compactly generated model categories. The fibrations and weak equivalences are created by forgetting to the ground categories
[G\scr{T}]
and
G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]
, and the generating cofibrations and acyclic cofibrations are obtained by application of the left adjoints
\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{D}}
to the generating cofibrations and acyclic cofibrations in the ground categories. This is an easy application of the standard criterion (e.g.
[
MP12
, 16.2.5]
) for a right adjoint to create a compactly generated model structure on its source. Incorporating
Proposition 7.4
, the proof of the following result is precisely analogous to the proof of
Proposition 7.2
.

Proposition 7.5
.

The Quillen equivalence
(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R})
between
G\scr{T}
and
G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]
restricts to a Quillen equivalence between
\mathbb{C}[G\scr{T}]
and
\mathbb{D}\big[G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]_{ss}\big]
, and that restricts to an isomorphism of categories between the full subcategories of cofibrant
\mathbb{C}
-algebras and cofibrant
\mathbb{D}
-algebras. Therefore
(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{R})
restricts to equivalences between
G\scr{T}
and
G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]_{ss}
and between
\mathbb{C}[G\scr{T}]
and
\mathbb{D}\big[G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]_{ss}\big]
.

7.3.
Orbital presheaves in the composite adjunction context

Here our philosophy needs psychological but not mathematical change. Consider the following diagrams.

(7.6)

\textstyle{G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{L}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{D}}}
\textstyle{G\scr{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{G\scr{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega^{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\Omega^{\infty}_{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{(\mathbb{R}\Omega^{\infty})_{\mathbb{D}}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathbb{C}[G\scr{T}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{\infty}_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathbb{D}[G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{L}}
\scriptstyle{(\Sigma^{\infty}\mathbb{L})_{\mathbb{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma}
\textstyle{G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]_{ss}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{L}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{D}}}
\textstyle{G\scr{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{G\scr{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega^{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\Omega^{\infty}_{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{(\mathbb{R}\Omega^{\infty})_{\mathbb{D}}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathbb{C}[G\scr{T}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{\infty}_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathbb{D}[G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]_{ss}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{L}}
\scriptstyle{(\Sigma^{\infty}\mathbb{L})_{\mathbb{D}}}

We think of the top diagram as the input diagram that leads to applications. We apply cellular cofibrant approximation
\Gamma
to feed the top diagram into the bottom diagram without loss of information. The bottom diagram gives homotopical control that allows us to implement our machine; as noted in
Remark 5.5
, we might as well erase its curved arrows. We have noted earlier that the bar construction can often be viewed as a cofibrant approximation, but we have no interest in that interpretation here. Rather, we just note that
Lemma 5.17
and
Remark 5.28
imply that if an orbital presheaf
Y
is strictly special, then so is the orbital presheaf
\overline{Y}
.
Looking back at Sections
5.1
and
5.4
, we see that all of our Assumptions are satisfied in the composite adjunction context displayed in the bottom diagram. Thus Theorems
5.31
and
5.32
specialize to give the following conclusions.

Theorem 7.7
.

There is a functor
\mathrm{Bar}\colon\mathbb{D}[G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]]\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}[G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]]
, written
Y\mapsto\overline{Y}
, and a natural equivalence
\zeta\colon\overline{Y}\longrightarrow Y
. If
Y
is strictly special, then so is
\overline{Y}
and the unit

\eta_{\mathbb{D}}\colon\overline{Y}\longrightarrow(\mathbb{R}\Omega^{\infty})_{\mathbb{D}}(\Sigma^{\infty}\mathbb{L})_{\mathbb{D}}\overline{Y}

is a group completion and is therefore an equivalence if
Y
is grouplike.

Theorem 7.8
.

\big((\Sigma^{\infty}\mathbb{L})_{\mathbb{D}},(\mathbb{R}\Omega^{\infty})_{\mathbb{D}}\big)
induces an adjoint equivalence from the homotopy category of grouplike
\mathbb{D}
-algebras in
G\mathscr{O}^{op}[\scr{T}]_{ss}
to the homotopy category of connective
G
-spectra.

Remark 7.9
.

We comment on how applications proceed. We find examples of
\mathbb{D}
-algebras
Y
. As said before
Y
is not strictly special or even special in the cases of interest. We therefore replace
Y
by
\Gamma Y
and apply
Theorem 7.7
with
Y
replaced by
\Gamma Y
. Because
\Gamma Y
is strictly special, the
\mathbb{D}
-algebra structure on
\Gamma Y
comes from application of
\mathbb{R}
to the induced
\mathbb{C}
-algebra structure on
\mathbb{L}\Gamma Y
. As said before, there is no contradiction since
\mathbb{L}\Gamma Y
is generally not weakly equivalent to
\mathbb{L}Y
. The
G
-spectrum we are after is
\Sigma^{\infty}_{\mathbb{C}}(\overline{\mathbb{L}\Gamma Y})
or, equivalently,
(\Sigma^{\infty}\mathbb{L})_{\mathbb{D}}(\overline{\Gamma Y})
.

Part III

The multiplicative theory

8.
The classical multiplicative theory

8.1.
Monad pairs

Consider monads,
(\mathbb{C},\mu_{\oplus},\eta_{\oplus})
and

(\mathbb{J}_{0},\mu_{\otimes},\eta_{\otimes})
, on a category
\scr{T}
.
As the notation indicates, we think of
\mathbb{C}
as “additive” and
\mathbb{J}_{0}
as “multiplicative with zero”. We think of
\scr{T}
as based and, intuitively, we think of basepoints as zero. The basepoint of
\mathbb{J}_{0}X
for
X\in\scr{T}
is thought of as zero.

Beck
[
Bec69
]
gives a monadic distributivity law encoded by an action of

\mathbb{J}_{0}
on
\mathbb{C}
. We recall the main features, following
[
May09a
, Appendix B]
.
Let
\mathbb{C}[\scr{T}]
and
\mathbb{J}_{0}[\scr{T}]
denote the categories of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras and of

\mathbb{J}_{0}
-algebras in
\scr{T}
.

Definition 8.1
.

An action of
\mathbb{J}_{0}
on
\mathbb{C}
is a structure of monad
on
\mathbb{J}_{0}[\scr{T}]
induced by the monad
\mathbb{C}
on
\scr{T}
. More explicitly, for an action
of
\mathbb{J}_{0}
on
X
, there is a prescribed functorial induced action of
\mathbb{J}_{0}

on
\mathbb{C}X
(and thus on
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{C}X
by iteration) such that

\eta_{\oplus}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}X
and
\mu_{\oplus}\colon\mathbb{C}\mathbb{C}X\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}X

are maps of
\mathbb{J}_{0}
-algebras. We then say that
(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{J}_{0})
is a monad pair.

The proof of the following result is sketched in
[
May09a
, Appendix B]
; full details are in Beck
[
Bec69
]
.

Theorem 8.2
.

The following data relating the monads
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{J}_{0}

are equivalent.

(i)

An action of
\mathbb{J}_{0}
on
\mathbb{C}
.

(ii)

A natural transformation
\mu\colon\mathbb{C}\mathbb{J}_{0}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{J}_{0}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathbb{J}_{0}
with
the following properties.

(a)

(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{J}_{0},\mu,\eta)
is a monad on
\scr{T}
, where

\eta=\eta_{\oplus}\mathbb{J}_{0}\circ\eta_{\otimes}\colon\text{Id}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathbb{J}_{0}
.

(b)

\mathbb{C}\eta_{\otimes}\colon\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathbb{J}_{0}
and
\eta_{\oplus}\colon\mathbb{J}_{0}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathbb{J}_{0}

are maps of monads.

(c)

The following composite is the identity natural transformation.

\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{J}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{C}\eta_{\oplus}\mathbb{J}_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{J}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{C}\eta_{\otimes}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{J}_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{J}_{0}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{J}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{J}_{0}}

(iii)

A natural transformation
\rho\colon\mathbb{J}_{0}\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathbb{J}_{0}
such that
the following diagrams commute.

\textstyle{\mathbb{J}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{J}_{0}\eta_{\oplus}}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{\oplus}\mathbb{J}_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{J}_{0}\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{J}_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{\otimes}\mathbb{C}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{C}\eta_{\otimes}}

\textstyle{\mathbb{J}_{0}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{J}_{0}\mu_{\oplus}}
\scriptstyle{\rho\mathbb{C}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{J}_{0}\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{C}\rho}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{J}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{\oplus}\mathbb{J}_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{J}_{0}\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{J}_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{J}_{0}\mathbb{J}_{0}\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{J}_{0}\rho}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{\otimes}\mathbb{C}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{J}_{0}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{J}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho\mathbb{J}_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{J}_{0}\mathbb{J}_{0}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{C}\mu_{\otimes}}

When given such data, the category
\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{J}_{0}[\scr{T}]]
of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras in

\mathbb{J}_{0}[\scr{T}]
, called
(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{J}_{0})
-algebras in
\scr{T}
, is isomorphic to the category
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{J}_{0}[\scr{T}]
of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{J}_{0}
-algebras in
\scr{T}
.

8.2.
Operad pairs

In retrospect,
30
30

When he defined operad pairs
[
May77
]
, the senior author did not know Beck’s work.
the definition of an operad pair
(\scr{C},\scr{J})
can be summarized as follows. As usual, we assume that
\scr{C}
and
\scr{J}
are reduced, meaning that
\scr{C}(0)
and
\scr{J}(0)
are each a point (a terminal object of
\scr{T}
); we name these objects
0
and
1
, respectively. We assume that
\scr{T}
is the category of based objects of a category
\scr{U}
of unbased objects. The
\scr{C}(j)
and
\scr{J}(j)
are objects of
\scr{U}
. Notationally, we assume that
\scr{T}
is cartesian monoidal for consistency with the literature, but that is not essential to the mathematics. We use the following definition to define the monads
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{J}_{0}
conceptually and explicitly.

Definition 8.3
.

Recall the category
\Lambda
from
Definition 6.2
. It is generated by the permutations of the
\underline{n}
and the ordered injections

\sigma_{i}\colon\underline{n-1}\longrightarrow\underline{n}
that skip
i
. For a contravariant functor
T\colon\Lambda\longrightarrow\scr{U}
and a covariant functor

S\colon\Lambda\longrightarrow\scr{U}
with actions

\tau\colon T(\underline{n})\times\Lambda(\underline{m},\underline{n})\longrightarrow T(\underline{m})\ \ \text{and}\ \ \sigma\colon\Lambda(\underline{m},\underline{n})\times S(\underline{m})\longrightarrow S(\underline{n}),

the categorical tensor product
T\otimes_{\Lambda}S
is the coequalizer

\textstyle{\coprod_{m,n}T(\underline{n})\times\Lambda(\underline{m},\underline{n})\times S(\underline{m})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau\times\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\times\sigma}
\textstyle{\coprod_{j}T(\underline{j})\times S(\underline{j})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T\otimes_{\Lambda}S.}

More explicitly

T\otimes_{\Lambda}S=\coprod_{j}T(j)\times_{\Sigma_{j}}S(j)/(y\sigma_{i},x)\sim(y,\sigma_{i}x)

for all
y\in T(\underline{n})
,
x\in S(\underline{n-1})
and all ordered injections
\sigma_{i}\colon\underline{n-1}\longrightarrow\underline{n}
.

Definition 8.4
.

An operad
\scr{C}
has the underlying contravariant functor
\Lambda\longrightarrow\scr{U}
given by sending
\underline{j}
to
\scr{C}(j)
; the functoriality is given by the right actions of the

\Sigma_{j}
on the objects
\scr{C}(j)
and the maps
\sigma_{i}\colon\scr{C}(n)\longrightarrow\scr{C}(n-1)
for ordered injections
\sigma_{i}
specified by

c\sigma_{i}=\gamma(c;\operatorname{id}^{i-1}\times\,0\times\operatorname{id}^{n-i})

for
c\in\scr{C}(n)
, where
\operatorname{id}\in\scr{C}(1)
is the identity element and
0\in\scr{C}(0)
is the element
0
. For a based object
X\in\scr{T}
, with “basepoint” understood to be a map

\iota\colon\ast=0\longrightarrow X,

define a covariant functor
X^{*}\colon\Lambda\longrightarrow\scr{U}
by
X^{*}(\underline{j})=X^{j}
and note that it takes values in
\scr{T}
; the functoriality is given by the permutation left actions of the
\Sigma_{j}
on the
X^{j}
and the maps

\sigma_{i}=\operatorname{id}^{i-1}\times\iota\times\operatorname{id}^{j-i}\colon X^{n-1}\longrightarrow X^{n}

for ordered injections
\sigma_{i}
. The monad
\mathbb{C}
on
\scr{T}
associated to
\scr{C}
is defined by

\mathbb{C}X=\scr{C}\otimes_{\Lambda}X^{*}.

The coequalizer is defined in
\scr{U}
but takes values in
\scr{T}
, with basepoint given by
\{0\}=\scr{C}(0)\times X^{0}
;

\mu_{\oplus}\colon\mathbb{C}\mathbb{C}X\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}X
is induced by the structure maps

\gamma\colon\scr{C}(k)\times\scr{C}(j_{1})\times\cdots\times\scr{C}(j_{k})\longrightarrow\scr{C}(j),\ \ \text{where}\ \ j=j_{1}+\cdots+j_{k},

and
\eta_{\oplus}
is induced by
\operatorname{id}\colon X\longrightarrow{\operatorname{id}}\times X\in\scr{C}(1)\otimes X
. Less formally,

\mathbb{C}X=\coprod_{j}\scr{C}(j)\times_{\Sigma_{j}}X^{j}/\text{basepoint identifications}.

We modify the definition of
\mathbb{C}
to define the monad
\mathbb{J}_{0}
associated to
\scr{J}
. We change the ground symmetric monoidal structure from cartesian product to smash product. We define an operad
\scr{J}_{0}
, no longer reduced, by adjoining disjoint basepoints
0
to the
\scr{J}(j)
, so that
\scr{J}(j)_{0}=\scr{J}(j)\coprod\{0\}
. In practice, we start with spaces under
S^{0}
, so given maps
e\colon S^{0}\longrightarrow X
and thus basepoints
0
and
1
, but the formal theory sees only the basepoint
0
; that is, the basepoint
1
only comes into play when
X
is a
\scr{J}
-algebra. As explained in a bit more detail in
[
May09b
, Section 4]
, we arrive at the monad in
\scr{T}
given by

\mathbb{J}_{0}X=\bigvee_{j\geq 0}\scr{J}(j)_{0}\wedge_{\Sigma_{j}}X^{(j)},

where
X^{(j)}
is the
j
-fold smash product defined with respect to the basepoint
{0}
. For obvious reasons, the construction is only of interest when
0
and
1
are in distinct path components. The construction is of particular interest when

X=Y_{0}=Y\coprod\{0\}
for an unbased object
Y
, and then

\mathbb{J}_{0}Y_{0}=\bigvee_{j\geq 0}\big(\scr{J}(j)\times_{\Sigma_{j}}Y^{j}\big)_{0}

Definition 8.5
.

An action of
\scr{J}
on
\scr{C}
consists of maps

\lambda\colon\scr{J}(k)\times\scr{C}(j_{1})\times\cdots\times\scr{C}(j_{k})\longrightarrow\scr{C}(j_{\times}),\ \ \text{where}\ \ j_{\times}=j_{1}\cdots j_{k},

which satisfy distributivity, unity, equivariance, and nullity identities relating the maps
\lambda
to the structure maps, permutation group actions, and units of the operads
\scr{C}
and
\scr{J}
. These identities are explicitly specified in
[
May09b
, Definition 4.2]
. They are precisely those needed to ensure that
\mathbb{J}_{0}
acts on
\mathbb{C}
. Thus an operad pair
(\scr{C},\scr{J}_{0})
has an associated monad pair
(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{J}_{0})
.

8.3.
Operad pairs in the category of
G
-spaces

So far, we have been general. We now specialize to
G
-spaces, where we have a canonical operad pair
(\scr{K},\scr{L})
. Here
\scr{K}
, denoted

\scr{K}_{\infty}
in
Section 4.5
, is the Steiner operad of
G
-spaces in the universe
U_{G}
, as described in
[
GM17
, Example 2.2]
. It is the colimit of the Steiner operads
\scr{K}_{V}
associated to the finite dimensional sub
G
-spaces
V
of
U_{G}
. The
\scr{K}_{V}
are described in detail and compared with the little
V
-disk operads
\scr{D}_{V}
in

[
GM17
, Sections 1.1 and 2.3]
, where the advantages of the
\scr{K}_{V}
over the
\scr{D}_{V}
are made clear. The operad
\scr{L}
is the infinite linear isometries operad in the universe
U_{G}
. It was defined in
[
LMS86
]
and is described in
[
GM17
, Example 2.2]
; we will recall its definition in the next subsection. The finite dimensional precursor of the action of
\scr{L}
on
\scr{K}
is described in
[
GM17
, Definition 1.22]
. The action of
\scr{L}
on
\scr{K}
is defined nonequivariantly in
[
May09b
, Section 3]
, and it works the same way equivariantly.

Remark 8.6
.

It is an embarrassment that we know of few other operad pairs, a problem that will be alleviated in
[
May24
]
. Nevertheless, it is sensible to work more generally with an operad pair
(\scr{C},\scr{J})
that maps by a pair of maps of operads to
(\scr{K},\scr{L})
. Examples might be of the form

(\scr{C},\scr{J})=(\scr{K}\times\scr{O},\scr{L}\times\scr{P})
for an operad pair
(\scr{O},\scr{P})
, where
\scr{O}
and
\scr{P}
are also
E_{\infty}
operads. Dropping the requirement that
\scr{O}
and
\scr{P}
are
E_{\infty}
operads, we can also take
\scr{O}
to be the commutativity operad
\scr{N}
and
\scr{P}
to be any operad. This makes sense since
\scr{K}\times\scr{N}\cong\scr{K}
and since any operad
\scr{P}
acts on
\scr{N}
(because each
\scr{N}(j)
is a point).

8.4.
Review of twisted half-smash products and
\scr{L}
-spectra

Historically,
E_{\infty}
ring spaces and
E_{\infty}
ring spectra were first defined in 1972, although a printed definition did not appear until
[
May77
]
. That was well before adequate foundations to do justice to the notions were available. The later introduction of twisted half-smash products (THPs) gave foundations in the context of spectra and
G
-spectra, as described in
Section 4.5
. While THPs are defined more generally and have other uses, they were originally designed to internalize a simple external smash product of
G
-spectra, and that is how we shall use them here. Their definition first appeared in print in
[
LMS86
, Chapter VI]
, They were applied there and throughout
[
BMMS86
]
.

Another decade later, THPs were beautifully redefined conceptually by Cole in the appendix to
[
EKMM97
]
and, equivariantly, in
[
May96
, Chapter XXII]
. In retrospect, EKMM should be called CEKMM because Cole’s appendix, which is written entirely in our present context of
Section 4.5
, reinterpreted the foundations upon which
[
EKMM97
]
was built. Cole never mentioned the word “operad” in either of the cited references. However,
[
LMS86
, Chapter VII]
, which is entitled “Operad ring spectra”, gives exactly the equivariant foundations we need and is quite readable, provided that we take Cole’s later redefinition of the twisted half-smash product as a foundational black box preliminary to that account.

We summarize what we need following and modifying the nonequivariant summary in
[
May09b
]
, which gives more background and compares various alternative monads with isomorphic categories of algebras. Here we use the linear isometries operad
\scr{L}
. Its
j
th space
\scr{L}(j)
is the
(G\times\Sigma_{j})
-space of linear isometries
U_{G}^{j}\longrightarrow U_{G}
, with (left)
G
-action by conjugation and (right)
\Sigma_{j}
-action induced by the (left)
\Sigma_{j}
-action on
U_{G}^{j}
. It is an
E_{\infty}
operad
[
GM17
, Example 2.3]
.

For a
j
-tuple of
G
-spectra
E_{i}
indexed on
U_{G}
, we have an external product
\barwedge_{i}E_{i}
, which is a
G
-spectrum indexed on sums (
=
products)
V_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus V_{j}
in the universe
U_{G}^{j}
. The THP
\scr{L}(j)\ltimes\barwedge_{i}E_{i}
internalizes
31
31

31

Internalization is treated in general categorical terms in work in progress by Bryce Goldman.

this external smash product to what we shall view as the internal smash product of the
E_{i}
; it is a
G
-spectrum indexed on
U_{G}
. This smash product is not symmetric monoidal. It is modified to an equivalent internal smash product in the category of
S_{G}
-modules constructed in
[
EKMM97
]
, but at the price of losing the homotopically meaningful adjunction

(\Sigma^{\infty},\Omega^{\infty})
that we require. As said earlier, the loss is dictated by Lewis’s result
[
Lew91
]
that we cannot have all of the good properties we want in the same category. This internal smash product is suitable for defining actions of the operad
\scr{L}
on a
G
-spectrum
E
.

Definition 8.7
.

An
E_{\infty}
ring
G
-spectrum, or
\scr{L}
-spectrum, is a

G
-spectrum
E
with an
action of
\scr{L}
given by an equivariant, unital, and associative system
of maps

(8.8)

\xi_{j}\colon\scr{L}(j)\ltimes E^{[j]}\longrightarrow E,

where
E^{[j]}
denotes the
j
th external smash power of
E
.

The unspecified diagrams are exactly like those in the original definition of an action of an operad on a space. There are canonical maps

(8.9)

\textstyle{\scr{L}(k)\ltimes(\scr{L}(j_{1})\ltimes E^{[j_{1}]}\barwedge\cdots\barwedge\scr{L}(j_{k})\ltimes E^{[j_{k}]})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{(\scr{L}(k)\times\scr{L}(j_{1})\times\cdots\times\scr{L}(j_{k}))\ltimes E^{[j]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma\ltimes\operatorname{id}}
\textstyle{\scr{L}(j)\ltimes E^{[j]},}

where
j=j_{1}+\cdots+j_{k}
. We use such maps to make sense of the required diagrams.

In practice, we usually first construct an
\scr{L}
-prespectrum
T
and then observe that its spectrification
E=LT
is an
\scr{L}
-spectrum since

L(\scr{L}(j)\ltimes T^{[j]})\cong\scr{L}(j)\ltimes(LT)^{[j]}.

We take
G
-spectra indexed on the zero universe to be based
G
-spaces. Since
\scr{L}(0)
is the inclusion
0\longrightarrow U
, the
functor
\scr{L}(0)\ltimes(-)
from
G
-spaces to
G
-spectra can and must be interpreted as the functor
\Sigma^{\infty}\colon G\scr{T}\longrightarrow G\scr{S}
. Similarly, the zero fold external smash power
E^{[0]}
can and must be interpreted as the
G
-space
S^{0}
. Since
\Sigma^{\infty}S^{0}
is the sphere
G
-spectrum
S_{G}
, the
0^{th}
structure map in (
8.8
) is a map
e\colon S_{G}\longrightarrow E
. We can either think of the map
e

as preassigned, in which case we think of our ground category as the category
G\scr{S}_{e}
of spectra under
S_{G}
, or we can think of
e=\xi_{0}
as part of the structure of an
E_{\infty}
ring spectrum, in which case we think of our ground category as
G\scr{S}
.

We take the latter perspective. In analogy with the
G
-space level monad
\mathbb{L}_{0}
we define a monad
\mathbb{L}_{0}

on the category of
G
-spectra by letting

\mathbb{L}_{0}E=\bigvee_{j\geq 0}\scr{L}(j)\ltimes_{\Sigma_{j}}E^{[j]}.

The
0^{th}
term is
S_{G}
, and
\eta\colon S_{G}\longrightarrow\mathbb{L}_{0}E
is the inclusion.
The product
\mu
is induced by passage to orbits and wedges from the canonical maps
(8.9)
.

A central feature of twisted half-smash products is that there is a natural untwisting isomorphism

(8.10)

\scr{L}(j)\ltimes(\Sigma^{\infty}X_{1}\barwedge\cdots\barwedge\Sigma^{\infty}X_{j})\cong\Sigma^{\infty}(\scr{L}(j)_{0}\wedge X_{1}\wedge\cdots X_{j})

for based
G
-spaces
X_{i}
. Using this, we obtain a monadic natural isomorphism

(8.11)

\mathbb{L}_{0}\Sigma^{\infty}X\cong\Sigma^{\infty}\mathbb{L}_{0}X

relating the space and spectrum level monads
\mathbb{L}_{0}
.

Using the subscript
+
, it has become usual to compress notation by defining

(8.12)

\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}Y=\Sigma^{\infty}(Y_{+})

for an unbased
G
-space
Y
, ignoring its given basepoint if it has one. Then

(8.13)

\mathbb{L}_{0}\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}Y\cong\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\big(\coprod_{j\geq 1}\scr{L}(j)\times_{\Sigma_{j}}Y^{j}\big).

The most interesting examples are of this form.

8.5.
The multiplicative context of
G
-spaces and
G
-spectra

We take
\scr{T}
in our general theory to be the category
\mathbb{J}_{0}[G\scr{T}]
of
\mathbb{J}_{0}
-algebras in
G\scr{T}
(alias
\mathbb{J}_{0}
-
G
-spaces), and we take
\scr{S}
to be the category
\mathbb{J}_{0}[G\scr{S}]
of
\mathbb{J}_{0}
-algebras in
G\scr{S}
(alias
\mathbb{J}_{0}
-
G
-spectra), where
(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{J}_{0})
is a monad pair as in
Section 8.1
. As said before, we have two operadic examples.
For the operad pair example treated here, we take the monad pair associated to a pair
(\scr{C},\scr{J})
of
E_{\infty}
operads of
G
-spaces that maps equivalently to the pair
(\scr{K},\scr{L})
, as in
Remark 8.6
. We are changing the ground categories from
G
-spaces and
G
-spectra to
\mathbb{J}_{0}
-
G
-spaces and
\mathbb{J}_{0}
-
G
-spectra. Here, for the latter, we can pull back
\mathbb{L}_{0}
-
G
-spectra along the projection
\mathbb{J}_{0}\longrightarrow\mathbb{L}_{0}
. We claim that this puts us in the framework of
Section 1.1
, so that we have the following special case of the diagram
(1.12)
.

(8.14)

\textstyle{\mathbb{J}_{0}[G\scr{T}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{J}_{0}[G\scr{S}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega^{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\Omega^{\infty}_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathbb{C}\big[\mathbb{J}_{0}[G\scr{T}]\big]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{\infty}_{\mathbb{C}}}

To prove the claim, we must first prove the following result. To distinguish notationally, let us write
X
for based
G
-spaces,
Z
for
\mathbb{J}_{0}
-
G
-spaces,

E
for
G
-spectra, and
R

\mathbb{J}_{0}
-
G
–spectra, which are
E_{\infty}

G
-spectra with structure given by
\mathbb{J}_{0}
